Impossibility Specification 1 (Architecture Ceiling). Transformers operate within
DLOGTIME-uniform TC0. The boundary condition B1(θ) = TC0 containment
(with θ = architectural depth) specifies that any task requiring NC1 or higher
complexity (general graph connectivity, arbitrary formula evaluation, context-free
language membership) requires external computation. The specification S1: before
deployment, verify that the target task is in TC0; if not, delegate to a symbolic
planner, database engine, or CoT pipeline. Attempting such tasks in a single
forward pass is not merely hard: it is provably impossible, regardless of parameter
count.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. Whether a multi-clause regulatory predicate is
expressible inside the FOC[Attn] hierarchy at a fixed transformer depth can, in principle,
be settled by a Duplicator strategy on the attention game; the game framework is a
methodological contribution, not a direct calculation for this specific task.
2.3
Where Does Reasoning Break? The Delegation Depth
§2.2 characterised what transformers compute in a single forward pass. Modern lan-
guage models reason through chains of thought [27, 28], extending effective computation
beyond TC0. This section establishes a fundamental limit on the depth of such extended
reasoning, a limit that is a capacity phenomenon, not an expressivity impossibility, but
is equally consequential for system design.
2.3.1
The Deterministic Horizon
The reasoning-depth wall d∗is architectural: the upper bound d∗= O(L ϕ(d)) with ϕ(d) ∈

p

across twelve architectures.
Consider an autoregressive transformer T of depth L, width d performing multi-
step reasoning. The model generates a chain c1, . . . , cm of intermediate tokens before
producing a final answer. We define the effective reasoning depth as δ(x) = m · L.
Definition 2.10 (Deterministic Horizon). The Deterministic Horizon d∗of a transformer
architecture is the supremum of effective reasoning depths δ such that the per-step error
probability remains below 1/2 for all steps:

The derivation of d∗depends on three modelling assumptions.
Assumption 2.11 (Conditions for the Deterministic Horizon). (A1) Approximate inde-
pendence. The query-error ∆Q = WQ∆and value-error ∆V = WV∆contribute
approximately independently to the attention output error. Empirical support:

(A2) Negligible higher-order terms. The softmax Jacobian expansion is dominated by
first- and second-order terms.
(A3) Layer-uniform amplification. The per-layer error amplification factor c1 is ap-
proximately constant across layers.
Relaxing (A1) to allow correlation ρ ∈[−1, 1] changes c1 by a factor in [1/2, 1] but
preserves the quadratic growth structure. The super-exponential functional form is
robust to violations.
[@] Intuition Under Assumption 2.11, a per-step error εt does not accumulate
linearly: the softmax Jacobian couples query- and value-error into a quadratic
amplification per layer. The resulting recurrence εt+1 ≈εt + c1ε2
t/B is a discrete
Riccati equation; it stays roughly linear while εt ≪1/c1 and blows up in finite
time once εt crosses a B-dependent threshold. Solving for the crossing time
and translating to effective reasoning depth gives the super-exponential decay
envelope below. The decay is super-exponential, not merely exponential, because
the amplification is quadratic rather than constant; the constant c1 governs how
fast the crossing happens but not the functional form.
Proposition 2.12 (Super-Exponential Accuracy Decay). Under Assumption 2.11, for a
bounded-depth softmax transformer with L layers, d-dimensional embeddings, and O(log n)-bit
precision performing reasoning at effective depth δ > d∗:

Proof sketch. The argument combines an information bottleneck with error amplification.
Information bottleneck. The residual stream carries at most B = d · O(log n) bits.
Tasks requiring m sequential operations with s outcomes need m log s bits; when m >
B/ log s, a base error ε0 > 0 is unavoidable.
Error amplification. The softmax Jacobian ∂α/∂q = (1/√dk) diag(α)(I −1α⊤)K⊤
causes O(∥∆∥/√dk) attention weight change. In CoT, the corrupted query reads cor-
rupted values, yielding (under (A1)) the recurrence εt ≥εt−1 + c1ε2
t−1/B.
Solution. Setting ut = 1/εt yields ut ≈u0 exp(−c1t/(Bu0)), diverging at t∗≈
(B/(c1ε0)) ln(1/(2ε0)). Beyond t∗, the cumulative error gives (2.2).
Solving for the depth at which the per-step error first exceeds 1/2 yields, after
substituting B = d · O(log n) and absorbing layer-uniform constants, a closed-form
scaling law. We state this as the principal theoretical result of this section; the empirical
proportionality constant is deliberately separated into Corollary 2.15 below.

about the functional form of the Deterministic Horizon and is independent of any specific

Remark 2.16 (Why this separation matters). The separation between Theorem 2.13
(asymptotic scaling, model-class property) and Corollary 2.15 (numerical fit, measure-
ment on a specific evaluation set) is deliberate. An empirical constant inside a theorem
statement conflates architectural claim with measurement artefact; it also invites an
implicit universality claim the data does not support. The scaling law is the scientific
claim; the fitted constant is the engineering quantity that operationalises it on today’s
models. Future architectures may shift ˆc without violating Theorem 2.13.

We estimate d∗by evaluating 12 architectures on three task families with control-

Relationship to test-time compute scaling.
The Deterministic Horizon constrains
a single reasoning trace. Test-time compute methods [130] that generate N independent
traces improve success probability proportionally to N for problems within the horizon.
For δ ≫d∗, all traces decay super-exponentially, and test-time scaling provides only
polynomial improvement against exponentially growing failure probability. The hori-
zon identifies a qualitative boundary that test-time compute can push against but not
eliminate.

Returning to the Compliance Assistant. Llama-2 7B’s row in Table 2.1 gives measured

2.3.3
The Fine-Tuning Impossibility
No fine-tuning procedure, at any rank, data scale, or loss, pushes depth-conditional accuracy at
d > d∗beyond an O(d∗/d) envelope under Hypothesis A.1; training-invariance of the envelope
holds unconditionally.
The measurement ˆc = 2.74 of Corollary 2.15 is taken on base checkpoints (models
out of pretraining, evaluated without task-specific adaptation). A natural objection is
that fine-tuning on well-chosen data could push the observed d∗outward: supervise a
model on 5,000 optimal-length chain-of-thought traces at depths d > ˆd∗, and perhaps the
model learns to extend its reliable reasoning window past the scaling law. The empirical
result is that this protocol recovers only 3.2 percentage points of accuracy beyond ˆd∗,
one order of magnitude below the ≥30% recovery that a preference-based account
(“Simplicity Bias” [1]) predicts, and essentially flat out to test-time depth δ = 50. This
subsection proves that the measurement is not a protocol-specific artefact: the accuracy
improvement from any fine-tuning procedure is bounded by a function of d∗and the test-
time depth d that does not depend on the training distribution. In particular, enlarging the


## Page 62


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
39
fine-tuning set, raising the adapter rank, or training to arbitrarily small fine-tuning loss
cannot erase the horizon; the architectural capacity bound of Theorem 2.13 dominates
any training-side improvement in a precise sense we now state.
Before giving the theorem we make explicit three assumptions that the proof
requires. Assumptions 2.17 to 2.19 are the minimal hypotheses under which a training-
invariant upper bound of the form O(d∗/d) can be derived; each corresponds to a
modelling choice the reader should be able to interrogate separately.
Assumption 2.17 (Test-time depth distribution). The test-time task distribution Dtest
places probability at least πd > 0 on instances requiring effective reasoning depth at
least d, for some d > d∗. The accuracy Acc(d) that we bound is the conditional accuracy
given that a test instance falls in the depth-d stratum {δ(x) ≥d}; we make no claim
about accuracy on instances with δ(x) < d∗.
Assumption 2.18 (Capacity budget of fine-tuning). The fine-tuning procedure modifies
the base model θbase to a fine-tuned model θft via any Lipschitz procedure (full fine-
tuning, LoRA at arbitrary rank r ≤d, prefix tuning with any prefix length, RLHF with
any reward model satisfying a bounded Lipschitz constant KR < ∞, any supervised
loss on any training distribution). The procedure is permitted unbounded training data,
unbounded compute, and arbitrarily small empirical training loss. The only constraint
is that θft inherits the same L-layer, d-width, O(log n)-bit-precision transformer architec-
ture as θbase; the architectural parameters (L, d, n) are not changed by fine-tuning. The
Lipschitz requirement excludes pathological reward models with unbounded gradient
(e.g., 0-to-1 cliffs in the reward signal); for such adversarially-sharp reward shaping the
impossibility’s constructive content weakens proportionally to the effective Lipschitz
constant. For all standard RLHF training practice (PPO with bounded KL penalty, DPO,
reward models trained with MSE or cross-entropy on bounded-reward scales), KR is
implicitly bounded and the theorem applies directly.
Assumption 2.19 (Base-model regularity). The base model θbase satisfies the horizon
conditions of Assumption 2.11, with baseline per-step error ε0 ≤εmax
0
< 1/2. In
particular, Proposition 2.12 holds for θbase with the super-exponential decay constant of
Equation (2.2).
The three assumptions map directly onto Assumption 2.11. (1) Test distribution:
we bound accuracy at a specified test depth d, not averaged over a training mixture;
the horizon is a depth-conditional phenomenon. (2) Capacity budget: the bound is a
statement about what any fine-tuning procedure can achieve, so we grant the fine-tuner
unrestricted training resources and only require that the architecture (and hence the
residual-stream capacity) be preserved. (3) Base regularity: the base model must itself
obey the horizon scaling law; otherwise the proof has nothing to compare against.


## Page 63


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
40
[@] Intuition One might hope that careful fine-tuning (on optimal-length traces,
with larger adapters, more data) could push d∗outward. The theorem below
says: no. The residual stream’s capacity B = d · O(log n) is fixed by architecture;
it is not a function of training data. Fine-tuning can rearrange what computation
the residual stream performs, but cannot increase how much. Depth-conditional
accuracy at test depth d > d∗is therefore capped at O(d∗/d) regardless of training
protocol. The 3.2 percentage-point recovery observed for fine-tuned Llama-3.3-8B
on 5,000 optimal-length traces (§2.3.3) sits quantitatively inside this envelope. The
theorem predicts what the experiment measures.

The theorem says: the depth-conditional accuracy improvement Accft(d) −Accbase(d)
from any fine-tuning procedure is O(d∗/d), a quantity that goes to zero as test depth
grows. Because the bound does not depend on how fine-tuning was performed, no pro-
tocol can escape it by training harder, on better data, or with a higher-rank adapter. The

Remark 2.22 (Scope). Theorem 2.20 closes one escape hatch (“just fine-tune harder”) at
fixed architecture. It does not rule out (i) within-horizon gains, which are unrestricted;
(ii) architectural changes enlarging d∗itself per Theorem 2.13; or (iii) tool- or retrieval-
augmented pipelines that bypass the residual-stream bottleneck, precisely the delegation
regime the horizon prescribes. The 3.2% empirical recovery at d = 40 sits well inside
the O(d∗/d) ≈0.68 envelope; the bound tightens to O(0.34) at d = 80 and O(0.17) at
d = 160, predicting vanishing recovery as test depth grows, the key discriminator from
preference-based accounts, which predict bounded but non-vanishing recovery.

2.3.4
Planning Capacity Bounds
Planning capacity on state-transition graphs is upper-bounded by O(L2 log d/(log s + log a))
unconditionally, with a lower bound Ω(L log d/(log s + log a)) under the in-context transition-
table assumption; the factor-L gap between the two directions is open.
Planning is a central AI capability, and the repeated observation that language
models struggle with multi-step planning [131, 30] demands formal explanation. We
formalise planning as computing a path in a state-transition graph: given state space S
(|S| = s), action set A (|A| = a), deterministic transition τ : S × A →S, initial state s0,
and goal sg, produce a valid action sequence of length ℓ.
[@] Intuition Planning is inherently sequential (each step depends on the previous
state), so the total number of planning steps a transformer can execute is bounded
by its sequential-computation budget. Under log-precision, each layer propagates
O(log d) bits sequentially (Merrill & Sabharwal’s parallelism tradeoff [10]); over
m ≤d∗/L CoT steps the total budget is O(L2 log d) bits, and each planning step
consumes log s + log a bits to name the current state and chosen action. Dividing
gives the maximum plan length. The matching lower bound is conditional because
it uses in-context key–value storage of the transition table τ: when τ is present
in the prompt, attention retrieves each successor in O(1) layers; when τ must
be recalled from learned weights, no matching construction is currently known
(Remark 2.24).


## Page 65


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
42
Theorem 2.23 (Planning Capacity (upper bound; conditional lower bound)). Let T be a
depth-L, width-d softmax transformer with O(log n)-bit precision. The maximum plan length
ℓ∗that T can reliably generate (success probability ≥2/3) satisfies ℓ∗= O(L2 log d/(log s +
log a)). Under the in-context key-value assumption of Remark 2.24, a matching-up-to-L lower
bound ℓ∗= Ω(L log d/(log s + log a)) holds, sandwiching ℓ∗between Ω(L log d/(log s +
log a)) and O(L2 log d/(log s + log a)) conditional on that assumption; closing the factor-L
gap between these two directions is open.
Proof sketch. Upper bound. Planning is inherently sequential. By the parallelism trade-
off [10], each layer propagates O(log d) bits sequentially. Over m ≤d∗/L CoT steps, the
total budget is O(L2 log d) bits, yielding ℓ∗≤O(L2 log d/(log s + log a)).
Lower bound (under the in-context assumption). For bounded state spaces,
τ is a lookup table of size s · a, storable as in-context key-value pairs. The query
at step t encodes (st, at); attention retrieves st+1. Each step requires O(1) layers, so
Ω(L log d/(log s + log a)) steps fit within the horizon.
Remark 2.24 (Scope of the lower bound). The lower bound assumes τ is available as in-
context key-value pairs, appropriate for bounded state spaces where τ can be explicitly
prompted. When τ must be recalled from learned weights, the lower bound does not
directly apply. The upper bound remains valid in all settings. Establishing a matching
lower bound for the memorised setting is an open problem.
The theory-practice gap.
We interpret the O(L2 log d) upper bound (with condi-
tional Ω(L log d) lower) as an architectural capacity ceiling: it identifies the correct scaling
regime and the dominant parameters, but effective constants render it a qualitative
rather than quantitative predictor. This interpretive stance is important for what follows.
The theoretical upper bound for a 32-layer, 4096-width transformer on Blocksworld (4
blocks, |S| = 73, |A| = 12) yields ℓ∗
theory = 89 steps; the observed empirical maximum
is 1.4–1.8 steps, a gap of approximately 50×. A five-factor decomposition, namely
(i) training distribution mismatch, (ii) tokenisation overhead, (iii) attention dilution,
(iv) representation misalignment, and (v) finite-sample effects, accounts for roughly half
the gap on a logarithmic scale (reducing the bound from 89 to ∼31 steps). The remaining
∼17× reflects compounding factor interactions that our single-factor ablation methodol-
ogy cannot fully isolate. The bound’s substantive content is that planning capacity scales
at most as O(L2 log d) and at least as Ω(L log d) conditional on the in-context key-value
assumption. The absolute ceiling is gap-affected, but the polynomial dependence on L
and logarithmic dependence on d are not. (This theory-practice gap is one of three that
Chapter 6 analyses as information-carrying diagnostics.)
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. If regulatory reasoning is framed as state-
space planning over clause states with jurisdiction-dependent transitions, the theoretical
O(L2 log d) upper ceiling is far larger than the observed per-model capacity, per the

∼50× theory–practice gap; the engineering implication is that the planning-capacity
bound specifies the scaling regime, not a deployment-ready numerical ceiling.

Let { f1, . . . , fK} be primitive operations acting as permutations on Y = {1, . . . , q}.
The correct output for input (i1, . . . , im) is fim ◦· · · ◦fi1(y0).
[@] Intuition The proof is an adversarial EF-game construction. When the primi-
tive operations { f1, . . . , fK} act transitively on the output alphabet Y, pigeonhole
forces at least half the input distribution into adversarial pairs: two inputs that
share all shallow sub-compositions but disagree on a deep composition. Because
those pairs differ only in frequency components of order O((m −mtrain)/n) (be-
low the 2−p log-precision threshold for large n) the transformer cannot distinguish
them and outputs identically on both, so accuracy on the adversarial half cannot
exceed random. The 3/4 + 1/(2|Y|) bound is what pigeonhole gives when half
the distribution is adversarial (at most 1/2 + 1/|Y| accuracy there) and the other
half is unconstrained (at most 1).
Theorem 2.25 (Compositional-Length Generalisation Bound). Let T be a depth-L softmax
transformer trained on inputs of composition depth ≤mtrain and length ≤ntrain. For inputs of
depth m > mtrain and length n > ntrain:
Acc(T) ≤3

Proof sketch. Adversarial partition. We partition the input space into adversarial inputs
(Aadv), whose equivalence class contains pairs producing different deep outputs, and
the rest (Afree). When the permutation group G = ⟨f1, . . . , fK⟩acts transitively on Y,
pigeonhole ensures Pr[Aadv] ≥1/2.
EF game argument. Adversarial pairs share shallow sub-compositions; frequency
differences in deeper components are O((m −mtrain)/n) < 2−p for large n. Duplicator
wins, so the transformer outputs identically on both elements.
Combining. Accuracy on Aadv is at most 1/2 + 1/|Y|; on Afree, at most 1. Therefore
Acc ≤(1/2)(1/2 + 1/|Y|) + (1/2)(1) = 3/4 + 1/(2|Y|).

2.4
How Reliably Can Extended Reasoning Work? The
Reliability Toolkit
§§2.2–2.3 characterised single-pass and bounded-depth limits. Modern LLMs reason
through chains of thought, and the natural question is: how reliably? This section
develops the complete mathematical theory of chain-of-thought reliability, modelling
reasoning as a Markov chain on states and deriving tight bounds on error propagation,
k-redundant verification, and optimal stopping. The results compose into a practitioner


## Page 68


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
45
toolkit that tells system designers, given per-step error rate ε and verification budget k,
exactly how long a chain can safely be and when it should terminate.
2.4.1
CoT as a Markov Chain
Chain-of-thought reasoning is modelled as a Markov chain on reasoning states with per-step
error ε and spectral gap γ∗; this is a modelling choice whose adequacy is an empirical question
revisited in Section 2.7.
Let X denote the input space (problem statements) and Y the answer space. A
reasoning chain of length n is a sequence s = (s0, s1, . . . , sn) where s0 ∈X is the input,
each si ∈S is an intermediate reasoning state, and sn determines the final answer
ˆy = g(sn) ∈Y through a readout function g.
We model the generation of each reasoning step as a Markov chain. This abstraction
reflects the structure of autoregressive generation: the transformer produces each step
conditioned on the preceding context, and the current reasoning state summarises
the information relevant for future steps. This is a modelling assumption rather than
an exact description; transformers condition on the full context window, introducing
dependencies the first-order Markov model does not capture. (The adequacy of this
approximation is discussed in Section 2.7.)
Definition 2.27 (CoT Markov Chain). A CoT Markov chain is a tuple M = (S, P, s0, S∗, ε)
where S is a finite state space partitioned into correct states S+ and error states S−,
P : S × S →[0, 1] is a transition kernel, s0 is the initial state, S∗⊆S is the absorbing
answer states, and ε = maxs∈S+ P(s, S−) is the worst-case per-step error probability.
Throughout, we assume ε < 1/2 and irreducibility on transient states. Write π for
the stationary distribution of P restricted to S \ S∗and γ∗= 1 −λ2(P) for the spectral
gap. A k-redundant verification scheme produces, at each step i, a total of k + 1 candidate
next-states drawn independently from P(si−1, ·) and advances to the majority-vote
candidate.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. The 12-hop chain becomes a 12-step Markov
model on clause-level reasoning states with per-step error ε = 0.03; the spectral gap γ∗
is estimated from a calibration set of similar regulatory queries and feeds the stopping
rule of Section 2.4.3.
2.4.2
Error Propagation: Tight Bounds in Both Directions
Chain error is sandwiched between matching rates: Pr(error) ≤1 −(1 −ε)n (tight on i.i.d.
steps) and Pr(error) ≥1 −(1 −ε/2)n −1/(n ln |Y|); the per-step exponent gap is O(ε).
[@] Intuition If a chain succeeds only when every one of n steps succeeds, and
each step fails independently with probability at most ε, the success probability


## Page 69


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
46
is at least (1 −ε)n: a simple product, not a sum. The proof is a tower-property
induction on the step-indicator sequence Xt = 1[step t correct]: conditional on
the chain through step t −1, step t lies above the (1 −ε) floor under the Markov
assumption of Definition 2.27, giving a geometric multiplicative lower bound.
The δ/ε safe-length corollary (valid for target error δ ≤1/2) is a first-order Taylor
expansion of the same inequality; tightness with equality is achieved when step
errors are i.i.d., as in single-path chain-of-thought without self-correction.
Theorem 2.28 (Chain Error Propagation). Let M be a CoT Markov chain of length n with
per-step error rate ε ∈(0, 1/2). Then the probability of producing an incorrect final answer
satisfies
Pr( ˆy ̸= y∗) ≤1 −(1 −ε)n.

⌊ln(1 −δ)/ ln(1 −ε)⌋≤δ/ε for δ ≤1/2.

Theorem 2.29 (Fano Lower Bound on Chain Error). For any CoT Markov chain with
per-step error rate at most ε and chain length n, there exists a problem instance such that

Proof sketch. The proof constructs a family of 2n instances with confusion points at each

a factor of ε/(2 −ε); for small ε the gap in rates is O(ε). The matching is tightest in the

tion: a majority-error event requires at least ⌈(k+1)/2⌉of the independent sam-
ples to err simultaneously, so the effective per-step error drops from ε to ε⌈(k+1)/2⌉.
Chaining this across n steps with a union bound gives the n · ε⌈(k+1)/2⌉envelope.
The cost-optimal k∗balances the (k+1)-fold per-step compute cost against the
exponent gain ⌈(k+1)/2⌉; the logarithmic form k∗∼2 ln(n/δ)/ ln(1/ε) −1 re-
flects the standard information-theoretic exchange rate between sample count and
error-tail exponent.

2.4.3
Minimax-Optimal Stopping
An entropy-threshold stopping rule Ht ≤h∗= (λ/γ∗) ln(1/λ) achieves Bayes-optimal
expected loss within O(ε) after a spectral-mixing burn-in; formal near-optimality applies to
∼4% of GSM8K chains, with Proposition 2.32 covering the rest.
The error propagation results say longer chains are riskier, but in practice some
problems require deep reasoning. The challenge is to decide, at each step, whether to
continue or stop. We formalise this as optimal stopping [132].
At each step t, the reasoner observes st and chooses between stopping (producing
g(st)) and continuing. The loss function penalises both errors and computation:
ℓ(τ) = 1[ ˆyτ ̸= y∗] + λ · τ,
(2.10)
where τ is the stopping time, λ ∈(0, 1) is per-step cost, and the Bayes-optimal rule
minimises E[ℓ(τ)].
[@] Intuition Stop when the posterior over the correct answer is confident enough,
but measure confidence by conditional entropy rather than probability, because
entropy gives a multiplicative decay rate tied to the chain’s spectral gap γ∗. After
a burn-in of tmix = O(ln |S|/γ∗) steps, Ht decays geometrically with rate (1 −γ∗)
plus an O(ε) floor from per-step error. The Bayes-optimal rule stops when the
marginal cost of continuing (λ per step) exceeds the marginal drop in expected
error; substituting the entropy-decay rate and solving gives h∗= (λ/γ∗) ln(1/λ).
A chain with large γ∗(confident reasoning) stops quickly; small γ∗(uncertain
reasoning) triggers more steps, adapting automatically. The formal guarantee
holds after tmix; Proposition 2.32 covers the finite-chain regime.
Theorem 2.31 (Near-Optimal Entropy-Threshold Stopping). For a CoT Markov chain with
spectral gap γ∗> 0, per-step cost λ ∈(0, 1), and per-step error rate ε, the entropy-threshold
stopping rule
τ∗= inf{t ≥0 : Ht ≤h∗(λ, γ∗)} ,
h∗(λ, γ∗) = λ

(2.11)
achieves expected loss within an O(ε)-additive gap of the Bayes-optimal rule: E[ℓ(τ∗)] ≤
infτ E[ℓ(τ)] + O(ε). Expected stopping time satisfies E[τ∗] ≤⌈ln(1/λ)/γ∗⌉.
Proof sketch. Three parts. First, Ht satisfies E[Ht+1 | Ft] ≤(1 −γ∗)Ht + ε ln |Y|, an
entropy decay inequality after a burn-in of tmix = O(ln |S|/γ∗) steps. The ε ln |Y| term


## Page 72


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
49
accumulates to at most ε ln |Y|/γ∗by geometric summation, yielding the O(ε) gap.
Second, we construct the Snell envelope and show the value function is monotone in Ht
via Fano applied to the posterior. Third, we optimise over worst-case initial entropy.
The connection to Wald’s Sequential Probability Ratio Test [133] is natural: the
log-likelihood ratio Lt = ln(Pr(correct path | st
0)/ Pr(error on path | st
0)) satisfies
Lt = ln |Y| −Ht + O(πmin), so the entropy-threshold rule is equivalent to SPRT with
boundaries determined by h∗. A chain with large spectral gap (confident reasoning)
stops quickly; a chain with small spectral gap (uncertain reasoning) requires more steps,
and the rule adapts automatically.
Relationship to the heuristic CoT-stopping literature.
A parallel line of empirically-
driven CoT stopping work has emerged since 2024. HALT-CoT [114] applies an answer-
entropy threshold and gives a Wald-style finite-time guarantee under sequential-analysis
assumptions (Assumption B.1–B.2 of that work, namely conditional independence of
tokens given the answer and rational generation maximising mutual information).
ESC [115] stops self-consistency sampling when the predicted answer distribution con-
verges. s1 [116] controls reasoning length via wait-token insertion during decoding.
These works demonstrate that simple entropy- or convergence-based stopping criteria
can reduce CoT token cost by 15–30% at near-baseline accuracy. The theoretical guaran-
tee in Theorem 2.31 is complementary rather than competing: where the heuristic works
supply engineering demonstrations of what an entropy-threshold rule can achieve in
practice, Theorem 2.31 supplies the matching O(ε)-additive Bayes-optimality guaran-
tee under a spectral-gap assumption, together with a closed-form threshold h∗(λ, γ∗)
derived from per-step cost and mixing. The theorem’s contribution is the optimality
statement, not the empirical observation that entropy-based stopping works, which was
documented first by the heuristic literature.
Mixing time caveat.
The entropy decay inequality requires a burn-in of tmix =
O(ln |S|/γ∗) steps. The appropriate |S| is not vocabulary size but the number of distinct
reasoning states, the effective number of semantically distinct intermediate configurations.
On GSM8K, the number of distinct reasoning patterns (measured by clustering step-
level hidden representations, cosine threshold 0.95) is approximately |S|eff ≈120 ± 35
for Llama-3.1-8B, yielding tmix ≈55 steps. This exceeds typical GSM8K chain lengths
(average 12.4), so the formal near-optimality of Theorem 2.31 applies to only ∼4% of
GSM8K chains, ∼2% of StrategyQA chains, and <1% of MATH chains. The following
finite-chain guarantee handles the rest.
Proposition 2.32 (Finite-Chain Stopping Guarantee). For any CoT Markov chain with
spectral gap γ∗> 0 and any stopping time t < tmix, the entropy-threshold stopping rule
achieves E[ℓ(τ∗)] ≤infτ E[ℓ(τ)] + O(ε) + λ · tmix. When λ ≤εγ∗/ ln |S|eff, this additional
cost is O(ε).

To quantify practical effectiveness, we measure the fraction of the oracle’s Bayes

λ = 0.025. Our method captures 78% of the oracle’s risk reduction on GSM8K (8B), 86%
on StrategyQA, averaging 81% across benchmarks. The stopping rule is sensitive to
spectral gap misestimation only moderately: a 20% overestimate of γ∗changes h∗by
≈17%, shifting accuracy by < 0.4 pp on GSM8K.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant at λ = 0.025 and calibrated ˆγ ≈0.3: the
threshold is h∗≈0.31 nats, stopping the chain once smoothed token entropy falls below
h∗; the 81% Bayes-risk-reduction figure is a benchmark average, not a claim about the
specific regulatory distribution.

An online implementation approximates the conditional entropy by EMA-smoothed token-level

during generation. We approximate it using the token-level entropy of the model’s

2.5
When Does Supervision Help? The Training Invest-
ment Rule
The reliability toolkit of §2.4 assumed a fixed generator and verifier. A complementary
question concerns training: given a fixed budget of examples, how much does access to
intermediate reasoning states (process supervision) help compared to observing only
final answers (outcome supervision)? Lightman et al. [34] demonstrated empirical
gains, but a complete theoretical characterisation of when process supervision helps has
remained open. This section closes it.
2.5.1
Setup
Under process supervision the learner observes step-level correctness labels; under outcome
supervision only the final answer; in both settings the learner outputs a chain-to-bit verifier
v : Sn →{0, 1}.
The learner receives T training examples, each consisting of a problem x, a rea-
soning chain (s0, . . . , sn), and either: (a) under outcome supervision, only the label
y∗= g∗(x); or (b) under process supervision, step-level labels (c1, . . . , cn) where ci = 1
if step i is correct. The learner’s goal is to produce a verifier v : Sn →{0, 1} that correctly
identifies whether a given chain reaches the right answer. Let err(v, T) denote expected
verification error of the best verifier given T examples.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. Process supervision requires each of the 12
intermediate legal-reasoning steps to be labelled correct/incorrect (by a human expert
or a PRM); outcome supervision requires only the final regulatory verdict.

2.5.2
The Θ(n/ log n) Separation
Under chain non-redundancy (Definition 2.35), the verifier-learning sample-complexity ratio is
Tout/Tproc = Θ(n/ ln n) = n/(ln n + O(ln ln n)); at n = 20 this predicts ∼6.7×, observed
ratio on MATH is 4.8 ± 0.3 at η = 0.15 after label-noise adjustment.
[@] Intuition Under chain non-redundancy (Definition 2.35), process supervision
is an n-fold VC problem: each of the n steps is a binary classification with VC
dimension dCoT, and union-bounding across steps gives the dCoT n ln(T/n)/T
upper bound. Outcome supervision must identify which step went wrong from
the final answer alone, posing a search problem over n hidden locations. Le Cam’s
method applied to a family of generators that agree on final-answer distributions
but differ at a single hidden step shows that distinguishing any pair requires
Ω(n/ ln n) samples: binary-search-style information leakage through the answer
reveals at most ln n bits, reducing the effective search space from n to n/ ln n. Di-
viding the outcome lower bound by the process upper bound yields the Θ(n/ ln n)
sample-complexity ratio.
Theorem 2.33 (Process vs. Outcome Supervision Separation). Let G be the class of CoT
generators with chain length n and state space S. Then:
(a) Upper bound. Under process supervision, the optimal verifier achieves
err(Vproc, T) ≤dCoT · n · ln(T/n)

(b) Lower bound. Under outcome supervision alone, for any verifier,
err(Vout, T) ≥dCoT · n
T/ ln T .

(c) Separation. The ratio of sample complexities to achieve verification error η satisfies

Proof sketch. (a) Under process supervision, each step’s correctness is a binary classifi-
cation with VC dimension dCoT. Applying VC uniform convergence at each step and
union-bounding over n steps gives the upper bound.
(b) The technically involved direction. We construct a family of n generators
g1, . . . , gn indistinguishable from final-answer observations but differing at a single
hidden step: generator gj has elevated per-step error rate 2ε at step j and compensating
reduced error rate ε(1 −ε/(n −1)) elsewhere, ensuring overall chain success probability


## Page 76


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
53
∏i(1 −εi) remains identical across j. An outcome-supervised verifier must identify the
hidden step via patterns across multiple problems, a search problem over n locations
solvable by implicit binary search with O(1) samples per level, leaking ln n bits and
reducing the effective search space from n to n/ ln n. Formalisation via Le Cam’s method
yields Ω(n/ ln n) samples to distinguish any pair (gj, gj′).
(c) Combines (a) and (b).
Remark 2.34 (Relationship to Jia–Rakhlin–Xie 2025). Jia, Rakhlin, and Xie [117] recently
established that in the offline reinforcement learning setting, outcome-supervised and
process-supervised learning are statistically equivalent up to polynomial factors in the
planning horizon H, under bounded state-action concentrability Csa(Π, πoff). Their
setting differs from ours along two axes. First, their learner consumes a dataset of trajec-
tories with either cumulative or per-step rewards and outputs a policy; ours consumes
labelled examples of chain-and-final-answer pairs and outputs a verifier v : Sn →{0, 1}.
The sample-complexity object is trajectory count under concentrability-weighted cover-
age in theirs, labelled-example count under chain non-redundancy in ours. Second, their
result is a reduction (up to polynomial-in-H factors); ours is a separation (Θ(n/ log n)).
These are fully compatible: n/ log n is polynomial in n, and a polynomial-horizon
reduction does not rule out polynomial-horizon separations. It leaves their size as a
free parameter that Theorem 2.33 pins down. The if-and-only-if characterisation of
Theorem 2.36 identifies the structural hypothesis that drives the separation: chain non-
redundancy. Outside this regime, when all generators in the class produce identical
intermediate distributions, our separation collapses to Θ(1) and the two paradigms are
statistically equivalent, as one would expect. Empirically, the 6.7× sample-efficiency
ratio measured below for 20-step chains sits within the window compatible with both
our separation and the Jia–Rakhlin–Xie polynomial-horizon reduction, and the thesis’s
design rule S4 accordingly prescribes process supervision when chain non-redundancy
can be verified (Definition 2.35) and outcome supervision as equivalently efficient when
it cannot.
For a 20-step chain, process supervision requires ≈6.7× fewer examples; for 100
steps, ≈21.7×. The leading constant 1/(ln n + O(ln ln n)) predicts ≈5.4× for the
average MATH chain length (14.3), which we validate empirically below.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant with n = 12. The predicted supervision
ratio is ≈12/ ln 12 ≈4.8× (or ∼5.4× under the leading-constant correction); process
supervision is warranted when chain non-redundancy of the deployed regulatory model
can be verified per Definition 2.35.

2.5.3
Chain Non-Redundancy: When Does the Gap Exist?
The Θ(n/ log n) supervision gap exists if and only if the generator class satisfies chain non-
redundancy: two generators produce identical final-answer distributions yet differ in intermediate-
step distributions on a positive-measure set of predecessors.
The separation depends on a structural property of the generator class.
Definition 2.35 (Chain Non-Redundancy). A generator class G satisfies the chain non-
redundancy condition if there exist g1, g2 ∈G producing identical final-answer distribu-
tions (g1(x) d= g2(x) for all x) but differing in intermediate reasoning: there exists a step
i such that TV(g1(· | si−1), g2(· | si−1)) > 0 on a set of predecessor states with positive
measure.
Chain non-redundancy says different generators can reason differently while reach-
ing the same answers. This holds for almost all practical classes: different transformer
architectures and training procedures produce the same answer distribution via different
internal computations. The condition fails only for trivial classes where intermediate
steps are deterministic functions of input and output.
Theorem 2.36 (If and Only If Characterisation). The Θ(n/ ln n) sample complexity sep-
aration holds for generator class G if and only if G satisfies chain non-redundancy. When
non-redundancy fails, outcome and process supervision have identical sample complexity up to
constant factors.
Proof sketch. The “if” direction uses the hidden-step construction. The “only if” direction
shows that without non-redundancy, every distinguishing feature visible in intermediate
steps is visible in aggregated form in the final answer: if all generators produce identical
intermediate distributions, the Fisher information about the verification target carried
by (c1, . . . , cn) equals that carried by y∗alone, up to constants.
Definition 2.37 (CoT-Discriminative Dimension). The CoT-discriminative dimension of a
generator class G is the VC dimension of Hdisc = {hε : s 7→1[Pg(s, S−) > ε] : g ∈G, ε ∈
[0, 1]}.
For transformers with L layers, hidden dimension d, and H heads: dCoT ≤O(L2Hd ln(Ld)).
For recurrent architectures: dCoT = Θ(d). For Llama-3.1-8B (L = 32, H = 32, d = 4096),
the transformer bound yields dCoT = O(109), making the sample complexity bounds in
Theorem 2.33(a) vacuous at practical training set sizes. We view this as reflecting loose-
ness of the VC bound rather than a deficiency of the separation. The n/ ln n functional
form is the key practitioner takeaway: it describes how much faster process supervision re-
duces verification error, independent of absolute sample complexity, and the functional
form is empirically confirmed (see experiments below).
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. Different regulatory-finetuned models can
reach the same final verdict via measurably different intermediate-step distributions


## Page 78


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
55
(different citation orders, different intermediate predicates), so chain non-redundancy is
plausibly satisfied and the Θ(n/ ln n) separation applies; verification of non-redundancy
for a specific deployment is the empirical check prescribed by S4.
2.5.4
Connections to Reinforcement Learning and Internal Reasoning
The Θ(n/ log n) separation transfers directly to RL agents with per-step rewards and to models
with internal chain-of-thought whose tokens are available for training; for genuinely hidden
internal reasoning it collapses to outcome supervision.
When an RL agent receives dense per-step reward signals (e.g., process reward
models [34]), the learning signal per step is functionally equivalent to step-level correct-
ness labels. The Θ(n/ ln n) separation transfers directly: an RL agent with step-level
rewards requires Θ(n/ ln n) fewer episodes than one receiving only terminal rewards.
For models with internal CoT (o1, DeepSeek-R1 [134]): if internal tokens are avail-
able for training (distillation, logging), the separation holds with n equal to the number
of internal steps. If truly hidden, the setting reduces to outcome supervision.
Robustness to label noise.
When step-level labels have symmetric noise rate η, the
process-supervised learner’s per-step signal is reduced by (1 −2η)2. The ratio Tout/Tproc
becomes Θ(n/ ln n) · (1 −2η)2, preserving the separation for η < 1/2. For the 5–8%
noise in PRM800K, the multiplicative degradation is (1 −2 × 0.065)2 ≈0.76, reducing
the theoretical ratio from 5.4 to ≈4.1 for the average MATH chain. The observed ratio
of 4.8 ± 0.3 lies between noise-free and noisy predictions.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. If the regulatory reasoner is trained via
reinforcement learning with a process reward model (following Lightman et al. [34]),
the Θ(n/ ln n) episode-count saving transfers directly from the verifier-learning setting
to the RL setting; if the reasoner uses genuinely hidden internal chain-of-thought whose
tokens are unavailable for training, the setting reduces to outcome supervision and the
separation collapses.
2.5.5
Universal Test-Time Compute Scaling
Test-time success follows Pr(success) = 1 −exp(−c Cα) with α = logbeff(beff −1) under
inference strategy of effective branching factor beff > 1; the theoretical ordering best-of-N+PRM
> beam > MCTS > self-consistency is matched empirically on GSM8K.
Different inference strategies explore reasoning paths with different efficiencies.
Definition 2.38 (Effective Branching Factor). For an inference strategy allocating total
compute C (measured in reasoning steps across all chains), let Ncorrect(d) denote the
expected number of distinct correct-answer paths reachable at exploration depth d. The
effective branching factor is beff = limd→∞[Ncorrect(d)]1/d when this limit exists.


## Page 79


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
56
This unifies the four strategies we analyse: best-of-N with perfect verifier has beff →
∞; best-of-N with imperfect verifier (error εv) has beff = 1/(1 −εv); beam search (width
b) has beff = b −1; single chain with per-step verification has beff = Istep/(Istep + ε).
[@] Intuition A tree-search inference strategy with effective branching factor
beff explores ∼b d
eff distinct paths at exploration depth d and succeeds when any
one of them is correct. A large-deviations tail over the independent per-path
success events gives Pr(success) ∼1 −(1 −p)b d
eff for per-path success probability
p; reparameterising compute C as the number of paths explored and matching
the tail gives the scaling exponent α = logbeff(beff −1). The special cases are all
instances of the same scheme: best-of-N is beff = ∞(independent samples, α = 1
with a perfect verifier); beam search of width b is beff = b −1; single-chain per-step
verification is a fractional beff tied to verifier signal-to-noise (Istep/(Istep + ε)). The
information-theoretic lower bound matches this rate up to constants.
Theorem 2.39 (Scaling Law for Reasoning). Under any inference strategy with effective
branching factor beff, the success probability satisfies
Pr
C (success) = 1 −exp(−c · Cα) + O(C−(1+α)),
(2.15)
where α = logbeff(beff −1) for beff > 1 and c depends on per-chain success rate and verifier
accuracy.
The rigorously derived special cases are: best-of-N (perfect verifier) α = 1; best-
of-N (imperfect, error εv) α = 1 −εv + O(ε2
v); beam search (width b) α = logb(b −1);
single chain with per-step verification α = 1/(1 + ε/Istep). A standard rate-distortion
argument on the per-step mutual information Istep suggests an asymptotic upper enve-
lope of the form α ≲log(1/ε)/Istep on the scaling exponent achievable by any strategy
whose verifier signal is bounded above by Istep bits per step; formalising this informal
bound and identifying its precise hypotheses is left to future work.
Conjecture 2.40 (MCTS Scaling Exponent). Under Monte Carlo Tree Search with UCT
exploration on a binary decision tree, beff = 2 and α = ln 2 ≈0.693.
Empirically, the fitted exponent on GSM8K is ˆα = 0.68 ± 0.04, consistent with the
conjecture.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. Pairing best-of-N sampling with a regulatory-
domain process reward model gives a scaling exponent near α ≈1 −εv + O(ε2
v) (e.g.,
≈0.94 at εv = 0.06), versus α = ln 2 ≈0.693 for binary-tree MCTS (Conjecture 2.40);
the practitioner implication is that verifier-quality investment dominates tree-search

2.5.6
Optimal Compute Allocation
In the verifier-error-dominated regime, process supervision shifts the budget optimum toward
inference: the training fraction is Θ(n ln n/B) under process versus Θ(n2/(ln n · B)) under
outcome supervision, a factor of Θ(n/(ln n)2).
The supervision separation and the scaling laws combine to address a practical
question that neither alone answers: given a fixed total budget, how should one split
resources between training a process reward model and running inference chains?

2.5.7
Empirical Validation
On GSM8K, MATH, and StrategyQA with Llama-3.1-{8B, 70B}: error propagation holds within
5% relative for nε < 1; entropy-threshold stopping saves 22–38% of chain length; the supervision
ratio is 4.8 ± 0.3 (theoretical 4.1 after label-noise correction).
We validate predictions through controlled synthetic experiments and evaluations
on standard reasoning benchmarks. Experiments run on 4 NVIDIA A100 (80GB) GPUs;
we report means and standard deviations across 5 random seeds.

controlled per-step error rates: n binary classification steps, correct label with probability
1 −ε. Varying n ∈{2, 5, 10, 15, 20, 30, 50} and ε ∈{0.01, 0.03, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15}, running

Impossibility Specification 4 (Training Investment Rule). The Θ(n/ log n) separa-
tion specifies when to invest in process supervision: the advantage exists if and only
if the generator class satisfies chain non-redundancy (Definition 2.35). The specifi-
cation S4: (i) test chain non-redundancy on held-out data by checking whether two
fine-tuned models with different seeds have measurably different intermediate
distributions despite matched final-answer accuracy; (ii) if yes, invest in process
reward model training (expected n/ ln n× data efficiency gain); (iii) if no, invest
equivalently in outcome supervision. The scaling law P(success) = 1 −e−c·Cα with
α determined by beff further specifies the optimal inference strategy.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant. The MATH benchmark’s observed supervision
ratio of 4.8 ± 0.3 at chain length 14.3 is the closest empirical surrogate for the regulatory
setting’s predicted 4.8 at n = 12; error propagation holding within 5% relative for nε < 1
(satisfied here at 0.03 × 12 = 0.36) supports the bound’s use on this deployment.

The four impossibility specifications compose into a single decision tree for system de-
signers. Given architectural parameters (L, d) and task parameters (required reasoning

1. Architecture ceiling: Regulatory reasoning is in TC0 for each hop; delegation not

scaling exponent α ≈0.94.
The four specifications together convert a vague engineering question (“will this system
work?”) into deterministic numerical rules.
2.7
Discussion and Limitations
Precision regime.
The tight characterisation of Theorem 2.4 assumes O(log n)-bit
precision, which does not directly capture the constant-precision arithmetic of practical
systems. The Deterministic Horizon and planning bounds are qualitatively robust to the
precision regime, but the FOC[Attn] characterisation specifically requires logarithmic
precision. Remark 2.6 discusses this trade-off.
Semi-empirical constant.
The proportionality constant c ≈2.74 in the Determin-
istic Horizon is fitted from data under the empirical form d∗≈ˆc log L
p
log d. The
banded asymptotic upper bound d∗= O(L · ϕ(d)) with ϕ(d) ∈[
p
log d, log d] follows
from Assumption 2.11 (lower edge of the band conditional on Hypothesis A.1); spe-
cific numerical predictions depend on the empirical constant and the empirical log L
dependence, which is milder than either edge of the band. Cross-validation across
12 architectures suggests the constant is stable (refitted range [2.58, 2.91]), but new
architectures could require recalibration.
Independence assumption.
Proposition 2.12 relies on Assumption 2.11(A1). While
the functional form is robust to violations (only the constant changes), the assumption is
justified empirically rather than proved. A formal derivation would require bounding
higher-order error correlations in the residual stream.


## Page 83


Chapter 2. The Deterministic Horizon
60
Planning theory-practice gap.
The O(L2 log d) planning upper bound (with con-
ditional Ω(L log d) lower) has a ∼50× gap between theory and practice for the upper-
bound ceiling. The five-factor decomposition accounts for roughly half the gap on a log
scale; the remainder reflects factor interactions we cannot currently quantify. (Chapter 6
revisits this gap as one of three diagnostic gaps that carry information about fruitful
research directions.)
Markov approximation.
The CoT Markov chain abstraction (Definition 2.27) is a
modelling assumption rather than an exact description of transformer computation.
Transformers condition on the full context window, introducing dependencies the first-
order Markov model does not capture. Empirically, the adequacy of this approximation
holds within 5% relative error on the synthetic benchmarks; on naturalistic tasks, the
match is qualitative (correct functional form) but constants can differ by factors of 2–3.
Mixing time caveat for stopping.
The formal near-optimality guarantee of Theo-
rem 2.31 applies to only ∼4% of GSM8K chains under the effective state-space interpre-
tation. For the remainder, Proposition 2.32 provides a weaker guarantee; the practical
performance (81% of oracle’s risk reduction) is strong empirical evidence that the rule
works beyond its formal regime, but the formal gap remains.
CoT-discriminative dimension looseness.
For frontier transformers, the worst-
case dCoT bound yields vacuous sample complexity bounds. This reflects VC dimension’s
known pessimism, not a deficiency of the separation. The functional form Tout/Tproc =
Θ(n/ log n) is empirically validated; developing tighter instance-dependent bounds
remains open.
Synthetic evaluation.
Empirical validation uses synthetic tasks with controllable
complexity. A preliminary mapping of 200 GSM8K problems to our framework (Spear-
man ρ = −0.74, p < 0.001, between estimated composition depth and Llama-2 7B
accuracy) suggests the framework captures meaningful difficulty variation in naturalis-
tic settings, but this is illustrative rather than definitive. Systematic validation across
diverse benchmarks is needed.
Specifications and Open Problems
This chapter established the base-model computational ceiling. The expressivity logic

methodological benefit, placing softmax transformers precisely below TC0 but strictly

[?] Open Problem
Open Problem 2.1 (Beyond bounded-depth log-precision). The horizon the-
orem assumes bounded depth and log-precision arithmetic. What is the corre-
sponding scaling law for (i) unbounded-depth recurrent variants (state-space
models, linear RNNs), (ii) mixture-of-experts architectures where effective capac-
ity is data-dependent, (iii) full-precision transformers on short contexts where
log-precision is not the binding constraint? Empirically these architectures also
exhibit a horizon, but its functional form is unknown.
[?] Open Problem
Open Problem 2.2 (Instance-dependent dCoT). The Θ(n/ log n) supervision
separation depends on the CoT-discriminative dimension dCoT, whose worst-
case bound is vacuous for frontier transformers. Develop instance-dependent
bounds that are practically informative for the frontier regime; preliminary
evidence (§2.7.4) suggests dCoT is controlled by chain-non-redundancy structure
but no tight bound is known.

Bridge to Chapter 3.
This chapter established that the base transformer model has
hard computational limits, and that each limit tells practitioners exactly what to do. But
the base model is never deployed naked. In practice, foundation models are adapted:
fine-tuned on downstream tasks, aligned via preference learning, edited to correct errors.
The natural question is whether adaptation can overcome the Deterministic Horizon
or circumvent the reliability bounds. Chapter 3 proves it cannot. Adaptation has its
own cliffs, each following the same impossibility-specification pattern: a sharp phase
transition in preference learning, the inevitability of model collapse under synthetic
data, the locality-generalisation impossibility for knowledge editing. The base-model
wall of this chapter thus stands; the adaptation cliffs of the next chapter are additional
barriers, not workarounds.

Chapter 3
The Adaptation Cliff
The Compliance Assistant now faces four adaptation decisions, one per cliff. The institu-
tion fine-tunes its Llama-2 7B base model on 2,000 annotated regulatory documents with
LoRA rank r = 16; the PAC-Bayes bound of §3.1 returns eO(
p
mr(d + k)/N) ≈0.18,
certifying generalisation (Decision Rule A1). Regulatory interpretation admits multiple
valid readings, so annotator agreement κ ≈0.65 translates to Bradley-Terry misspecifica-
tion γ ≈0.08, firmly in the quadratic regime, requiring ≈15,000 preference pairs rather
than the ∼800 a well-specified budget would suggest (Decision Rule A2). Synthetic-data
augmentation for edge-case coverage is flagged dangerous under pure replacement; the
1%-real-data floor of §3.3 applies (Decision Rule A3). When 8 factual errors surface post-
deployment, the edit-capacity formula of §3.4 warns that the 9th–13th edits approach
the K∗≈13 ceiling, and the 14th requires re-fine-tuning rather than another point edit
(Decision Rule A4). This chapter gives the theorems these rules are corollaries of.
Chapter 2 established the base model’s limits. In practice, foundation models are
never deployed as-is: they are fine-tuned for downstream tasks, edited to correct factual
errors, aligned via preference learning, and composed across domains. The natural
practitioner response to the Deterministic Horizon is to ask: can adaptation overcome these
limits? This chapter proves it cannot, at least not without encountering new cliffs. Every
adaptation operation hits a hard threshold, and each threshold encodes an impossibility
specification.
We establish four:
§3.2 proves the first non-vacuous PAC-Bayes bounds scaling in the adapter parameter
count for parameter-efficient adaptation at 7B–70B scale, complementary to Lotfi et
al.’s [44] and Hu et al.’s [45] adapted-model-as-a-whole bounds and identifying a rank-
32 deployment ceiling at Alpaca scale, establishing the safety certificate that adaptation
generalises rather than memorises (Impossibility Specification 5).
§3.3 proves a sharp phase transition in preference learning: sample complexity jumps
discontinuously from Θ(n log n/∆2) to eΘ(n2/γ2) (where eΘ absorbs a log n factor from
the matching upper bound) under any misspecification of the Bradley-Terry model,
specifying the misspecification tolerance (Impossibility Specification 6).


## Page 91


Chapter 3. The Adaptation Cliff
68
§3.4 proves that pure synthetic-data replacement causes inevitable model collapse:
E[TV(pT, p∗)] ≥1 −exp(−T2deff/(128πnmin)); the accumulation bound then shows
ρ ≥0.01 real-data retention suffices for an n0-independent ceiling, specifying the real-
data requirement (Impossibility Specification 7).
§3.5 proves a locality-generalisation impossibility for knowledge editing under super-
position, with an edit capacity K∗≈13 beyond which retraining is required, specifying
the editing budget (Impossibility Specification 8).
§3.6 demonstrates the impossibility-specification methodology in action: the pref-
erence phase transition specifies that gradient-based alignment collapses under misspeci-
fication; EvoPref is the constructive response, a multi-objective evolutionary algorithm
maintaining a population of LoRA adapters that reduces preference collapse by 47 per-
centage points. This is not a systematic treatment of evolutionary computation. It is a
single worked example showing that the impossibility, once properly framed, prescribes
its own solution.
Notation for this chapter.
n denotes the number of items (preference learning) or
features (superposition); γ denotes the Bradley-Terry misspecification level; ∆denotes
the minimum preference gap; d denotes the representation dimension; r denotes LoRA
rank; N denotes training sample size; K denotes the number of edits; T denotes synthetic
data generation count; ρ denotes the fraction of real data retained. Where conflicts with
Chapter 2’s notation arise, we use subscripts (εpref, εmech, etc.) as introduced.
3.1
Relationship to Prior Work
This chapter’s four specifications span generalisation theory, preference learning, synthetic-
data scaling, and knowledge editing. We locate each contribution relative to its direct
predecessors.
Generalisation theory for adapted LLMs.
Classical PAC-Bayes bounds [135, 136]
underwent a decade of tightening: Dziugaite and Roy [42] achieved the first non-
vacuous bounds for small neural networks; Zhou et al. [43] extended to larger scales.
The breakthrough at LLM scale came from Lotfi et al. [44] (and the related compression-
prior work of Hu et al. [45]), who obtained non-vacuous bounds for models up to 70B
parameters using compression and structural priors. Our LoRA PAC-Bayes bound
(Theorem 3.1) is complementary: Lotfi et al. bound generalisation of the adapted model
as a whole; we bound adaptation-induced generalisation specifically, scaling with
eO(
p
mr(d + k)/N) in the adapter parameter count rather than the full model size p.
The rank-32 ceiling we derive is a new practitioner-facing finding not present in prior
work. Related parameter-efficient methods (LoRA itself [39], QLoRA [40], AdaLoRA [41])
focus on training dynamics and empirical performance rather than formal generalisation.


## Page 92


Chapter 3. The Adaptation Cliff
69
Aghajanyan et al. [137] established low intrinsic dimensionality of fine-tuning; our PAC-
Bayes bound operationalises their observation as a quantitative rank ceiling. Biderman
et al. [46] and Malladi et al. [47] analysed LoRA training dynamics; our bounds are
consistent with but orthogonal to these. Stap et al. [138] documented the fine-tuning
paradox (larger models more fragile under adaptation); our bound formalises this as the
r(d + k)/N term growing faster than sample size for large d. Concurrent with this work,
Kratsios et al. [139] prove sharp generalisation bounds for asymmetric randomised
low-rank adapters, with a single-run concentration rate eO(√
r/N) and a matching
O(1/
√
N) lower bound; where Theorem 3.1 and the bounds of Lotfi et al. are average-
case statements, theirs concentrates the generalisation gap for one fine-tuning run, so
the two readings are complementary rather than competing.
Preference learning and alignment.
The RLHF-to-DPO arc [140, 48, 49, 141] es-
tablished alignment-from-preferences as standard practice; Bai et al. [142] proposed
self-critique. Theoretical analysis emerged in parallel: Azar et al. [143] generalised to
ΨPO and IPO; Tang et al. [144] extended to arbitrary preference models; Ethayarajh et
al.’s KTO [145] reformulated alignment as prospect-theoretic optimisation to sidestep the
Bradley-Terry assumption; Chowdhury et al.’s Robust DPO [146] introduced provable
robustness to noisy preference feedback under an explicit noise model; Song et al. [147]
proved coverage necessity, and Xiao et al. [51] established preference collapse as an
algorithmic bias of RLHF Our phase transition result (Theorem 3.4) sharpens these in
a specific way: existing works characterise suboptimality rates under misspecification,
whereas our result establishes a discontinuous transition at γ∗= Θ(∆/n). IPO, KTO,
and Robust DPO each address noise and misspecification, but none characterises the
discontinuity itself or the population-based response (EvoPref) as the constructive dual
above the transition. The DPO-versus-RLHF gap (Theorem 3.7) complements Xu et
al. [50], who documented empirical DPO degradation under noise; we explain the
degradation as first-order bias in the Bradley-Terry reparameterisation. Xiong et al. [148]
analysed iterative-RLHF dynamics consistent with our compounding-misspecification
prediction. Annotator-disagreement studies [149, 150] report γ values in the 0.06–0.14
range, placing real preference learning firmly in our quadratic regime.
Knowledge editing.
ROME [52] and MEMIT [53] established targeted weight edit-
ing; Mitchell et al. [151] proposed memory-based alternatives (SERAC) that avoid weight
modification altogether. Wolf et al. [152] identified failure modes; Lu et al. [153] ob-
served capacity limits empirically. The NeurIPS 2024 WISE framework [154] introduced
the “impossible triangle” framing (reliability, locality, and generalisation cannot be
simultaneously optimised) of which our Locality-Generalisation Impossibility (Theo-
rem 3.14) formalises the locality-generalisation edge under the superposition hypothesis.
Recent 2025 methods including LyapLock [155], Latent Knowledge Scalpel [156], and
NeuralDB [157] report sequential editing to much higher K values than the K∗≈13
our bound predicts on Llama-2-7B; we reconcile this by noting that K∗here scopes

the in-weight, locality-preserving, generalisation-preserving regime under Assumption 3.13.
Each of these 2025 methods relaxes at least one of these three conditions: LyapLock
uses Lyapunov-constrained updates that operate in a different feasible region than
the tolerance-τ regime our theorem analyses; Latent Knowledge Scalpel operates in a
disentangled latent-concept basis rather than the approximate-orthogonality regime of
Assumption 3.13; NeuralDB augments the model with an external memory lookup so
edits are not strictly in-weight. K∗therefore remains the correct bound for in-weight,
superposition-regime editing, and the 2025 scaling results are consistent with, rather
than contradictory to, the impossibility specification. ROME’s degradation at K ≈15
and MEMIT’s at K ≈25 are both predicted by K∗≈τ
√
d/(cη(1 −1/α)) using Llama-
2’s superposition parameters [54, 55]. The superposition hypothesis is the geometric
foundation our theorem exploits. Cross-architecture predictions for Pythia-6.9B and
Mistral-7B (within ±1–2 standard deviations of theory) validate the specification beyond
the original benchmark model.
Model collapse and synthetic data.
Shumailov et al. [59] proved the first collapse
theorem for Gaussian models under replacement; Alemohammad et al. [60] taxonomised
collapse regimes; Dohmatob et al. [61, 158] connected to scaling laws. Gerstgrasser et
al. [62] showed accumulation (mixing real data) avoids collapse. Our contribution
(Theorems 3.8 and 3.12) provides explicit constants (c1 = 1/(128π), π2/6), extends via
chain-rule KL to autoregressive sequences (Proposition 3.11, avoiding the vanishing-
constant pitfall of the naive union-bound approach), and quantifies exactly how small ρ
suffices: ρ ≥0.01 bounds divergence independently of T.
Evolutionary alignment.
EvoPref’s place in the literature is more recent. Multi-
objective alignment is an emerging area [51]; personalised combinations [159] and
pluralistic alignment frameworks have been proposed but rarely formalised as quality-
diversity search. Population-based training [160, 57] treats populations statically; NSGA-
II selection with behavioural-diversity objectives is our specific methodological commit-
ment. The 47-point coverage improvement over single-policy DPO emerges precisely
from the preference phase transition: gradient methods collapse to one mode because the
BT likelihood surface is unimodal, so population-based search is the natural response to
the impossibility. EvoPref is not the chapter’s primary contribution. It is the constructive
response to Impossibility Specification 6, demonstrating the methodology in miniature.
Running Example (Continued): Adapting the Compliance Assistant.
The
institution fine-tunes a Llama-2 7B base model on 2,000 annotated regulatory documents
using LoRA with rank r = 16. Four questions determine whether the adaptation is safe:
• Does the PAC-Bayes bound certify generalisation? With r = 16, d + k = 8192, N = 2000:
eO(
p
r(d + k)/N) ≈0.18, non-vacuous.


## Page 94


Chapter 3. The Adaptation Cliff
71
• Are preference annotations reliable enough? Regulatory interpretation admits multiple
valid readings; annotator disagreement κ ≈0.65 corresponds to γ ≈0.08, firmly in
the quadratic regime. Need eΘ(n2/γ2) ≈15,000 preference pairs, not 800.
• Can synthetic data expand the corpus? Pure replacement guarantees collapse; 1% real
data suffices to stabilise.
• Can point edits correct errors? K∗≈13 for Llama-2 7B. Beyond 13 edits: re-fine-tune.
Four cliffs, four specifications. Each converts a “how do I adapt safely” question into a
computable rule.
3.2
Do Adapted LLMs Generalise? The Safety Certificate
The first question is whether adaptation preserves generalisation.
Classical PAC-
Bayes [135, 136] provides generalisation certificates, but achieving non-vacuous bounds
for LLMs at 7B–70B scale has required exploiting structure beyond the naive param-
eter count [44, 45]. We show that LoRA [39], which constrains adaptation to a rank-r
subspace at each layer, admits non-vacuous PAC-Bayes bounds scaling in the adapter
parameter count q = mr(d + k) rather than the full model size p, complementing the
adapted-model-as-a-whole bounds of Lotfi et al. [44] and Hu et al. [45]; the immediate
practitioner consequence is a rank-32 ceiling at Alpaca scale not derivable from the
whole-model analysis.

isotropic Gaussian prior.

The key structural insight: LoRA constrains adaptation to a rank-r subspace, so
the effective parameter count is q = mr(d + k) rather than the full model size p. For
Llama-2 7B with rank 16 and LoRA applied to WQ, WV across 32 layers: m = 64,
q = 64 × 16 × (4096 + 4096) = 8.4 × 106, compared to p = 6.7 × 109, a three-orders-of-
magnitude reduction that makes the bound non-vacuous.
[X] Limitation The LoRA PAC-Bayes bound does not assert that every LoRA-
adapted model generalises. It asserts that the population risk is upper-bounded
by the right-hand side of (3.2), which is useful only when this quantity is below
one. Three scope boundaries: (i) the bound requires an isotropic Gaussian prior P
and Gaussian posterior Q; informative or data-dependent priors can yield tighter
bounds but require a separate derivation. (ii) The Monte Carlo correction of
Lem. 3.2 assumes the LoRA Hessian has low effective rank (keff ≤50), validated
empirically on Llama-2 but not proved architecturally. (iii) The rank-32 ceiling is
an Alpaca-scale (N ≈50,000 tokens per document) finding; larger corpora shift
the ceiling upward by
√

conditional on the corpus scale at which the bound was computed.

3.3
When Does Preference Learning Break? The Misspeci-
fication Tolerance
RLHF [140, 48] and its reparameterisation DPO [49] assume the Bradley-Terry model [161]:
P(y1 ≻y2 | x) = σ(r∗(x, y1) −r∗(x, y2)). Real human preferences deviate from this
model, a deviation we quantify by γ = sup |P∗−σ(r∗
1 −r∗
2)| for the best-fitting reward
r∗. We prove that any γ > 0 triggers a sharp phase transition.
3.3.1
The Phase Transition
Under uniform random pair sampling, any Bradley-Terry misspecification γ > 0 triggers a
discontinuous jump in asymptotic sample complexity from Θ(n log n/∆2) to eΘ(n2/γ2).
Consider n items with minimum gap ∆= miny1̸=y2 |r∗(y1) −r∗(y2)|.
Theorem 3.3 (Well-Specified Complexity). When γ = 0: Nwell = Θ(n log(n/δ)/∆2).
[@] Intuition The “Bradley-Terry works slightly imperfectly” intuition is wrong.
Under the exact model, tournament-style comparison has n log n complexity be-
cause each comparison is informative. Any deviation γ > 0 means an adversary
can route γ fraction of the probability mass to nuisance outcomes, and these nui-
sance outcomes can be routed to cancel the signal from non-adjacent comparisons.
Only the 2(n −1) comparisons directly involving an adjacent pair remain infor-
mative: the fraction of usable information collapses from 1 to 4/n. The asymptotic
sample-complexity rate jumps from n log n to n2, a discontinuity in the rate at the
first nonzero γ, rather than a gradual degradation. For n = 500 the boundary is
γ∗≈1.6 × 10−5; annotator agreement is never that precise, placing real preference
learning in the quadratic regime.
Theorem 3.4 (Phase Transition). For any γ > 0: Nmis = Ω(n2/γ2) (lower bound), Nmis =
O(n2 log n/γ2) (upper bound). The transition is discontinuous.
Remark 3.5 (Terminology). The result is called the “Phase Transition” by analogy to
physical phase transitions in statistical mechanics. The discontinuity is in asymptotic
sample complexity as n →∞(the rate jumps from Θ(n log n) to Θ(n2) at the first nonzero
γ), not a sharp behavioural change at finite n; at finite n, the transition is mediated
by the γ∗
n = Θ(∆/n) threshold identified in the intuition above. In statistical-learning
literature, the same phenomenon is sometimes called a “rate discontinuity” to emphasise
this asymptotic character. The thesis retains “phase transition” as the theorem’s formal
name for consistency with the decision-theoretic literature that introduced it.
[X] Limitation The Phase Transition does not assert that DPO/RLHF are useless
at any nonzero γ. They remain the best-known methods; the theorem asserts
that the sample budget needed to achieve a given suboptimality level jumps


## Page 98


Chapter 3. The Adaptation Cliff
75
quadratically at the first nonzero γ. Three scope remarks: (i) the theorem assumes
uniform random pair sampling; active-learning or experimental-design sampling
can recover Θ(n log n) comparisons against a known γ, but the bound of Thm. 3.4
is against the worst-case adversary within a γ-neighbourhood of BT. (ii) The log n
gap between the Ωand O matching bounds is an open technical question (see §3.6,
“The log n gap”); empirical scaling at n ∈{50, 100, 200, 500} favours eΘ(n2/γ2)
with no observable log n growth. (iii) The transition is in sample complexity, not
computation. The upper-bound construction requires a round-robin schedule that
itself has Θ(n2) pair-generation cost.
Proof sketch. Lower bound. Under misspecification, Pr[i ≻j] = (1 −γ)σ(r∗
i −r∗
j ) +
γqij for adversarial noise qij ∈[0, 1]. Consider hypotheses H0, H1 differing only in
the ordering of adjacent items i, i + 1. The adversary designs qij to cancel the signal
from the (1 −γ)σ(·) term for comparisons not involving items i, i + 1, rendering those
comparisons uninformative. Only 2(n −1) of (n
2) comparisons involve either target item,
so under uniform sampling the informative fraction is at most 4/n.
For informative comparisons, KL(P0∥P1) ≤4(1 −γ)2∆2/(γ2(1 −γ2)). By Fano,
distinguishing all n −1 adjacent pairs with probability ≥2/3 requires total information
≥(n −1) log 2, yielding N = Ω(n2/γ2).
Upper bound. A round-robin tournament comparing every pair m = Θ(log n/γ2)
times, with concentration via Hoeffding, yields the matching upper bound up to a
logarithmic factor.
The transition boundary is γ∗= Θ(∆/n): as soon as any misspecification appears,
sample complexity jumps from n log n to n2. For n = 500, ∆= 0.008, the transition
occurs at γ∗≈1.6 × 10−5, far below any practical annotator precision.
Figure 3.1 visualises the transition as a discontinuous step at γ = 0+: the Bradley-
Terry regime and the misspecified regime obey categorically different scaling laws, and
no smooth curve interpolates between them.

and RLHF at γ ≈0.08–0.14 (typical annotator regime per Davani et al. [150])
face a gap of Ω(0.1) in expected reward, which is practically consequential; at

DPO hard-codes the Bradley-Terry structure. The DPO population minimiser con-
verges to a reward rDPO that best fits the corrupted distribution under BT: σ(rDPO(yw) −
rDPO(yl)) = (1−γ)σ(r∗(yw) −r∗(yl)) + γ/2. A Taylor expansion of σ−1 yields rDPO(yw) −
rDPO(yl) = (r∗(yw) −r∗(yl)) + Θ(γ), so ∥rDPO −r∗∥∞= Θ(γ). By the performance
difference lemma [162], policy suboptimality is Ω(γ).
RLHF first learns an explicit reward model ˆr, then optimises against it. When the re-
ward class is rich enough (Assumption 3.6), ˆr absorbs the first-order bias, leaving O(γ2)
residual. When Assumption 3.6 fails (W ≪n/γ), both methods degrade comparably,
yielding a three-regime picture: (i) γ = 0 makes methods equivalent; (ii) γ > 0 with
sufficient capacity favours RLHF; (iii) γ > 0 with insufficient capacity yields comparable
degradation.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant: at γ ≈0.08, the DPO gap is Ω(0.08) while the
RLHF gap is O(0.006) when reward-model width satisfies W ≥C1n/γ, favouring RLHF.

γ ∈[0.06, 0.14] places practical preference learning in the quadratic regime.

increase in sample complexity (γ = 0 →γ = 0.01: 1,847 →15,329). At n = 500: 20.0×
(19,412 →387,241), consistent with n/ log n scaling.

3.4
Is Model Collapse Avoidable? The Real Data Require-
ment
With frontier models trained increasingly on their own outputs, a critical question is
whether synthetic data is safe. We prove it is not, at least not under pure replacement of
real training data with synthetic samples across successive generations.
3.4.1
Inevitability Under Replacement
Under pure synthetic-data replacement across T generations, expected TV distance to the Gaus-
sian population is lower-bounded by 1 −exp(−T2deff/(128πnmin)), forcing quadratic-in-T
expected collapse.
[@] Intuition Model collapse is often explained as “errors compound over gener-
ations”. The Gaussian analysis sharpens this: errors do not just compound. Their
variance depends on the previous generation’s covariance, which already contains
errors. The result is multiplicative rather than additive error propagation, and the
TV-distance bound grows quadratically in T, not linearly. The quadratic growth is
inevitable under pure replacement, no matter how large the per-generation sample
size is: the T2 factor dominates any 1/nmin from sample size. The escape is not
“larger batches” but “keep some real data”; the Accumulation Bound below shows
why even 1% real data changes the qualitative picture.


## Page 102


Chapter 3. The Adaptation Cliff
79

Proof sketch. At generation t, the MLE on nt samples from pt−1 yields ˆΣt with E[ ˆΣt] =
Σt−1 and Var[ ˆΣt] = O(∥Σt−1∥2/nt). Errors propagate multiplicatively: the variance of
ˆΣt depends on Σt−1, which already contains errors from previous generations. Track-
ing Dt = KL(pt∥p∗), each generation contributes E[Dt −Dt−1] ≥c · deff/nmin with
compounding. Summing over T generations and applying Pinsker yields the T2 lower
bound.
Remark 3.9 (Provenance of the 128π constant). The tracked constant 1/(128π) in the
exponent of Equation (3.3) aggregates four standard factors: (a) the Pinsker-family
reverse-direction constant (Θ(1) from the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality, E[TV] ≥
1 −exp(−c′ · KL /2)), (b) the Gaussian density normalisation factor (2π)−deff/2, con-
tributing 2π per dimension-scaled term, (c) the MLE concentration constant from the
d-dimensional Wishart distribution, and (d) the doubling factor from the per-generation
KL accumulation lower bound. A full tracking derivation in an analogous single-
distribution model collapse setting appears in Shumailov et al. [59]; the thesis ap-
plies the same structural chain to the population-collapse setting with the generation-
compounding factor yielding the T2 form. The precise constant 128π is specific to
this thesis’s assumption regime (Gaussian MLE under generation-level compounding);
alternative normalisations yield constants differing by factors of 2–8 but preserve the
T2deff/nmin scaling.

3.5
Can Knowledge Editing Be Local and General? The
Editing Budget
Knowledge editing methods such as ROME [52] and MEMIT [53] promise targeted
weight modifications that update specific facts without disturbing unrelated knowledge.
We prove this promise has a hard ceiling: under the superposition hypothesis [54],
perfect locality and perfect generalisation are jointly impossible beyond a computable
capacity K∗.
3.5.1
The Locality-Generalisation Impossibility
Under Assumption 3.13 with superposition ratio α > 1, perfect locality and perfect generalisation
are jointly unachievable; the edit budget K∗= τ

[@] Intuition The temptation is to view knowledge editing as the “change this
one fact” version of fine-tuning: clean, surgical, repeatable. Superposition says
otherwise. When a model stores n > d features in a d-dimensional residual stream,
the feature keys cannot be orthogonal; they are correlated at level O(1/

Scope note.
Structurally the impossibility is the dual of restricted-isometry failure for
overcomplete dictionaries: when α > 1 no dual frame simultaneously reconstructs one
feature exactly while preserving all others. The mechanistic-interpretability reading is
that superposition [54] is not merely a description of what LLMs do but a constraint on
what post-hoc editing can do; the

[X] Limitation The Locality-Generalisation Impossibility does not assert that edit-
ing is useless. It asserts that the locality-generalisation frontier is strictly outside
the origin for α > 1. Three scope caveats: (i) the theorem assumes Assump-
tion 3.13, the approximate-orthogonality model of superposition; architectures
violating this assumption (e.g., models trained with explicit sparse autoencoder
disentanglement) may admit tighter locality. (ii) K∗is a bound on the expected
cumulative perturbation reaching the tolerance τ; individual edit sequences may
exceed K∗harmlessly or fail below it depending on which features are targeted.
The bound is a design guideline, not a per-instance ceiling. (iii) The

Step 2: The locality correction operates in (d −1) dimensions. To reduce interference
while preserving generalisation, one adds a correction term ∆W⊥satisfying ∆W⊥wi = 0
(preserves edit to i) and minimising ∆W⊥wj for j ̸= i. The constraint ∆W⊥wi = 0
restricts ∆W⊥to the (d −1)-dimensional subspace orthogonal to wi in each row of ∆W⊥.
Perfect locality requires (∆Wmin + ∆W⊥)wj = 0 for all j ∈[n] \ {i}. This imposes
n −1 linear constraints on ∆W⊥, each of the form ∆W⊥wj = −∆Wminwj.
Step 3: Overdetermination forces residual interference. The correction ∆W⊥has at most
d −1 degrees of freedom per row (due to the orthogonality to wi). With n −1 constraints,
the system is:
• Just-determined when n −1 = d −1, i.e., α = 1. In this regime, an exact solution
exists.
• Overdetermined when n −1 > d −1, i.e., α > 1. The system has no exact solution;
by least-squares projection, the best ∆W⊥satisfies the constraints on a (d −1)-
dimensional subspace, leaving n −d = d(α −1) features with residual interference.
For the overdetermined case, the fraction of features with residual interference
is (n −d)/(n −1) = (α −1)/(α −1/n) ≈1 −1/α (for n ≫1). Each residual feature
