Part A: Strategic Interaction
Classical mechanism design assumes agents with fixed types drawn from a known
distribution [78]. This framework breaks down for LLM agents in three ways: they have
prompt-dependent preferences (the same model under different prompts produces different
rankings); they exhibit bounded contingent reasoning (GPT-4 solves only 53.4% of complete-
information games); they display emergent strategic behaviour (supra-competitive pricing
without collusion instructions [81]; punitive strategies in repeated games [82]). We
formalise these observations and derive the mechanism-choice implications.
5.2
The LLM-Rationality Model
Definition 5.1 (LLM-Rationality Model). An LLM-rational agent is a tuple (L, k∗, µ)
where L = (M, Π, C) is a language-model agent with model M, admissible prompt set Π,
and computational budget C; k∗= k∗(L, C) is its effective lookahead; and µ : Π →∆(V)
maps prompts to distributions over valuations. The agent is (k∗, ε)-rational if:
(i) it correctly evaluates all k∗-step contingencies;
(ii) it may deviate from optimal play for contingencies requiring depth greater than
k∗;
(iii) the probability of deviating from the best response among k∗-depth-accessible
strategies is at most ε.
The parameter ε captures residual irrationality within the agent’s planning hori-
zon and can be estimated empirically. Using the game-theoretic evaluation suite GT-
Bench [182], we measure ε for frontier models by computing the fraction of games in
which the model deviates from the optimal strategy among those requiring at most k∗
steps of lookahead.
Claude-3 Opus exhibits the lowest violation (ε = 0.127), consistent with strong
strategic reasoning. The prompt-reversal component ε2 is small across all models (0.015–
0.031), indicating that prompt shifts rarely reverse local orderings in well-designed
mechanisms.


## Page 154


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
131
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
GTBench-calibrated ε measurements
transfer directly: for a GPT-4-class compliance assistant serving institution, regulator,
and auditor prompts, the violation parameter ε ≤0.157 bounds aggregate deviation from
honest play across the three stakeholder prompt classes, with small prompt-reversal
(ε2 = 0.019) when interpretations are semantically close.
5.3
VCG Impossibility and OSP Feasibility
5.3.1
VCG Fails for LLM Agents
Classical VCG fails existentially for LLM agents: for any payment scheme, there is a prompt
assignment under which truthful reporting is strictly dominated, because prompt-dependent
preferences break the single-valuation assumption VCG needs.
[@] Intuition Classical VCG rests on a hidden assumption: each agent has a
single, well-defined preference over outcomes. An LLM agent violates this. The
same model instance, given the same task, can report two incompatible preference
rankings under two different prompts: an exogenously imposed role, a system
message, or a subtly different framing of the decision. The VCG payment scheme
cannot repair this: its incentive guarantee is derived from the agent’s true valua-
tion, but there is no such thing when the valuation is a function of which prompt
happens to be attached at report time. The impossibility is not that VCG is slightly
leaky for LLMs; it is that truthful reporting can be strictly dominated by misreport-
ing for at least one prompt assignment, with ε = 1 not ε = o(1). The constructive
response in §5.2.2 is to drop the assumption that the same preference must govern
every counterfactual: OSP mechanisms require only locally obvious honesty, one
information set at a time.
Theorem 5.2 (VCG Incompatibility for LLM Agents). Let MVCG be a VCG mechanism
for na ≥2 LLM-rational agents with prompt-dependent preferences. For any payment scheme
p : Vna →Rna, there exists a prompt assignment (π1, . . . , πna) ∈Πna such that truthful
reporting is not a dominant strategy for at least one agent.
Proof. Let MVCG = ( f, p) where f : Vna →A is the allocation rule and p : Vna →Rna is
the payment rule. The VCG payment scheme is pi(v) = ∑j̸=i vj( f (v)) −∑j̸=i vj( f (v−i)),
where v−i is the profile with agent i removed.

Scope note.
Theorem 5.2 formalises the endogenous-type impossibility of Bergemann
and Välimäki [78] for the specific case of LLM agents, building on Dütting et al.’s [79]
framework for rationality-aware mechanism design. Our contribution is twofold. (i)


## Page 156


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
133
A concrete endogeneity mechanism. We identify prompt-dependent preference reversal as
the specific failure mode, distinct from mutable types in the Bergemann-Välimäki
sense (where types drift across interactions) and from bounded contingent reasoning
in Dütting et al.’s sense (where agents fail counterfactual computations). Prompt-
dependent preference reversal is a third, distinct failure mode specific to instruction-
tuned transformers: the same model, given the same task, produces strictly-different
preference orderings under two prompts drawn from the same admissible prompt set
Π. (ii) An existentially tight construction. The proof exhibits a specific prompt assignment
(π1, . . . , πna) with a strictly-dominating deviation for at least one agent, for any VCG
payment scheme and any na ≥2. The incompatibility holds even if the mechanism
designer knows M (but not the prompt), even if Π is finite, and even if valuations
follow a known distribution over prompts. The constructive response of §5.3.2 is k∗-OSP
mechanisms with Chebyshev-controlled prompt-reversal, which requires neither fixed
preferences nor deep counterfactual reasoning, addressing the prompt-dependence
directly rather than patching VCG.
[X] Limitation The VCG Incompatibility theorem does not assert that every
mechanism fails for LLM agents; it asserts that VCG in particular fails when
preferences are prompt-dependent. Three points require care. First, the failure
is existential (∃prompt assignment), not universal: for many natural prompt
distributions, the failing assignment has low probability and empirical VCG
performance can still be tolerable. Second, the argument assumes agents know
their own valuations under the realised prompt; if the prompt is unknown to the
agent itself, weaker solution concepts (Bayes-Nash incentive compatibility, ex-
post incentive compatibility) may still admit VCG-style constructions. Third, the
theorem is silent on payoff-equivalent randomised mechanisms or OSP alternatives;
the latter is the constructive route taken in §5.2.2. The correct operational reading:
if a deployment cannot bound the adversarial prompt distribution, VCG is unsafe;
if it can, standard quantitative refinements apply and the OSP fallback is strictly
more conservative.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
Under VCG across the three stakeholder
groups, Theorem 5.2 guarantees that at least one group has a strictly dominating misre-
port available under some regulatory-interpretation prompt, regardless of the payment
rule. The institution benefits most directly: supplying a lenient-interpretation prompt
on regulator-facing queries and a strict-interpretation prompt on auditor-facing queries
creates exactly the prompt assignment the impossibility proof exhibits.
5.3.2
OSP Feasibility
Obviously-strategy-proof mechanisms restore incentive compatibility for LLM agents with
violation ε ≤ε1 + ε2, under a Chebyshev bound on prompt-reversal; the empirical bound
ε ≤0.16 holds for GPT-4 on the GTBench evaluation suite.

The VCG incompatibility motivates Obviously Strategy-Proof mechanisms [85].
OSP mechanisms do not require agents to have fixed preferences over all outcomes; they
only require that at each decision point, the agent can identify the honest action without
reasoning about distant counterfactuals.
[@] Intuition An OSP mechanism does not ask the agent to simulate the entire
game tree; it asks the agent only to compare, at each information set it reaches,
the worst-case honest payoff with the best-case deviation payoff: a local compar-
ison. For an LLM this is exactly the regime where bounded-lookahead agents
perform well: a two-step local check is tractable, a full subgame-perfect analysis
is not. The violation then splits into two additive pieces (under the approximate-
independence assumption ρ < 0.2 verified on GTBench-style admissible-prompt
sets; see the proof sketch): within-horizon irrationality (the agent fails the lo-
cal check despite having the information) and prompt-reversal probability (the
agent’s valuation shifts mid-protocol before the check completes). The Chebyshev
bound on the second piece is the key quantitative contribution: ε2 ≤Tσ2
π/δ2
min
converts prompt sensitivity into an information-set-count-weighted tax that the
mechanism designer can bound in advance. At GPT-4 scale this yields ε ≤0.157,
tight enough for practical OSP deployment.

achieves incentive compatibility with violation parameter
ε ≤ε1 + ε2,

Proof sketch. Following Pycia and Troyan [85], k-OSP implementable functions corre-
spond to “millipede games” of depth k. For k∗= 2, the OSP condition compares
worst-case honesty against best-case deviation at each information set within the 2-step
horizon. This local comparison remains valid under bounded prompt-induced pref-
erence shifts. The violation arises from two sources: within-horizon irrationality ε1
(measured via GTBench) and prompt-reversal probability ε2 ≤Tσ2
π/δ2
min (bounded via
Chebyshev). We assume ε1 and ε2 are approximately independent across the admissible
prompt set Π: within-horizon rationality is a structural property of the model, whereas
prompt-reversal is a valuation-shift property of Π, and the two do not share a causal
mechanism. Under this independence hypothesis, the union-bound decomposition
ε ≤ε1 + ε2 follows directly. If Π is restricted to semantically similar prompts (para-
phrases), the independence assumption may fail and the additive decomposition is

replaced by a tighter coupling ε ≤max(ε1, ε2) + ρ · min(ε1, ε2) where ρ ∈[0, 1] captures
the correlation; in practice ρ < 0.2 on GTBench-style admissible-prompt sets.
Corollary 5.4 (Empirical Violation Bounds). With marketplace parameters T ≤10 and
σπ/δmin ≤0.05, the violation bounds from Table 5.1 are: GPT-4 ε ≤0.157; Claude-3 Opus
ε ≤0.127; Llama-3-70B ε ≤0.207; Mixtral-8x22B ε ≤0.220.
[X] Limitation The OSP Feasibility theorem does not assert ε →0; it asserts
ε ≤ε1 + ε2 with the second term controlled by Chebyshev. Three misreadings are
tempting. First, ε = 0.16 for GPT-4 is not zero: roughly one in six agent-actions
may violate OSP, which is acceptable for low-stakes marketplaces but not for
safety-critical ones without further protection. Second, the Chebyshev bound on
ε2 requires the prompt-induced shift σπ to be bounded and estimable in advance;
for adversarial prompt distributions σπ may be unbounded and the theorem is
silent. Third, the result applies to k∗-OSP with bounded lookahead; deep-lookahead
mechanisms (arbitrarily complex clinching sequences) are outside the theorem’s
scope and may not admit a comparable bound. In practice, k∗= 2 is a conservative
default; k∗≥3 should be accompanied by an empirical GTBench measurement
confirming ε1 is small at that depth.
Algorithm 4 provides the explicit construction of a k∗-OSP mechanism via Pycia-
Troyan millipede games.
Millipede games realise OSP because agents face only binary local choices at each
clinching node: accept at posted price pj∗or reject and continue. The worst-case payoff
from accepting (Vj∗qij∗−pj∗≥0 by construction) dominates the best-case payoff from
rejecting in subsequent rounds. The local comparison only requires lookahead depth
k∗= 2: the agent compares current-round acceptance against the best subsequent-round
offer, not against arbitrary future strategic possibilities. This matches the empirical
effective lookahead of frontier LLMs (Section 5.2).
Figure 5.1 visualises why VCG fails and why OSP succeeds for LLM-rational agents.
Impossibility Specification 14 (Mechanism Choice Rule). Classical VCG fails for
agents with prompt-dependent preferences; Obviously Strategy-Proof mechanisms
succeed with bounded lookahead. Boundary condition: prompt-dependent prefer-
ence reversal, computable from the agent’s prompt admissibility set. Violation cost:
unrestricted preference reversals make VCG dominant-strategy incompatible for
any na ≥2. The specification S14: (i) measure ε1 empirically via GTBench; (ii) use
k∗-OSP mechanisms with k∗= 2 for current frontier models; (iii) bound prompt-
reversal via σπ/δmin ≤0.05 design rule; (iv) expect ε ≤0.16 as the operational IC
guarantee.

5.4
Coalition Formation and the Strategic Manipulation
Dimension
5.4.1
LLM Coalition Formation
Coalition-of-Thought with k∗-step lookahead achieves Nash stability with probability at least
1 −naε −η; empirical stability rate is 73.2% on the na ∈{4, 8, 16} test suite, above chain-of-
thought’s 58.4%.
Beyond individual strategic behaviour, LLM agents can form coalitions to improve
collective outcomes. We model this as a hedonic game.
Definition 5.5 (LLM Coalition Formation Game). An LLM coalition formation game is
(N , (⪰i)i∈N ) where N is the set of na agents and each agent i has preferences ⪰i over
coalitions containing i, induced by an LLM scoring function si : 2N →R. A partition
P = {C1, . . . , CK} is Nash stable if no agent i prefers a different coalition in P ∪{∅} to
its current one.
[@] Intuition Extending CoT stepwise reasoning to coalition formation: each
agent’s k∗-step lookahead evaluates stay-versus-deviate locally, and aggregate
stability follows by union bound when within-horizon reasoning is correct. The
probability bound 1 −naε −η decomposes the failure budget into two sources:
naε for within-horizon irrationality summed over agents (each misreporting with
probability at most ε), and η for preference-structure features that genuinely re-
quire depth greater than k∗. The bound is a union-bound statement, not a tightness
claim; empirical 73.2% Nash stability on the na ∈{4, 8, 16} test suite calibrates
the aggregate failure rate and exceeds baseline chain-of-thought prompting by


## Page 161


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
138
14.8 pp.
Theorem 5.6 (Coalition-of-Thought Stability). Under the Coalition-of-Thought (CoT-CF)
protocol, where agents reason about coalition formation through k∗-step lookahead and the
mechanism employs verified best-response moves, the resulting partition is Nash stable with
probability at least 1 −naε −η, where η is the probability that k∗-lookahead insufficiently
captures the preference structure.
The solution concept here is individual-deviation Nash stability in the sense of
Definition 5.5 (following Bogomolnaia and Jackson [190]), not strong stability or coalition-
proof stability. A partition is Nash-stable if no single agent gains from unilaterally
switching coalitions; it may still admit profitable deviations by subcoalitions. Strong
stability (immunity to subcoalition deviations) is computationally harder to enforce and
beyond the scope of the CoT-CF protocol, which targets the weaker but more tractable
individual-deviation concept.
Empirically, CoT-CF achieves 73.2% Nash stability: 31.4 pp above standard prompt-
ing (41.8%) and 14.8 pp above chain-of-thought prompting (58.4%). The evaluation uses
a coalition-game test suite of hedonic games with na ∈{4, 8, 16} agents; the numbers
here summarise the aggregate stability rate across all settings.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
When internal audit teams, regulators,
and external auditors form review coalitions (for example, internal audit and external
auditors partnering to examine a disputed interpretation), CoT-CF with k∗=2 lookahead
achieves Nash stability with probability at least 1 −3ε −η on the three-stakeholder
instance, bounding the expected rate at which a review coalition unilaterally dissolves.
At the 73.2% empirical stability rate, roughly one in four review cycles requires recon-
vening.
5.4.2
The Strategic Manipulation Dimension
Manipulation detection is PAC-learnable with sample complexity scaling linearly in the Strategic
Manipulation Dimension; computationally intractable for k ≥3 coalitions, tractable when
SMD(G) = O(log na).
The central question for detection: given a coalition formation game G, can we
reliably identify manipulating coalitions from observed behaviour?
Definition 5.7 (Strategic Manipulation Dimension). For a coalition formation game G,
the Strategic Manipulation Dimension SMD(G) is the VC dimension of the hypothesis
class Hmanip = {hC : P →{0, 1}} where hC indicates whether coalition C ⊆N is
manipulating under observed partition P.


## Page 162


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
139
[@]
Intuition PAC learnability of manipulation detection reduces to VC-
dimension control of the manipulation-hypothesis class:
coalitions are de-
tected by predicates hC over observed partitions, and the sample complexity
O(dSMD/(γ2λ2ε2) · log(na/δ)) is the standard PAC-learning bound with γ the
observed manipulation signal, λ the separation margin between manipulating
and non-manipulating partitions, and ε the residual detection error. Compu-
tational tractability flips at k = 3 because 3-coalition identification admits a
reduction from 3-SAT; for games with SMD(G) = O(log na), the hypothesis class
is small enough that empirical risk minimisation runs in polynomial time. The
SMD = O(log na) tractability boundary is the practical version of the statistical-
versus-computational tradeoff.
Theorem 5.8 (PAC Detection Bounds). For a coalition formation game G with Strategic
Manipulation Dimension SMD(G) = dSMD:
(a) Sample complexity: O(dSMD/(γ2λ2ε2) · log(na/δ)) observations suffice for (1 −δ)-
confident detection with manipulation probability γ, separation margin λ, and residual
error ε.
(b) Computational complexity: Detection is NP-hard for coalitions of size k ≥3; polynomial-
time when SMD(G) = O(log na).
The NP-hardness result follows by reduction from 3-SAT: identifying a k = 3
manipulating coalition is equivalent to finding a satisfying assignment of a 3-CNF
formula over the agent preference structure. The polynomial-time tractability for
SMD(G) = O(log na) follows from the standard ERM algorithm on a VC-dimension-
O(log na) hypothesis class.
SMD-DETECT, our theory-inspired detection heuristic, achieves 94.2% accuracy on
standard coalition games (Cohen’s κ = 0.89); the comparable baseline (majority-vote
over Myerson-Satterthwaite detectors, evaluated against the same GTBench-derived
coalition suite of [182]) achieves 67.4% under the same protocol.
Ancillary Specification (Manipulation Detection). Coalition formation among
LLM agents is detectable up to the Strategic Manipulation Dimension. Sample
complexity O(SMD(G)/(γ2λ2ε2) log(na/δ)); NP-hard for k ≥3 coalitions unless
SMD(G) = O(log na). This ancillary specification supports the main mechanism
choice rule (§5.3) by quantifying detection cost.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
If the coalition graph among audit sub-
teams has small SMD (e.g., SMD(G) = O(log 3) in the canonical institution/regulator/auditor
structure), SMD-DETECT flags manipulating sub-coalitions in polynomial time at 94.2%
accuracy. A k = 3 full-coalition collusion among all three stakeholder groups falls in the
NP-hard regime and must be prevented structurally (via separation-of-duties) rather
than detected statistically.

Chapter 5. The Trust Tax

Part B: Cryptographic Verification
Even with honest coordination (Part A), clients need proof that claimed computations
were actually executed. Zero-knowledge proofs provide this: the prover demonstrates
correct execution without revealing inputs, weights, or intermediate activations. But the
cost of such proofs is enormous in practice, 100–200× overhead for neural inference, and
no tight lower bound existed until now. We establish that this overhead is fundamental.
5.5
IOP Lower Bounds for Neural Operations
The dominant cost in proving neural network inference comes from non-linear activation
functions. This chapter studies interactive oracle proofs (IOPs), a class of proof systems
widely used in zero-knowledge proving for machine learning (zkML). A ReLU operation
σ(x) = max(0, x) involves a comparison that, over the prime field Fp on which the
IOP operates, requires bit-decomposition or range proofs to encode the non-algebraic
max as an arithmetic constraint. Softmax involves exponentiation and division, each
costly to express arithmetically. Despite the practical observation that non-linearities
cost 100–200× more than linear operations [95, 94, 191, 96], no formal lower bound
explaining this plateau had been established.
5.5.1
The Algebraic-Boolean Bridge
Assuming an efficient (polynomial-time) prover, IOP proof length for n evaluations of activation
σ over Fp is at least Ω(n · Cσ/ log p), tying proof cost to the Boolean circuit complexity of σ.
[@] Intuition The lemma converts an IOP proof-length lower bound into a Boolean
circuit lower bound via verifier-circuit emulation. If the IOP had a proof shorter
than Ω(Cσ/ log p), we could simulate the verifier (as a bounded-depth circuit
of size O(q log2 p)) and the prover (as a polynomial-size circuit, by the efficient-
prover assumption) and compose them into a Boolean circuit for σ smaller than
its circuit lower bound, contradicting the assumed Cσ. The log p loss is the cost of
Boolean-to-field encoding; it is tight for comparison-style activations where the
circuit lower bound is itself Θ(log p). Dropping the efficient-prover assumption
breaks the composition step, which is why Remark 5.10 flags the unbounded-
prover case as open.
Lemma 5.9 (Algebraic-Boolean Bridge). Let σ : Fp →Fp be an activation function, and
let Cσ denote the Boolean circuit complexity of computing σ on the binary representation of
elements of Fp. Any IOP with an efficient (polynomial-time) prover for the relation {(x, y) :
yi = σ(xi) for all i ∈[n]} over Fp requires proof length at least Ω(n · Cσ/ log |Fp|).
Proof sketch. Suppose for contradiction an IOP with proof length ℓ= o(n · Cσ/ log p)
exists. Setting n = 1 gives proof length ℓ1 = o(Cσ/ log p) for a single evaluation. We


## Page 164


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
141
emulate the IOP verifier as a Boolean circuit of size O(q · log2 p) (where q is query com-
plexity) and encode the efficient prover’s strategy as a circuit of size poly(ℓ1 · log p). The
composed circuit computes σ with total size O(ℓ1 · log2 p), which for ℓ1 = o(Cσ/ log p)
and q = O(log p) yields o(Cσ · log p) < Cσ for sufficiently large p, contradicting the
circuit lower bound.
Remark 5.10 (Efficient-prover assumption). The bound applies to polynomial-time
provers, covering all deployed systems. For computationally unbounded provers,
the bound still holds if oracle messages can be computed by polynomial-size circuits.
Whether the bound extends unconditionally to all IOPs remains open.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
For each of the m verified compliance
determinations run by the institution’s inference service, Lemma 5.9 forces proof length
at least Ω(n · Cσ/ log p) per activation, regardless of which zkML protocol the audit
infrastructure selects. Because deployed proving systems all use polynomial-time
provers, the efficient-prover hypothesis of Remark 5.10 holds automatically for the
compliance deployment.
5.5.2
Tight Bounds for Neural Activations
Per-activation IOP lower bounds split by epistemic type: unconditional Ω(n log p) for ReLU
and for Softmax’s exponentiation substep, an unconditional AC0[p] strengthening for Softmax,
and conditional-on-conjecture sharper bounds for Softmax and GELU.
Conjecture 5.11 (Softmax Circuit Complexity). Fixed-point exponentiation and normalisa-
tion over Fp require Boolean circuits of size Θ(log2 p).
Conjecture 5.12 (GELU Circuit Complexity). Gaussian error function approximation to
accuracy p−1 over Fp requires Boolean circuits of size Θ(log p · log log p).
Both conjectures are consistent with best known algorithms: repeated squaring
achieves O(log2 p) for exponentiation; polynomial evaluation of degree O(log log p)
achieves O(log p · log log p) for GELU [184, 185]. For the Softmax case, Theorem A.4
in Appendix A.4.3 establishes an unconditional AC0[p] (constant-depth circuits with
modular gates) lower bound via a Razborov-Smolensky reduction: modular exponentia-
tion (hence softmax) in the AC0[p] circuit model requires size 2Ω((log p)1/(d−1)) at depth d.
This closes part of the gap to Conjecture 5.11; the remaining factor of log p/ log log p to
the conjectured general-circuit bound is at the frontier of circuit complexity.
[@] Intuition The observation that proving non-linearities costs roughly 100–200×
more than proving linear layers has been empirical folklore in the zkML literature
for years. The question is whether this is a protocol-engineering artefact (solvable
by better encoding) or a structural limit (unavoidable). The Algebraic-Boolean
Bridge settles it as structural: any efficient-prover IOP is no cheaper than the


## Page 165


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
142
Boolean circuit for the same activation, up to a log p factor. ReLU reduces to
a comparison and comparisons over Fp take Θ(log p) Boolean gates; Softmax
reduces to modular exponentiation, unconditionally hard in AC0[p]. The practical
consequence is simple and actionable: do not try to make ReLU cheaper to verify;
change the architecture so there are fewer non-linearities to verify in the first place.
The 147× tax is the floor, not the ceiling, at κ = 128.
Scope note.
The Algebraic-Boolean Bridge says IOP proof length for activation
σ is Ω(n · Cσ/ log p), tying the proof system to the Boolean complexity of σ on bit-
representations; ReLU’s Ω(log p) falls out from the Θ(log p) comparison bound. The
composed argument, verifier-as-Boolean-circuit simulation together with an efficient-
prover constraint, yields an unconditional lower bound on proof length for a structured
relation, tight up to the log p factor. The bound applies uniformly to sumcheck-based,
lookup-argument-based, and GKR-based schemes for verifying n activations; the 147×
figure reported by [95] matches within protocol-specific constants.
Theorem 5.13 (IOP Lower Bounds for Neural Activations). Over a prime field Fp with
log p = Θ(κ), any efficient-prover IOP proving n evaluations of activation σ requires proof
length:
(i) Ω(n log p) for σ = ReLU (unconditional), arising from Θ(log p) circuit complexity
of comparison over Fp [184];
(ii) Ω(n log p) for σ = Softmax (unconditional, via the exponentiation substep);
strengthened to Ω(n · 2(log p)1/(d−1)) in the AC0[p] model at depth d (unconditional,
Theorem A.4); further strengthened to Ω(n log2 p) (conditional on Conjecture 5.11);
(iii) Ω(n log p · log log p) for σ = GELU (conditional on Conjecture 5.12).
All three bounds are tight: matching upper bounds are achieved by the sumcheck-based protocol
of [93] (ReLU), the lookup-argument of [94] (Softmax), and a hybrid protocol we construct
(GELU).
For ReLU, the Θ(log p) circuit complexity of comparison is established uncondition-
ally in the general Boolean circuit model. For Softmax, Theorem A.4 (Appendix A.4.3)
establishes an unconditional AC0[p] lower bound via Razborov-Smolensky; the full
general-circuit Ω(log2 p) bound remains conditional on Conjecture 5.11. For GELU,
both the unconditional and conjectured bounds coincide at Ω(log p · log log p), with the
conditional status retained.

5.5.3
The 147× Non-Linearity Tax
The empirical 147× non-linearity tax in deployed zkML systems is an empirical calibration of
the theoretical floor τop ≥log p = 128 at κ = 128; reducing verified cost requires removing
non-linear operations, not optimising the prover.
Definition 5.14 (Non-Linearity Tax). For a neural layer with n neurons, m input con-
nections, and activation σ, the non-linearity tax is τ(σ) = ℓσ(n)/ℓlin(n, m), where ℓσ(n)
is the IOP proof length for n evaluations of σ and ℓlin(n, m) is the length for the linear
map.
For the linear component, Brakedown [100] achieves ℓlin(n, m) = O(nm). The
per-operation non-linearity tax for ReLU is
τop(ReLU) =
ℓσ(n)/n
ℓlin(n, m)/(nm) = log p.
(5.2)
For κ = 128, τop(ReLU) ≥128. The operational range [128, 151] in practice reflects
protocol-specific constant factors above the GKR-family sum-check baseline: the sum-
check of [93] introduces 1.05× overhead from multilinear-extension interpolation rounds,
while the lookup-argument of [94] incurs approximately 1.18× from table initialisation.
Both factors are overheads on top of the log p per-round sum-check cost; the specific
constants are independently reported in each cited construction. Empirical observations
match: Chen et al. [95] reported ≈130×; Peng et al. [191] found 110–190×.
Numerical example (deriving the 147× headline).
Take a 1B-parameter language
model with κ = 128 bits of soundness, prime field Fp with log2 p = 128, and n = 109
ReLU evaluations per forward pass. The theoretical floor is τop(ReLU) = log2 p = 128.
Stacking the protocol overheads yields the deployed-system ratio:
τdeployed = log2 p · (1 + ϵMLE) · (1 + ϵlookup) · (1 + ϵFS),
with ϵMLE = 0.05 (multilinear-extension interpolation rounds per [93]), ϵlookup = 0.085
(lookup-argument table initialisation per [94] averaged across ReLU/Softmax), and
ϵFS ≈0.03 (Fiat-Shamir transcript hashing overhead). Substitution gives τdeployed = 128·
1.05 · 1.085 · 1.03 ≈150.2; averaging across the two dominant activation classes (ReLU
and Softmax) using the standard transformer compute fractions (≈0.9 ReLU/GELU in
MLP blocks and ≈0.1 Softmax in attention, by operation count per forward pass on a
standard decoder block) yields the ≈147× headline. The 147× figure is therefore best
read as an empirical calibration of the log p theoretical floor within the [110, 190]× band
reported by Chen et al. [95] and Peng et al. [191], with the floor τop ≥log p = 128 the
structural content.

overhead is approximately 147× relative to the unverified linear baseline. A determina-
tion that runs in 10 seconds unverified takes roughly 22 minutes verified. Reducing this
cost requires architectural change (fewer activations per determination, e.g., distilled
smaller models or fused layers), not prover tuning: Theorem 5.13 shows the log p = 128
floor is structural.

5.6
The Collapse Folding Scheme
Proving a d-layer neural network requires a circuit whose size is the sum of all layer
circuits. Recursive folding enables the verifier to process the network incrementally.
Collapse [97, 98] achieves O(d) verifier cost via Layered Sumcheck Accumulation (LSA).
For a d-layer network computing hℓ= σ(Wℓhℓ−1 + bℓ), the correctness claim is
∀ℓ∈[d] : ∑x ˜hℓ(x) · ˜gℓ(x) = cℓ, where ˜hℓ, ˜gℓare multilinear extensions of the layer
output and constraint polynomial. LSA accumulates these sumcheck claims layer by
layer:
Aℓ= (Aℓ−1, ρℓ, γℓ, Com(˜hℓ)),
(5.3)
where ρℓis the random challenge at step ℓ, γℓ= ˜hℓ(ρℓ) is the prover’s claimed partial
evaluation, and Com(˜hℓ) is a binding commitment. Each update requires a single
random challenge and O(log nℓ) field operations.
Definition 5.15 (State-Binding Accumulator). An accumulator scheme is state-binding
if the accumulator Aℓat step ℓcontains a binding commitment to the entire protocol
transcript up to step ℓ.
Lemma 5.16 (State-Binding Property). The Collapse accumulator is state-binding under
the binding property of the commitment scheme. In the random oracle model, any Fiat-Shamir
instantiation of Collapse is immune to the transcript-omission attacks of [187].
[@] Intuition HyperNova already achieves logarithmic verifier cost per layer,
but the recursive verifier circuit (the thing a successor proof must verify) grows
polylogarithmically with layer width. For billion-parameter models this is the
binding bottleneck, not the verifier cost itself. Collapse exploits the fact that
every layer’s sumcheck has the same form: its Layered Sumcheck Accumulation
collapses all layer checks into a single running accumulator whose size grows
logarithmically only in depth. The payoff is not theoretical alone: at LLaMA-7B
width, the recursive circuit shrinks from 921,600 gates (HyperNova) to 294,912 (a
3.1× reduction), and the state-binding property of Lem. 5.16 closes the transcript-
omission attack of Dao et al. structurally rather than by patching individual
challenge sequences.
Positioning against recent folding-scheme advances (2024).
Collapse is bench-
marked alongside HyperNova, but two further folding schemes from late 2024 warrant
explicit engagement. Mova [192] (IACR 2024/1220) removes the commitment to error
and cross terms by replacing them with multilinear-extension evaluations at a verifier-
sampled random point, achieving a 1.05–1.3× prover speedup over HyperNova under
the assumption that the R1CS witness contains only small elements. Mova’s optimisa-
tion axis (prover work under small-witness structure) is complementary to Collapse’s,
which optimises verifier cost and recursive-circuit size; Collapse’s Layered Sumcheck
Accumulation could in principle be composed with Mova’s error-term-free commitment

address different problem scales: NeutronNova is asymptotically optimal for folding a
single zero-check or a small bounded number of them, whereas Collapse is asymptoti-
cally optimal for layer-by-layer accumulation in deep neural-network inference where d
is large and each layer presents a distinct constraint system. The O(d log nmax) verifier
cost of Theorem 5.17 remains the tightest known bound for this specific task. A unified
framework combining NeutronNova’s zero-check efficiency with Collapse’s layered
accumulation is an open problem for the folding-scheme programme.
Theorem 5.17 (Collapse Folding Complexity). For a d-layer network with maximum width
nmax, Collapse achieves:
• verifier cost O(d · log nmax), depth-only overhead;
• recursive circuit size O(log2 nmax), 2–3× smaller than HyperNova [98];
• prover cost O(d · nmax · log nmax).
The improvement exploits the structured nature of neural network constraints:
each layer’s sumcheck has the same form, allowing shared verification work.
Figure 5.3 illustrates the Layered Sumcheck Accumulation pipeline and compares
recursive circuit sizes.
Collapse is positioned as the optimal construction given the non-linearity tax: its
O(d) verifier cost means that for a fixed number of layers d, the only way to reduce total
verified cost further is to reduce the number of non-linear operations per layer, which is
exactly what Theorem 5.13 says cannot be avoided below the log p floor. The two results
combine to establish that: Collapse is depth-optimal; non-linearity reduction is width-optimal;
further reductions require architectural change, not protocol engineering.

Part C: The Welfare Composition Theorem
Parts A and B establish two independent impossibilities with their constructive re-
sponses. But the deepest result of the chapter, and the thesis’s first full compositional
claim, is that the two cannot be separated. Neither honest mechanism design alone nor
cryptographic verification alone suffices; both are jointly necessary, and their composi-
tion yields guarantees exponentially better than either alone.
5.7
The Welfare Composition Theorem: Joint Necessity
5.7.1
The AI Agent Marketplace Model
The marketplace model has na agents bidding on m tasks of value Vj, and two failure modes:
undetectable computation substitution and strategic misreporting of competence.


## Page 171


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
148
Consider a marketplace with na LLM agents and m tasks. Each task j ∈[m] has a
client who specifies the task, a required computation Cj (a neural network inference),
and a value Vj ∈[Vmin, Vmax] to the client. Agent i has cost cij ∈[0, Vmax] for executing
Cj and competence qij ∈[0, 1]. The mechanism allocates tasks to agents and determines
payments, maximising social welfare
W =
m
∑
j=1
Vj · q f (j),j,
(5.4)
where f : [m] →[na] is the assignment function. Let f ∗denote the socially optimal
allocation maximising W, and W∗= W( f ∗).
We model two complementary failure modes:
• Computation substitution. An agent allocated task j may return an approximation
˜Cj with degraded quality ˜qij = qij −∆j instead of executing Cj. Without verifica-
tion, the client cannot detect this substitution; with verification, the substitution is
detected with probability 1 −e−κ.
• Strategic task selection. An agent may misreport competence to bid on easier rather
than higher-value tasks. Under an incentive-compatible mechanism with violation
parameter ε, the probability of strategic misreporting is at most ε.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
The marketplace maps directly: the insti-
tution (as client) specifies m regulatory determinations with per-determination values
Vj; three stakeholder groups act as agents bidding with competence qij reflecting their
expertise on each determination class. Both failure modes apply: cheaper-but-wrong
interpretations substitute for correct ones (computation substitution), and stakeholders
over-bid on easier interpretations they prefer (strategic selection).
5.7.2
The Theorem
Without verification, expected welfare loss is Ω(m∆); without mechanism design, expected loss
is Ω(naεVmax); with both (Part (iii), in the random-oracle model), the composition achieves
O((ε + e−κ)Vmax), with a negligible standard-model coupling residual.
[@] Intuition The thesis’s Parts A and B each delivered one impossibility and
one constructive scheme. The natural critical question is whether they are two
parallel stories or a single story. The welfare composition theorem answers: they
are a single story whose joint bound is asymptotically far better than either pillar
alone. Without verification, a mechanism alone leaks Ω(m∆) welfare because
agents substitute cheaper approximate computations once they cannot be caught.
Without a mechanism, verification alone leaks Ω(naεVmax) because agents bid
strategically on easy tasks once prices do not incentivise truthfulness. With both,

either pillar alone comes from the fact that e−κ is negligible at cryptographic
security parameters (not from multiplicative error cancellation). The result is the
thesis’s first proof that the trust stack is more than the sum of its parts: omitting
either pillar is not a partial solution, it is a linear-in-scale liability.

Remark 5.20 (Graceful degradation under heterogeneous rationality). Part (i) of The-
orem 5.18 is tight under the worst-case assumption that every agent substitutes ap-
proximate computations on every task. In practice, deployments are heterogeneous:
reputation-conscious agents, reputationally-salient tasks, and auditor-triggered spot
checks each reduce the effective substitution rate. Let ρ ∈[0, 1] denote the fraction of
(agent, task) pairs that undergo substitution in a given deployment. Tracing the proof
through yields the refined lower bound W∗−E[W(M, no V)] ≥Ω(ρ · m∆), which
degrades gracefully from the worst case ρ = 1. The thesis’s qualitative conclusion,
that some form of verification is welfare-necessary, is robust to heterogeneous ρ as long
as ρ ≥1/m, i.e., at least one substitution per deployment. The quantitative Ω(m∆)
benchmark is a worst-case calibration, not a claim that real deployments lose this much
welfare uniformly. This is important when the theorem is cited as policy evidence: the
correct framing is “verification prevents the welfare loss that would occur in the worst
case,” not “every unverified deployment loses Ω(m∆) in practice.”


## Page 175


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
152
[X] Limitation The Welfare Composition theorem does not prove verification
and mechanism design are sufficient; it proves they are jointly necessary for the
specific welfare functional studied. Four clarifications prevent over-reading. First,
part (iii) requires independence in the ROM; a standard-model reduction incurs an
additional multiplicative coupling term, negligible at κ = 128 but not zero. Second,
the Ω(m∆) lower bound in part (i) is worst-case over adversarial substitution; real
deployments with reputation effects or audit-triggered spot checks may experience
loss Ω(ρ · m∆) for substitution fraction ρ ∈[0, 1] (Rem. 5.20). Third, the theorem
is stated in the one-shot AI Agent Marketplace Model; repeated interactions,
sequential allocations, and dynamic preferences are outside its scope. Fourth,
the welfare functional is quasilinear; generalisations to fairness-constrained or
risk-sensitive welfare (the usual objects of regulatory concern) require separate
analysis. The practical reading: the composition is necessary under the model, and
robust enough to survive the most common deployment modifications, but it is not
a certification that a deployed system is trustworthy end-to-end.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
Applying Theorem 5.18 to the three-
stakeholder compliance deployment: omitting verification leaks Ω(m∆) welfare through
substituted approximate determinations (Part (i)); omitting the mechanism leaks Ω(3εVmax)
welfare through strategic stakeholder bidding (Part (ii)); the composition bounds total
expected loss by O((0.16 + e−128)Vmax) at GPT-4 scale in the ROM (Part (iii)), with the
e−128 verification term negligible and the mechanism term dominating.
5.7.3
Independence Assumption and Its Justification
In the random-oracle model, oracle-drawn challenges factor through any adversarial strategy, so
mechanism and verification failure events are independent up to a negligible term; deployment-
correlated strategies preserve the factored bound.
The proof of Part (iii) depends on independence of the mechanism and verification
errors, formally, that Emech and Ever are disjoint or near-disjoint events. We justify this
in the random oracle model:
[@] Intuition Independence in the ROM has two threat models. Oracle-correlation:
an adversary tries to guess or control the random challenges that drive both the
OSP information-set selection and the Collapse sumcheck folding; the ROM rules
this out by treating the hash as a true random function. Deployment-correlation: a
single LLM acting as an agent correlates its own strategy across the two sub-
systems (when to misreport a valuation, when to substitute a computation),
conditioning each decision on prompts and tasks. This does not correlate the
challenges themselves (still oracle-drawn) but couples the agent’s strategies. The
per-strategy factored bound survives because OSP incentive compatibility and


## Page 176


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
153
Collapse soundness each hold for every fixed strategy A, and maximising over
strategies preserves factorisation up to a negl(κ) residue.
Proposition 5.21 (Independence in the ROM). In the random oracle model, if the OSP
mechanism uses random challenges drawn from the oracle for information-set selection, and the
Collapse protocol uses independent random challenges from the oracle for sumcheck folding, then
Pr[Emech ∩Ever] = Pr[Emech] · Pr[Ever] + negl(κ).
Proof sketch. We must address two distinct correlation threats.
(a) Oracle-correlation adversary. The OSP mechanism’s challenges determine which
information sets the agent faces; the Collapse protocol’s challenges determine the sum-
check verification equations. In the ROM, the two challenge sequences are drawn from
independent random variables. Any adversary strategy that correlates the two must
either predict future oracle outputs (which occurs with probability negl(κ)) or control
the oracle itself (ruled out by the ROM assumption). This gives the factored probability
statement in the proposition.
(b) Deployment-correlation adversary. A more subtle threat arises when a single
LLM deployed as an agent correlates its own behaviour across the two subsystems: the
agent may decide when to substitute computations conditional on which prompt it
receives, and may decide which prompt-induced valuation to report conditional on
which computations it plans to substitute. Crucially, this deployment-correlation threat
does not violate the random-variable independence of the challenges themselves (the
challenges are still drawn independently by the oracle) but it may correlate the agent’s
strategy across the two subsystems.
The resulting bound is nevertheless compatible with the factored statement, as
follows. Let A denote the agent’s joint strategy mapping prompts and computation-
tasks to deviations in each subsystem. Write EA
mech and EA
ver for the strategy-conditioned
error events. The OSP guarantee (Pr[EA
mech] ≤naε) holds for every strategy A, because
OSP incentive-compatibility is a property of each information set in isolation; similarly
Pr[EA
ver] ≤me−κ for every A by the Collapse soundness theorem. By the ROM-derived
independence of challenges, strategy-conditioned events inherit the factored bound:
Pr[EA
mech ∩EA

and the welfare bound becomes O((ε + e−κ + δcoup)Vmax), which at κ = 128 remains
numerically dominated by ε. Finer characterisation of δcoup under specific hash-function
classes is left to future work.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
The deployment-correlation threat mat-
ters for the Compliance Assistant specifically because one LLM plays all three stake-
holder roles behind different prompts; a fully rational strategic LLM could in principle
correlate its stakeholder-facing misreports with its verification-bypass strategy. The
ROM-derived factored bound of Proposition 5.21 holds regardless, because challenges
driving OSP and Collapse are independently oracle-drawn, and OSP and Collapse
soundness each hold for every fixed strategy the LLM might adopt.

and a 7-minute audit on average.
5.7.5
Why This Theorem Matters
The composition is the thesis’s first joint-necessity proof: each pillar’s absence produces linear-
in-scale welfare loss, their combination yields an exponentially-smaller additive bound (in the
ROM), serving as the template for cross-chapter composition in Chapter 6.
Theorem 5.18 is the thesis’s first proof that two impossibility specifications are
jointly necessary. Its significance is threefold.
Each component specifies the cost of omitting the other. Part (i) quantifies the cost of
omitting verification as Ω(m∆); Part (ii) quantifies the cost of omitting mechanism
design as Ω(naε). These are not abstract concerns: they are computable from observable
marketplace parameters.
The composed system is exponentially better than either alone. The shift from Ω(m∆)
to O(me−κ) is the key leverage: verification’s exponential suppression combined with
mechanism design’s ε tolerance yields a guarantee stronger than the sum of parts.
This is the template for cross-chapter composition. Chapter 6 will prove the corre-
sponding composition theorem for computation × grounding (Chs. 2×4). The welfare
composition is the blueprint: two impossibility specifications compose via an additive
error bound under ROM independence, the composed bound is exponentially better
than either independent bound at cryptographic security parameters, and the composi-
tion is itself a third impossibility specification (omitting the composition violates joint
necessity).

Impossibility Specification 16 (Joint Necessity). Mechanism design and crypto-
graphic verification are jointly necessary for trustworthy AI deployment in multi-
agent settings. Boundary condition B16(θ): either M is absent OR V is absent
⇒the system fails at least one welfare axiom. Without verification: welfare loss
Ω(m∆). Without mechanism design: welfare loss Ω(naε). With both: welfare loss
O(ε + e−κ), exponentially better. The specification S16: deploy both jointly; use
k∗-OSP mechanisms for allocation and Collapse folding for verification; the welfare
theorem quantifies the cost of omitting either, enabling principled tradeoffs via
selective verification when full verification overhead is prohibitive.
Returning to the Compliance Assistant.
The three-stakeholder audit trail requires
both pillars jointly: a k∗=2 OSP mechanism for honest stakeholder reporting (deliv-
ering ε ≤0.16 per Theorem 5.3) and Collapse folding with selective verification for
execution integrity (delivering e−128 forgery probability on verified determinations per
Theorem 5.17). Omitting either yields linear-in-scale welfare loss per Theorem 5.18(i)–
(ii); deploying both achieves the composed bound of Part (iii). This is the operational
template that Decision Rules T1–T3 and C1–C2 implement.
5.8
Discussion and Bridge
Limitations of the welfare theorem.
Theorem 5.18 is stated for the AI Agent Mar-
ketplace Model: a one-shot, single-round allocation with quasilinear utilities. Extensions
to repeated interactions, sequential auctions, and dynamic preferences would require
techniques beyond the scope of this thesis, particularly to handle reputation effects
and long-horizon incentives. The independence assumption (Proposition 5.21) uses the
ROM; characterising welfare under relaxed independence is open.
Scope of Part B.
This chapter addresses computational verification (proving correct
neural-network inference). It does not address broader verification targets: training-
process verification (proving a model was trained on the claimed data), privacy-preserving
verification under differential privacy constraints, or composition with federated learn-
ing protocols. Each is an open research programme; the techniques here (IOP lower
bounds, folding schemes) are applicable but not directly applied.
The ε notation conflict.
Throughout Chapter 2, ε denoted the per-step CoT error
rate; here it denotes the strategic manipulation parameter. These are genuinely different
quantities, bounded respectively by the Deterministic Horizon analysis (Chapter 2) and
the GTBench measurement. We use εCoT and εmech when necessary to disambiguate; in
isolation, context determines which is meant.


## Page 181


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
158
Specifications and Open Problems
This chapter established the trust layer of the thesis’s full-stack argument and proved
that it admits two irreducible taxes and one joint-necessity law. Part A proved that VCG
mechanisms fail for LLM agents with prompt-dependent preferences (not asymptotically,
but for a constructible prompt assignment exhibiting strictly dominating misreports,
Thm. 5.2) and that k∗-OSP mechanisms restore incentive compatibility with violation
parameter ε ≤ε1 + ε2 controlled by a Chebyshev bound on prompt reversal (Thm. 5.3,
yielding ε ≤0.16 for GPT-4). The Strategic Manipulation Dimension (Def. 5.7) then
gave PAC manipulation-detection bounds that are tractable when SMD(G) = O(log na)
and NP-hard for k ≥3 coalitions (Thm. 5.8). Part B proved that proving non-linear
activations in zero knowledge is irreducibly expensive: the Algebraic-Boolean Bridge
(Lem. 5.9) tied IOP proof length to Boolean circuit complexity up to a log p factor, yield-
ing Ω(n log p) unconditional lower bounds for ReLU and Softmax (Thm. 5.13) and
explaining the empirical 147× non-linearity tax as theoretical floor rather than engi-
neering ceiling. The Collapse folding scheme (Thm. 5.17) then achieved O(d log nmax)
verifier cost via Layered Sumcheck Accumulation: a 2–3× reduction in recursive circuit
size over HyperNova, with structural immunity to the Dao et al. transcript-omission
attack via the state-binding property.
Part C then proved the thesis’s first joint-necessity composition (Thm. 5.18): with-
out verification, welfare loss is Ω(m∆); without mechanism design, welfare loss is
Ω(naεVmax); with both, in the random oracle model, welfare loss collapses to O((ε +
e−κ)Vmax), exponentially better than either pillar alone and numerically under 10−36 rel-
ative loss at ε = 0.16, κ = 128. The composition is not an analogy; it is an additive-error
decomposition over independent events (mechanism misreport, verification forgery)
whose independence is justified via random-oracle challenges (Prop. 5.21), with the two
events strictly composable and the second’s probability exponentially suppressed by the
security parameter. The structural consequence closes Chapter 5: the two communities
this chapter bridges, mechanism design and cryptographic verification, are not neigh-
bouring literatures but two halves of a single required guarantee. Chapter 6 articulates
the higher-order composition programme that Theorem 5.18 initiates.

of full-verification welfare at 70% overhead.
[?] Open Problem
Open Problem 5.1 (Welfare composition beyond the one-shot marketplace).
Thm. 5.18 is proved for the one-shot AI Agent Marketplace Model with quasi-
linear utilities. Real deployments are repeated, sequential, and often involve
reputation effects and regulatory constraints that are not quasilinear. Does the
joint-necessity guarantee survive? Concretely: (i) in the repeated game, does
reputation substitute for cryptographic verification as a sufficient enforcement
mechanism, or does it merely reduce ρ without removing the Ω(m∆) term?
(ii) Under fairness-constrained welfare (e.g., max-min, Gini-penalty), does the
composition bound still decompose as O(ε + e−κ), or does the fairness constraint
introduce a coupling term that does not suppress under the ROM? (iii) For dy-
namic preferences (prompts that drift over the protocol), is there an analogue of
the Chebyshev bound on ε2? A resolution would extend the thesis’s trust layer
from one-shot auctions to the operating regime of deployed AI marketplaces.
[?] Open Problem
Open Problem 5.2 (Closing the Softmax circuit-complexity gap).
Theo-
rem 5.13(ii) is unconditional at Ω(n log p) via the exponentiation substep, and
is strengthened to Ω(n log2 p) conditional on Conjecture 5.11 (that fixed-point
exponentiation over Fp requires Θ(log2 p) Boolean circuits). The conditional
bound matches deployed 147–200× overhead exactly; the unconditional bound
explains only half of it. Close the gap: either prove the Θ(log2 p) lower bound on
modular exponentiation circuit complexity (solving a frontier question in circuit
complexity), or exhibit a sub-quadratic Boolean circuit for fixed-point exponenti-
ation (which would revolutionise symmetric cryptography as a side-effect). The
Razborov-Smolensky approach in Appendix A.4.3 closes part of the gap in the
AC0[p] model; general-circuit closure is open.
Bridge to Chapter 6.
The four technical chapters have established four impossibility
specifications (Horizon, adaptation, grounding, trust) plus a fifth from their composition
(welfare). Each is a complete instance of Definition 1.1: a computable boundary, a


## Page 183


Chapter 5. The Trust Tax
160
quantified violation cost, and a constructive design rule. Theorem 5.18 is the first proof
that two of these specifications compose.
But a full compositional theory across all four domains remains the thesis’s central
open problem. Chapter 6 articulates what such a theory would require, proves the
computation-grounding composition (the second of the three cross-chapter compositions
the thesis attempts), demonstrates empirical validation via trajectory-level testing, and
extracts three emergent principles (impossibility as specification, theory-practice gaps
as diagnostics, reliability as composition) that unify the preceding chapters and define
the research programme for the next decade.

composition. Scenario (i): without verification V, an incentive-compatible mech-
anism M alone admits computation substitution (agents bid honestly but ex-
ecute approximate computations), yielding welfare loss Ω(m∆), linear in the

position M ⊙V couples honest bidding with verified computation, yielding loss

than multiplicative) error decomposition rests on error-event independence in
the random oracle model (Proposition 5.21); at production parameters (κ=128,
ε ≤0.16), e−κ is negligible and the mechanism term ε dominates. Numerical
calibration (GPT-4, 100 tasks, 1% approximation gap): scenarios (i) and (ii) each

orders of magnitude. Verification costs 128× per verified task; selective verifica-

specifications (S14 OSP and S15 non-linearity tax) combine via an additive error
bound (under ROM independence) to yield a third (S16 joint necessity) whose
guarantee is exponentially stronger than either alone.

## Page 186

Chapter 6
Synthesis
Chapters 2–5 established sixteen impossibility specifications across four domains: the
Deterministic Horizon (computation), the Adaptation Cliff (fine-tuning, preference learn-
ing, collapse, editing), the Grounding Gap (retrieval, knowledge graphs, attribution),
and the Trust Tax (mechanism design, cryptographic verification). Each specification is a
complete instance of Definition 1.1: a computable boundary, a quantified violation cost,
and a constructive design rule. Theorem 5.18 proved the first joint-necessity composition:
mechanism design and verification compose with additive error ε + e−κ, exponentially
better than either component alone because the verification term e−κ is negligible at
production security parameters (κ = 128).
This chapter does not summarise. It synthesises. The technical material of the
preceding four chapters reveals patterns invisible from within any single chapter, and
the purpose of this chapter is to expose those patterns explicitly. We (§6.1) reproduce the
16-specification catalogue with full evidence; (§6.2) prove the computation-grounding
composition theorem, the thesis’s second cross-chapter composition result; (§6.4) val-
idate trajectory-level testing empirically; (§6.5) extract three emergent principles that
unify the chapters; (§6.6) articulate the central open problem (compositional verification
across all four domains) and three additional research frontiers; (§6.7) offer a personal
reflection on what the PhD taught me about the interfaces between subfields; and (§6.8)
transparently enumerate the thesis’s limitations.
Running Example (Concluded): The Compliance Assistant, Fully Stacked.
The compliance assistant has appeared in every chapter, each time exposing a differ-
ent facet of the same challenge. The Deterministic Horizon told us when to delegate
reasoning to tools (d∗∈[19, 31]). The Adaptation Cliff told us which adaptations are
safe (rank ≤32 PAC-Bayes, K ≤13 edits, ρ ≥0.01 real data, γ-measurement before
preference training). The Grounding Gap told us how to evaluate retrieval (at least k
metrics), when to retrieve (during reasoning), and which passages actually caused the
determination (causal attribution, not attention). The Trust Tax told us how to ensure
honest multi-stakeholder auditing (OSP mechanisms, ε ≤0.16) and what to verify cryp-
tographically (147× non-linearity tax). The welfare composition theorem told us that


## Page 187


Chapter 6. Synthesis
164
the first three cannot be skipped separately without Ω(m∆) loss each. The compliance
assistant is one system, but its impossibility specifications are universal.
6.1
A Unified View: Sixteen Specifications, One Method-
ology
Table 6.1 reproduces the preview table from Chapter 1, now populated with the full evi-
dence from the preceding four chapters. Each row is a verified instance of Definition 1.1:
the boundary condition Bi(θ) is computable, the violation cost is quantified, and the
design rule is constructive.
The catalogue makes three structural features visible:
The specifications are heterogeneous in mathematical type but homogeneous in method-
ological role. S1 is a logical characterisation (FOC[Attn]); S6 is a phase transition; S9 is a
topological dimension argument; S15 is a circuit-complexity lower bound. Yet all sixteen
play the same role: converting what reliable systems cannot do into rules for how to
build them.
The specifications span the four key domains AI systems must navigate. No domain
can be omitted without leaving the system underconstrained: computation without
grounding produces hallucination; grounding without computation produces shallow
retrieval; both without trust produce strategic exploitation; trust without the first three
produces expensive but incorrect systems.
The specifications are composable, but the compositions are non-trivial. Theorem 5.18
proved mechanism × verification; the next section proves computation × grounding. A
full four-way composition remains open. Figure 6.1 visualises the sixteen specifications
organised by domain, with the two proved compositions highlighted.
6.2
Composing Two Specifications: Computation × Ground-
ing
The welfare composition theorem (Theorem 5.18) established the template: two impossi-
bility specifications, individually necessary, compose to yield a third (joint necessity).
We now apply this template to the computation-grounding interface. The resulting
theorem validates the thesis’s opening vignette: why improving retrieval from the
25th to 75th percentile gains only 2 percentage points at deep reasoning depth, while
chain-of-thought gains 15 points at no retrieval cost. This is the second of the two
checkmarked cells in the 4 × 4 compositions matrix of Figure 1.1 Row D; the first was
welfare composition (Chapter 5).

6.2.1
Setup: Joint Reliability as a Product
Grounded reasoning reliability factors into a reasoning-survival term (1 −ε)n from Chapter 2
and an information-retention term qn(1−η), with η the capacity-to-entropy ratio of Definition 6.1.
Before the formal definition.
Imagine a reasoning chain that at each hop receives
retrieved evidence of entropy H(Rt | Rt−1) bits but can only push Chop bits through
the residual-stream bottleneck of Chapter 2. If Chop ≥H(Rt | Rt−1), the bottleneck
is slack and the full evidence survives; if Chop < H(Rt | Rt−1), the bottleneck is tight
and only a fraction of per-hop evidence survives. The definition below formalises this
capacity-to-entropy ratio as η and distinguishes it from the superficially similar SDPI
contraction coefficient, which measures contraction of total-variation distance under a
specific Markov kernel and is not what we are tracking here.
Definition 6.1 (Information Retention Factor). The information retention factor η ∈[0, 1]
quantifies the fraction of per-hop information retained from retrieved evidence through


## Page 189


Chapter 6. Synthesis
166
the computational bottleneck of the base model, defined as the capacity-to-entropy ratio
η = min

1,
Chop
H(Rt | Rt−1)

,
where Chop = dmodel · O(log n) bits is the per-hop computational capacity. Throughout
this chapter, η refers to this capacity-to-entropy ratio; we do not use η to denote a strong-
data-processing-inequality (SDPI) contraction constant, which is a distinct concept tied
to specific Markov-kernel structure.
The factor η is measurable for any deployed system: dmodel is the model dimension,
and H(Rt | Rt−1) is estimated from held-out retrieval corpora. For Llama-2 7B on
multi-hop QA, η ≈0.7, estimated from per-hop retrieval-chain conditional entropy
H(Rt | Rt−1) on a held-out subset of the multi-hop QA evaluation corpus (procedure
detailed in Chapter 4).
6.2.2
The Composition Theorem
Under the Markov structure of Definition 2.27 and Assumption 6.2, grounded reasoning reli-
ability is bounded by (1 −ε)nqn(1−η), with crossover depth nc ≈6.3 at operating parameters
(ε, η, q) = (0.03, 0.7, 0.6).
[B] Aside The Computation-Grounding Composition is the second proved com-
position in the thesis, following the Welfare Composition of Theorem 5.18. The
template is identical: two specifications, individually necessary, combined via an
explicit bounding function that is monotone in both arguments and exhibits a
crossover at which the optimal investment tradeoff shifts. The crossover depth
nc ≈6.3 is the quantitative content of the thesis’s opening vignette, turning “re-
trieval and reasoning interact” from slogan into threshold: at reasoning depth
below nc, retrieval investment dominates; above nc, tool-delegated reasoning
does. The honest-obstruction report on Adaptation-Grounding (§6.3) shows what
happens when this template does not apply, and names the obstructions rather
than papering over them.
Before the formal assumption.
The Computation-Grounding composition depends
on a specific form of error independence between successive hops of the grounded chain.
The n-step product structure (1 −ε)n that underlies the Chapter 2 CoT error-propagation
analysis requires reasoning errors at hops t and t + 1 to be conditionally independent
given the state history (s0, . . . , st−1), a direct specialisation of the chain-level Markov
structure of Definition 2.27. The assumption is plausible under autoregressive decoding
but is not argued rigorously; its known failure modes (shared prompt structure, retrieval-
content recurrence, training-distribution overlap) are enumerated as Obstruction 3 of
the Adaptation × Grounding report below and recur among the ten open problems of
Section 6.6.4.

Assumption 6.2 (Reasoning-Error Conditional Independence). Reasoning-error events
at successive hops of the grounded chain are conditionally independent given prior
state:
Pr[Et ∩Et+1 | st−1, . . . , s0] = Pr[Et | st−1, . . . , s0] · Pr[Et+1 | st−1, . . . , s0, st],
where Et is the event of reasoning error at hop t. This is the transformer-specific
reasoning-errors analogue of the standard Markov assumption underlying the CoT
error-propagation analysis of Section 2.4 (Definition 2.27). The assumption is plausible
under the Markov structure of autoregressive decoding but not argued rigorously under
the dependence patterns introduced by shared prompt structure, training-distribution
overlap, or retrieval-content recurrence across hops. The crossover-depth prediction
nc ≈6.3 of Theorem 6.3 is therefore scoped as approximate under Assumption 6.2; the
analogous obstruction for the Adaptation × Grounding composition is enumerated as
Obstruction 3 in §6.3, and relaxing the assumption is listed among the ten open problems
in §6.6.4.
Before the formal theorem.
Think of a grounded reasoning chain of n hops as a two-
channel process. The reasoning channel of Chapter 2 accumulates CoT errors at rate ε per
hop, compounding to (1 −ε)n. The grounding channel of Chapter 4 receives retrieved
evidence of quality q per hop, but only the η-fraction that fits through the residual-stream
bottleneck (Definition 6.1) is actually usable, so effective per-hop evidence quality is
q1−η, compounding to qn(1−η). Under Assumption 6.2, the two channels’ error events
are independent and joint reliability is the product of the two terms. The crossover depth
nc is where the marginal benefit of improving reasoning equals the marginal benefit of
improving grounding; at n > nc reasoning dominates, at n < nc grounding dominates.
This is the quantitative content of the opening vignette’s observation that retrieval gains
2 percentage points while CoT gains 15 at deep reasoning depth.
Theorem 6.3 (Computation-Grounding Composition). Let n be reasoning depth, ε the

manipulation parameter of Chapter 5 is denoted εmech where it reappears), q the retrieval quality
of Chapter 4 (taken as top-k recall against the gold-passage set used in Section 6.4.2), and
η the information retention factor (Definition 6.1; a capacity-to-entropy ratio, not an SDPI
contraction constant). Under the Markov assumption of Definition 2.27 and the capacity-
bottleneck contraction model of Definition 6.1:
(i) Joint reliability bound:
geff
1→2(n, ε, q) ≤(1 −ε)n · qn(1−η).
(ii) Ceiling effect: The marginal benefit of improving q attenuates by a factor in [7, 30]×
at depth d∗, explaining the retrieval-reasoning asymmetry highlighted in Section 1.1

Proof sketch. (i) Model the grounded reasoning chain as a Markov chain: at each hop,
reasoning succeeds with probability 1 −ε independent of evidence, and evidence infor-
mation is retained at per-hop rate η (the capacity-to-entropy ratio of Definition 6.1); the
exponent n(1 −η) captures the information leak rate, the fraction of per-hop retrieval con-
tent the computational bottleneck cannot retain. Compounding this capacity-bottleneck
contraction over n hops yields effective evidence quality qn(1−η); joint reliability is the
product of this evidence-quality contribution and the reasoning-survival factor (1 −ε)n.
The bound does not require SDPI structure on the retrieval kernel; it requires only

Markov chain structure of Definition 2.27) and (b) the information-retention definition
of Definition 6.1.

6.2.3
Why This Composition Matters
The welfare composition of Theorem 5.18 and the computation-grounding composition above share
a common structural template (monotone bounding function, computable crossover parameter),
supporting but not proving the conjecture of full four-way composition.
The welfare composition theorem (Theorem 5.18) is about multi-agent deploy-
ments; the computation-grounding composition is about single-agent reasoning chains.
Together they establish two data points of the same compositional structure:
• Each composition takes two impossibility specifications, individually necessary.
• Each yields a bounding function that is monotone in both arguments.
• Each exhibits a computable crossover parameter (the verification-budget ratio α∗for
Mechanism × Verification; the crossover depth nc for Computation × Grounding)
at which the marginal benefit of investment in each component equalises. The
Mechanism × Verification composition yields a strictly stronger bound than the
sum of its components because ROM independence (Proposition 5.21) suppresses
the verification-forgery term exponentially in the security parameter κ; the Compu-
tation × Grounding composition yields the product form (1 −ε)n · qn(1−η), whose
contribution is the functional interpolation via η and the crossover-depth analysis
rather than strict sub-multiplicativity.
The pattern is suggestive but not conclusive: proving full four-domain composition
(computation × grounding × trust × ...) remains the central open problem (§6.6).
A self-referential caveat.
The Computation × Grounding bound above assumes per-
step error independence across the reasoning chain. The same error-event-independence
concern identified as Obstruction 3 of the Adaptation × Grounding report (§6.3) applies
here too: reasoning errors at successive steps may share a common cause (prompt
structure, training-distribution overlap) and be positively correlated. We scope the
crossover-depth prediction nc ≈6.3 as approximate under the independence assumption; a
complete analysis under dependence remains open and is listed among the ten open
problems (Section 6.6). Naming this obstruction here makes the thesis’s methodological
stance on error-dependence consistent across compositions.
6.3
Adaptation × Grounding: An Honest-Obstruction Re-
port
The preceding section proved one cross-pillar composition (Computation × Grounding).
Chapter 5 proved another (Mechanism Design × Verification). A third candidate compo-
sition, Adaptation × Grounding, is conspicuously absent, and this section explains why.
The omission is deliberate: the composition is what the thesis’s methodology would


## Page 193


Chapter 6. Synthesis
170
most naturally predict to exist, but current technical tools do not yield a composition
theorem of the same form as the two we have proved. Rather than fabricate one, we
articulate the obstruction precisely. This is an honest-obstruction report in the spirit
of the methodology: an impossibility specification of a different kind, a methodological
impossibility naming what the current state of the art cannot currently deliver.
6.3.1
The target composition
An Adaptation × Grounding composition would couple an adaptation guarantee from Chapter 3
with a grounding guarantee from Chapter 4 into a joint reliability bound of the same form as
Theorem 6.3 or Theorem 5.18.
The target is a theorem of the form: if an adaptation method (LoRA, DPO, evo-
lutionary alignment) satisfies its specification (r ≤32 for LoRA PAC-Bayes; γ ≤∆/n
for DPO; or the EvoPref robustness condition) and a grounding pipeline satisfies its
specification (≥k orthogonal metrics for a k-stage pipeline; causal attribution at preci-
sion pattr), then the composed adapted-plus-grounded system achieves a joint reliability
guarantee expressible in the same form as Theorem 6.3 or Theorem 5.18. Call such a
theorem an A-G composition.
For the two provable compositions, the combined guarantees take the form of a
product (Computation × Grounding: (1 −ε)n · qn(1−η)) or a monotone sum (Mechanism
× Verification: ε + e−κ). The A-G composition would require a similar closed-form
coupling between an adaptation-guarantee (a property of the parametric model after
training) and a grounding-guarantee (a property of the retrieval-and-attribution pipeline
at inference). Three factors make this coupling technically recalcitrant.
6.3.2
Three obstructions
Three obstructions block a closed-form A-G composition: Chapter 3’s sample-conditional guar-
antees do not compose pointwise with Chapter 4’s input-conditional ones, adaptation shifts the
retrieval distribution, and the two error events are not obviously independent.
Obstruction 1: Adaptation guarantees are sample-conditional; grounding
guarantees are input-conditional.
LoRA’s PAC-Bayes bound (Theorem 3.1) is a
statement about generalisation over the training distribution: with high probability
over the training sample, the adapted model’s test loss is within a specified gap of its
empirical loss. Grounding guarantees such as the Construct Conflation Impossibility
(Theorem 4.2) are properties of the evaluation metric itself, evaluable per-input once
the metric is fixed: for any query, a k-stage pipeline cannot be diagnosed to sub-stage
resolution by fewer than k metrics (and k metrics suffice under the generic-rank condi-
tion). The two guarantees live at incommensurable levels of quantification, one over
training sets, one over test inputs, and the composed statement would have to specify


## Page 194


Chapter 6. Synthesis
171
how to combine them. The natural candidate (a uniform-in-input version of the PAC-
Bayes bound, combined pointwise with the grounding bound) is false without further
assumptions: PAC-Bayes bounds are not uniform-in-input. Obtaining uniform bounds
requires additional machinery (e.g., margin-based PAC bounds) which are known to be
strictly weaker than the distributional PAC-Bayes bounds the thesis uses.
Obstruction 2: Adaptation interferes with the retrieval distribution.
LoRA-
adapted models, DPO-aligned models, and evolutionary-alignment populations change
the model’s likelihoods over candidate tokens, and therefore change the retrieval dis-
tribution in any retrieval-augmented pipeline that uses the adapted model to compute
embeddings or to rerank retrieved passages. The retrieval guarantee of Chapter 4 as-
sumes a fixed embedding model; a change in that model changes which passages are
retrieved for any given query. An A-G composition theorem would have to quantify
the distributional shift induced by adaptation and bound its effect on retrieval quality.
The most direct tool (total-variation bounds on the retrieval distribution pre- and post-
adaptation) does not obviously scale: LoRA adapters can be rank-32 yet induce arbitrary
changes in the top-k retrieval set if the retrieval bottleneck is dimensionally-sensitive.
Quantifying the retrieval shift as a function of the adaptation rank is an open problem
we have not solved.
Obstruction 3: Adaptation and grounding error events are not obviously in-
dependent.
The Computation × Grounding composition (Theorem 6.3) assumes
per-step error events are independent across the reasoning chain. The Mechanism ×
Verification composition (Theorem 5.18) derives independence of mechanism and ver-
ification errors in the random oracle model (Proposition 5.21). An A-G composition
would require independence of adaptation-induced error events (e.g., LoRA general-
isation failures) from grounding-induced error events (e.g., retrieval misses). These
events share a common cause, the training-data distribution, and are plausibly posi-
tively correlated: training samples that stress the adapter’s generalisation ability are the
same samples whose passages the retrieval pipeline was calibrated on. Demonstrating
near-independence would require either an explicit decoupling mechanism (which no
current adaptation or grounding technique provides) or a conditional-independence
proof under deployment-realistic distributions (which is open).
6.3.3
What partial progress is available
Three partial results from Chapters 3 and 4 contribute toward an A-G composition without
closing it: individual guarantees combined via union bound, empirical validation from the
compliance walkthrough, and a conditional theorem under adaptation-grounding independence.
Three partial results exist in the thesis that contribute toward the A-G composition
but do not aggregate to a full theorem.


## Page 195


Chapter 6. Synthesis
172
(i) Separate guarantees. The LoRA PAC-Bayes bound (Theorem 3.1) and the construct-
conflation impossibility (Theorem 4.2) each bound their respective failure mode in isola-
tion. A practitioner can invoke both simultaneously: the adapted model’s generalisation
is bounded, AND the grounding pipeline’s metric structure is sufficient. The composed
operational risk is the sum of the two (union-bound composition), which is valid but
asymptotically weaker than what a proper multiplicative or coupled composition would
yield.
(ii) Empirical validation. The compliance-assistant walkthrough (Section 6.4.2) ex-
hibits a five-layer deployment (base LLM + fine-tuning + RAG + multi-agent audit +
selective verification) whose accuracy is empirically consistent with the separate guar-
antees’ union bound. The 12–25 percentage-point ablation costs observed are additive
at the margins, not multiplicative, consistent with Obstruction 3’s expectation that adap-
tation and grounding errors are not independent at realistic deployment scales, but
sub-additive enough that the union bound is not catastrophically loose.
(iii) A conditional theorem. Under an additional assumption of conditional inde-
pendence between adaptation and grounding errors given the training distribution
(formally: Pr[Eadapt ∩Eground | Dtrain] = Pr[Eadapt | Dtrain] · Pr[Eground | Dtrain]), a
multiplicative composition of the form (1 −εadapt) · (1 −εground) follows from the two
separate guarantees. This conditional theorem is stated here but deferred to future work;
the conditional independence assumption is plausible under i.i.d. deployment but not
argued rigorously.
6.3.4
Why this matters
The A-G obstruction itself satisfies the thesis’s impossibility-specification template of Defini-
tion 1.1, showing the methodology can be applied to its own current limits rather than papered
over.
The A-G obstruction is not a defect in the methodology. It is a legitimate research
gap the methodology usefully names. A less honest framing would claim a third
composition by citing the conditional theorem of (iii) without stating its conditionality,
or by treating the union-bound composition of (i) as if it were multiplicative. The
methodology’s commitment to falsifiable statements and quantified violation costs
makes such framings unavailable. The honest report is that of the six pairwise pillar-
compositions one is proved (Computation × Grounding) and a second is proved within
the Trust pillar, one (Adaptation × Grounding) has articulated obstructions, and the
remaining four pairwise cases (Computation × Adaptation, Computation × Trust,
Adaptation × Trust, Grounding × Trust) and the full four-way composition are open
problems characterised in Section 6.6 below.
This is also a specific demonstration that the impossibility-specification methodol-
ogy applies to its own limits: the obstruction above is formally of the same type as the
other impossibilities in the thesis (a computable gap, a quantified cost of crossing it, a


## Page 196


Chapter 6. Synthesis
