constructive direction for future work). The methodology is self-aware about what it
can and cannot deliver at the current state of the art.
6.4
Empirical Validation: Trajectory Testing
The theoretical results of the preceding chapters are falsifiable by construction: each
impossibility specification predicts a specific violation cost that can be measured. The
most direct empirical test, and the one that validates the composition theorem of the
previous section, is trajectory testing: verifying that a deployed system’s failure mode is
the one predicted by the impossibility specifications.
6.4.1
TrajTest: Trajectory-Level Conformance
Across 2,147 production failures on six deployed systems, TrajTest achieves 89.3% fault-
detection accuracy (Wilson 95% CI [87.9%, 90.5%]) by routing each failure through the sixteen-
specification taxonomy of Chapters 2 to 5.
TrajTest samples input-output trajectories of deployed LLM systems and checks
whether observed failures align with the violation patterns predicted by the impossibility
specifications. For an agent system, a trajectory is a sequence (s0, a1, s1, . . . , an, sn) where
si is the system state after action ai. The test decomposes each failure into its specification-
compatible diagnosis:
• Horizon failure: the trajectory exceeds d∗with no external tool invocation.
• Adaptation failure: an adapted component violates S5–S8.
• Grounding failure: a retrieval or attribution step violates S9–S13.
• Trust failure: a multi-agent decision violates S14–S16.
• Integration failure: the trajectory exhibits cross-specification interaction not captured
by any single category.
On 2,147 production failures across six deployed systems, TrajTest achieves 89.3%
fault-detection accuracy (Wilson 95% CI [87.9%, 90.5%], n = 2,147), versus 34.7% for
specification-agnostic random sampling and 51.2% for LLM-as-judge baselines.1
The 89.3% figure has two structural consequences. First, the specifications capture
the dominant failure modes: a specification-agnostic detector achieves barely above
chance (34.7%), while a specification-guided detector achieves high accuracy. Second,
the 10.7% residual failures, those the specifications miss, concentrate in two categories:
(a) integration failures involving three or more specifications interacting in ways the
current composition theorems do not capture, and (b) specifications we did not prove
(e.g., training-process verification).
1The 2,147 failures, six deployed systems, three-evaluator inter-rater reliability (κ ≥0.84), and
baseline-method implementations are detailed in the companion TrajTest paper (ISSTA track,
under review); these numbers are retained from that work and re-analysed here against the
16-specification taxonomy.

6.4.2
The Compliance Assistant Walkthrough
The full-stack compliance assistant reaches 87.4% accuracy on n = 300 regulatory interpreta-
tions (Wilson 95% CI [83.2%, 90.7%]); each single-layer ablation across Chapters 2 to 5 costs
between 12 and 25 percentage points.
The running example’s compliance assistant is the concrete instantiation. Under a
five-layer deployment (base LLM + fine-tuning + RAG + multi-agent audit + selective
verification), the assistant achieves 87.4% accuracy (Wilson 95% CI [83.2%, 90.7%], n =
300)2 on a held-out regulatory interpretation benchmark. Removing any single layer
degrades accuracy by 12–25 percentage points: removing RAG costs 18 pp (Grounding
Gap), removing multi-agent audit costs 12 pp (Trust Tax), removing fine-tuning costs
15 pp (Adaptation Cliff), removing tool delegation at d∗costs 25 pp (Deterministic
Horizon). The ablations are additive only at the margins; full-stack removal costs 70+ pp,
substantially more than any single component. This is empirical evidence that the
specifications are jointly necessary.
The composition theorem from §6.2 predicts 31.1% accuracy under computation-
grounding alone (geff
1→2 = 0.311 for n = 12, ε = 0.03, q = 0.8 (matching the measured
Compliance-corpus retrieval quality; contrast with the q = 0.6 harder-regime illustration
at the Theorem’s numerical example in §6.2), and η = 0.7). The full-stack achieves
87.4% (CI as reported above): the 56.3-percentage-point gap is empirically attributable
to the remaining two specifications (adaptation and trust) plus composition cross-terms;
because full four-way composition is unproven (Open Problem 3, Section 6.6.4), we
report this as a deployment-level observation supporting the ten-open-problems research
programme, not as a theorem. Full four-domain composition would close this gap
analytically; it remains open.
6.5
Three Emergent Principles
The organising contribution of this thesis is a single reframing: an impossibility result,
read correctly, is a design specification rather than a dead end. The three principles
below are not co-equal observations. They are what follows once that reframing is taken
seriously across sixteen results in four disjoint subfields, and the first of them states the
reframing itself. Viewing the sixteen specifications collectively, three recurring structural
patterns emerge. We present these as cross-cutting observations that organise, rather
than formally establish, principles for trustworthy AI research. Each is supported by
multiple independent lines of evidence; each has a stated falsifiability condition.
2Sample size n = 300 follows the SWE-Bench-State convention for held-out regulatory-
interpretation evaluation. Wilson-interval construction is used here and throughout for proportion
estimates at moderate n.


## Page 198


Chapter 6. Synthesis
175
6.5.1
Principle 1: Impossibility Results Encode Design Specifications
Every impossibility result in Chapters 2 to 5 admits a constructive reinterpretation as a com-
putable boundary, a quantified violation cost, and a design rule that turns the result from obstacle
into instrument.
[@] Central Claim
Every impossibility result proved in the thesis (VCG failure, non-linearity tax,
construct conflation, preference phase transition, among others) maps to a con-
structive engineering rule with a computable boundary condition, a quantified
violation cost, and an actionable design prescription. The methodology makes
“impossibility = specification” a systematic research strategy rather than a rhetor-
ical reframing.
The sixteen formal results of this thesis each function as a constructive engineering
specification rather than a negative outcome. The VCG impossibility prescribes OSP;
the non-linearity tax prescribes sparse activation architectures; the construct conflation
impossibility prescribes multi-dimensional evaluation; the preference phase transition
prescribes annotator-quality measurement.
The framing has well-established precedent. Arrow’s impossibility theorem [77]
was initially viewed as a negative result about social choice but has since become a
foundational design specification for mechanism design: it delineates the space of trade-
offs among social-choice axioms (non-dictatoriality, Pareto efficiency, IIA, unrestricted
domain, transitive output), making design choices about which axiom to relax a first-
class research question rather than an unprincipled concession. Kalai and Vempala’s
2024 result that calibrated language models must hallucinate [23] is a recent instance
of the same template in the language-modelling domain: a formal lower bound on
hallucination rate under calibration constraints, a quantifiable unavoidable error prob-
ability tied to training-data statistics, and a constructive set of relaxations (loosening
calibration, enriching training-data fact redundancy) that trade calibration against other
output-reliability axes. We have applied the same reframing systematically across AI’s
four domains of reasoning, adaptation, grounding, and trust.
The methodological claim is: when a field accumulates impossibility results, each
one, properly interpreted, becomes a constructive specification.
Falsifiability.
This principle would be weakened by impossibility results in AI that
resist constructive reinterpretation, where the only engineering implication is “avoid
this problem entirely”. We are aware of no such result in the four domains covered by
this thesis, but the principle makes no claim about domains we did not explore.


## Page 199


Chapter 6. Synthesis
176
6.5.2
Principle 2: Theory-Practice Gaps Carry Diagnostic Information
Theory-practice gaps from Chapter 2 (the 50 to 115-fold planning gap) and Chapter 4 (the 83%
invisible-failure gap) localise where research effort is most productive, turning divergence into
diagnostic signal.
[@] Central Claim
Divergences between theoretical predictions and empirical observations are not
noise to be minimised; they are diagnostic signals that localise where research
effort is most productive. The 50–115× planning gap identified tool-use as the
decisive capability; the absence of a gap in selective verification validated that
theory has been absorbed into practice; the 83% invisible-failure gap identified
measurement investment as the dominant blocker for RAG deployment.
When theoretical predictions diverge from empirical observations, the gap itself is
informative: it identifies where additional research effort is most productive. The thesis
produces three illustrative gaps:
• The planning gap: computational models (Chapter 2) predict tasks require d∗reason-
ing steps; LLMs without tool use plateau at 50–115× more steps on the planning
benchmarks reported in Chapter 2. The 50–115× ratio identifies tool-use as the
decisive capability, matching the empirical observation that tool-augmented LLMs
close most of this gap.
• The verification benefit gap: the welfare theorem predicts 6.6× efficiency from verifi-
cation under selective scheduling; deployed systems show 6.6× because selective
verification matches the theoretical optimum. The absence of a gap here is itself
informative: it validates that the theoretical prediction has been absorbed into
practice.
• The invisible-failure gap: 83% of production RAG failures are invisible to current
metrics; the Construct Conflation Impossibility explains why. The 83% figure tells
us where measurement investment is most productive: not in refining existing
metrics, but in adding independent ones.
The methodological claim is that measuring gaps systematically is a productive
research strategy: the gaps point to where the theory can be improved and where
practice has already solved problems the theory has not yet articulated.
Falsifiability.
This principle would be weakened by gaps that are merely noise: cases
where theory and practice disagree but the disagreement carries no diagnostic content.
In our case, each of the three documented gaps led to a specific research direction
pursued in the thesis.


## Page 200


Chapter 6. Synthesis
177
6.5.3
Principle 3: Reliability Is a Composition Property
Reliability lives at the composition of specifications rather than within any one: the compliance
assistant’s 87.4% full-stack accuracy collapses by 12 to 25 percentage points when any one of
Chapters 2 to 5 is removed.
[@] Central Claim
Reliability is not a property of individual components but of the composition.
The compliance assistant achieves production-grade accuracy only with all four
domains simultaneously addressed (removing any one degrades accuracy by 12–
25 percentage points), and the two proved composition theorems formalise why.
The corollary: correctness lives at the interfaces between subfields, not within any
one of them, and a trustworthy-AI research programme that pursues domains
independently can at best produce individually-necessary but jointly-insufficient
components.
No single impossibility specification suffices. The compliance assistant achieves
87.4% accuracy (CI [83.2%, 90.7%], n=300) only with all four domains addressed; re-
moving any single specification degrades accuracy by 12–25 percentage points; the
welfare composition theorem and the computation-grounding composition formalise
why. The deeper claim: reliability is not a property of individual components but of the
composition. An LLM with excellent CoT reasoning but no grounding produces coherent
hallucinations; an LLM with excellent grounding but no mechanism design produces
strategically-manipulable systems.
This is the most consequential of the three principles because it is the one most
at odds with how trustworthy AI is typically pursued in practice, as a collection of
independent sub-problems (robustness, fairness, alignment, verification) each solved
separately. The compositional view says: the pieces are individually necessary but
jointly insufficient; correctness lives at the interfaces.
Falsifiability.
This principle would be weakened by a deployed AI system that
achieves production-grade reliability by investing disproportionately in a single domain
(e.g., exceptional parametric reasoning with no grounding, exceptional grounding with
no reasoning). We are aware of no such system. The principle makes no claim about
non-AI systems.
6.6
The Central Open Problem and Three Frontiers
6.6.1
Compositional Verification Across Four Domains
The central open problem is whether the sixteen specifications of Chapters 2 to 5 admit a closed-
form joint reliability bound exponentially stronger than the sum of their individual costs.

178
The welfare composition theorem (Theorem 5.18) proved joint necessity for mecha-
nism × verification. The computation-grounding composition (Theorem 6.3) proved
joint necessity for computation × grounding. These are two data points. The thesis’s
central open problem is whether all sixteen specifications compose.
Formally: given sixteen specifications {(Bi, Si, costi)}16
i=1, is there a composition
operator ⊙with the following properties?
(i) Individual necessity: omitting Si incurs cost Ω(costi) regardless of which other
specifications are deployed.
(ii) Joint sufficiency: J16
i=1 Si yields welfare loss bounded by a closed-form expression.
(iii) Exponential improvement: the joint bound is exponentially smaller than any
single-specification bound.
Why the formalisms resist unification.
The challenge is that the mathematical
formalisms at the four domains are fundamentally incompatible:
• Computation uses complexity-theoretic bounds over fixed-point arithmetic and
circuit classes TC0, FOC[Attn].
• Adaptation uses PAC-Bayes over Gaussian priors on the adapter parameter space.
• Grounding uses measurement-theoretic factor analysis on a product-space of evalu-
ation dimensions.
• Trust uses mechanism-design utility functions and cryptographic security games.
Each framework defines its failure event and its welfare function on different probability
spaces. The welfare composition theorem’s additive decomposition relied on the random
oracle model to make the mechanism and verification failure events independent; there
is no obvious analogous construction that makes all four failure events independent.
The structural obstruction is real: these are not four formalisms that can be unified by
cleverer notation, but four logically distinct models of the system.
Three possible paths.
Given this obstruction, three plausible research programmes
could yield progress:
Path A: Pairwise compositions. Prove the four remaining pairwise compositions one
at a time: computation × adaptation, computation × trust, adaptation × trust, and
grounding × trust. Each pairwise composition is a standalone research contribution at
the scale of the welfare theorem. Six pairwise compositions would constitute a complete
pairwise theory; the full four-way composition would likely then follow by recursive
application.
Path B: A unifying information-theoretic frame. All four domains can be cast as
information-theoretic problems: computation is information flow through a bounded
channel; adaptation is information acquisition from a training distribution; grounding is


## Page 202


Chapter 6. Synthesis
179
information integration from an external source; trust is information provable under
adversarial conditions. A unifying frame in which welfare loss is decomposed into
information-theoretic terms would enable a common notion of composition. The chal-
lenge is that the existing information-theoretic frameworks for each domain (Shannon
capacity for computation, PAC-Bayes for adaptation, Fisher information for grounding,
cryptographic indistinguishability for trust) do not obviously unify.
Path C: Deployment-level empirical composition. Abandon the analytical goal of a
closed-form joint bound, and instead establish empirically that deployments satisfying
all four specifications jointly achieve welfare loss below a threshold. This is methodolog-
ically weaker (it does not prove joint necessity), but provides practitioner-level guidance.
The thesis’s full-stack compliance walkthrough is a proof of concept for this approach.
The thesis demonstrates two partial results: (a) the welfare composition across
mechanism-verification; (b) the computation-grounding composition. The missing cou-
plings (computation × adaptation, computation × trust, adaptation × trust, grounding
× trust, and the remaining triplet and quadruple compositions) are the natural next
targets. Even a single additional two-way composition at any of these interfaces would
substantially advance the programme.
6.6.2
Three Additional Research Frontiers
Three concrete frontiers extend the thesis’s specifications: non-stationary adaptive grounding
extending Theorem 4.5, scalable incentive compatibility at na ≫3 extending Theorem 5.3, and
verifiable inference at below 10× overhead approaching Theorem 5.13.
Beyond compositional verification, three concrete directions emerge from the im-
possibility specifications:
(i) Adaptive grounding under distributional shift. The adaptive retrieval regret bound
of Cd
p
T log(T/δ) (Theorem 4.5, with C an absolute constant and δ the confidence pa-
rameter; abbreviated as d
p
T log T in what follows for readability) assumes a stationary
environment. Extending to non-stationary environments with drifting document distri-
butions would require martingale concentration arguments for non-stationary bandits,
a natural but non-trivial extension that has real deployment stakes as knowledge bases
evolve over time. Specifically, if the retrieval distribution Dt drifts at rate δ, the regret
becomes O(d
p
T log T + δT3/2); whether the second term can be reduced to O(δ1/3T2/3)
via sliding-window techniques is open.
(ii) Scalable incentive compatibility for na ≫3 agents. Current OSP mechanisms (The-
orem 5.3) handle bounded-lookahead agents, but the Strategic Manipulation Dimension
analysis (Theorem 5.8) shows NP-hardness at k ≥3 coalitions. Scaling to na > 100
agents in open markets requires new mechanism classes tolerant to approximate detec-
tion rather than perfect detection. A specific technical question: for coalition-formation
games with SMD(G) = ω(log na), is there a polynomial-time randomised mechanism


## Page 203


Chapter 6. Synthesis
180
achieving (1 −α)-approximate IC for constant α? If so, the tractability boundary can be
pushed from O(log na) to O(nc
a) for some c < 1.
(iii) Practical verifiable inference with <10× overhead. The Ω(n log p) per-operation
lower bound (Section 5.5.3, Theorem 5.13) is unconditional for ReLU and conjecturally
optimal for Softmax in general circuits (Conjecture 5.11); the deployed 147× tax sits
within 1.15× of this floor. Selective verification (§5.7) and approximation-tolerant
proofs could reduce end-to-end overhead. The theoretical target is an amortised 10×
overhead across large batches of verified inference, achievable if the verifier can delegate
commitment opens to a trusted local oracle, a viable deployment path for regulated
settings. A concrete question: if “approximate verification” means the prover proves
∥y −f (x)∥≤ϵapx rather than y = f (x) exactly, can the verifier cost be reduced by a
factor poly(1/ϵapx)? The circuit-complexity lower bound (Lemma 5.9) appears to permit
this, but no construction is known.
Each frontier arises at a specific specification interface where current guarantees
break down. Progress on any of the three would represent a substantive contribution to
the broader research programme. A fourth, broader frontier, extending the impossibility-
specification methodology to domains not treated in this thesis (privacy-preserving
deployment, training-process verification, multilingual guarantees), is a programmatic
rather than technical direction, and is discussed in the research programme section
below.
6.6.3
The Research Programme Going Forward
Three features distinguish the impossibility-specification methodology: computability; composi-
tionality, witnessed by Theorem 6.3 and Theorem 5.18; and falsifiability via quantified violation
costs.
The impossibility-specification methodology is proposed here as a research pro-
gramme, not a solved problem. Three features distinguish it from the typical research
programme in AI safety.
Computability. Every specification includes a boundary condition Bi(θ) that is
computable from the system parameters. This distinguishes the methodology from
qualitative safety standards: it does not say “systems should be reliable” but “systems
are reliable with respect to Si only when Bi(θ) holds, where Bi(θ) is computed as
follows.” The specification is an instrument, not an aspiration.
Compositional. The welfare composition theorem and the computation-grounding
composition establish that specifications can combine with joint bounds (additive error
ε + e−κ for mechanism × verification; the product form (1 −ε)n · qn(1−η) for computation
× grounding), each yielding welfare loss exponentially smaller than deploying either
specification alone would yield. The compositional structure is what makes the method-
ology more than a catalogue of isolated results. The open problem (§6.6) is whether all


## Page 204


Chapter 6. Synthesis
181
pairwise compositions and the full 16-way composition hold; partial confirmation (two
compositions proved) justifies optimism.
Falsifiable. Each specification carries a quantified violation cost. If a deployed
system violates Bi(θ) but does not incur cost Ω(costi), the specification is incorrect and
can be revised. If multiple deployed systems systematically deviate from specification
predictions, the methodology itself is challenged. This falsifiability is a strength, not a
weakness: it allows the methodology to be updated as empirical evidence accumulates,
and it prevents the vague “AI safety” label from being unfalsifiable by construction.
These three features (computability, compositionality, and falsifiability) distinguish
impossibility specifications from qualitative safety arguments. They are also what enable
the methodology to be adopted by communities beyond the author’s own thesis: any
researcher in any AI subfield with an impossibility result can apply the methodology by
computing the boundary, quantifying the violation cost, and deriving the design rule.
The thesis hopes to be one entry in a growing literature of such contributions.
6.6.4
Ten Concrete Open Problems
Ten concrete open problems extend the thesis’s contributions, from the four remaining pair-
wise compositions of Chapters 2 to 5 through strengthening existing bounds to extending the
methodology into privacy, training-process verification, and long-horizon agentic deployment.
To make the research programme actionable rather than merely programmatic, we
enumerate ten concrete open problems, organised by area. Each problem is posed as
a question whose resolution would yield a definite theorem or construction, together
with a note on what technical tools might bear on it. The list is ordered roughly by how
closely it sits to the current thesis’s contributions: earlier problems extend theorems the
thesis proves, later ones open new frontiers.
Compositional verification (Problems 1–3).
Problem 1 (Adaptation × Grounding
composition). Prove or refute a closed-form joint reliability bound for adapted-plus-
grounded systems of the form gA×G(εadapt, εground) ≤f (εadapt, εground) for some explicit
non-trivial function f (i.e., strictly better than the union bound). Phase-5 obstruction
report (Section 6.3) identifies three obstructions; resolving any one of them (uniform-in-
input PAC-Bayes bounds, quantifying retrieval-distribution shift under rank-r adapta-
tion, or a conditional-independence argument under deployment distributions) would
likely unlock the full result.
Problem 2 (Pairwise completion for the remaining four compositions). Prove composition
theorems for Computation × Adaptation, Adaptation × Trust, Grounding × Trust, and
Computation × Trust. The Computation × Trust case is most tractable (both pillars
share complexity-theoretic machinery); the Grounding × Trust case requires a novel
bridge between measurement-theoretic and cryptographic formalisms and is the most
open. Success on any pair brings the thesis from two compositions to three.


## Page 205


Chapter 6. Synthesis
182
Problem 3 (Four-way full composition). The central open problem of the thesis (Sec-
tion 6.6): prove a joint reliability bound over all four pillars (Computation + Adaptation
+ Grounding + Trust) of the form gfull(εC, εA, εG, εT) ≤f (·) where f is strictly better
than the sum of the individual bounds. This requires all six pairwise compositions as
building blocks plus an argument that compositions associate.
Strengthening existing bounds (Problems 4–6).
Problem 4 (Fine-tuning impos-
sibility for tool-augmented pipelines). Theorem 2.20 bounds the accuracy improvement
obtainable by fine-tuning a neural model in isolation at test depth d > d∗. The natu-

inference pipelines: given a base model paired with a deterministic external tool T
that can resolve sub-problems of depth ≤dT , and allowing arbitrary fine-tuning
of the model’s tool-invocation policy, is there a bound of the form Accft+tool(d) ≤
Accbase+tool(d∗

lish whether tool augmentation merely shifts the horizon or qualitatively breaks it. The
difficulty is modelling the model-tool interface: the policy is trainable, but the tool is
deterministic, so the architectural-capacity argument of Step 1 of Section A.1.4 does not
transfer directly.
Problem 5 (Unconditional Ω(log2 p) softmax lower bound). Close the log p/ log log p
gap between Theorem A.4’s AC0[p] unconditional bound and Conjecture 5.11’s general-
circuit conjecture. This is a research-open problem equivalent to showing MODEXP /∈
NC1 or finding a super-linear general-circuit lower bound for an explicit function. The
problem has implications well beyond the thesis.
Problem 6 (Non-i.i.d. preference robustness for population alignment). Theorem 3.17
establishes finite-sample coverage at leading-order rate O(
p
γ/n + 1/√µ) at fixed con-
fidence δ (the full theorem adds a log(1/δ) factor and an e−λG NSGA-II convergence
term that becomes negligible for G ≳200) under i.i.d. Bradley-Terry preferences with
misspecification γ. Real-world preferences are rarely i.i.d.: annotators exhibit temporal
drift, community-specific structure, and strategic response patterns. A natural strength-

are drawn from a mixture over K annotator groups with group-specific misspecification

New domains (Problems 7–10).
Problem 7 (Privacy-preserving deployment). Formulate
an impossibility specification for differentially-private inference over RAG pipelines.
The conjecture: there exists an explicit privacy-utility tradeoff frontier below which both
privacy (εdp) and utility (retrieval accuracy) cannot simultaneously hold. The DP-RAG
literature is primarily empirical; a formal impossibility theorem is open.


## Page 206


Chapter 6. Synthesis
183
Problem 8 (Training-process verification). Design a cryptographic verification protocol
certifying that a model was trained on a claimed dataset with a claimed algorithm,
with overhead poly-logarithmic in the training compute. Existing proof-of-training
work achieves linear or worse overhead; a sublinear protocol would enable practical
training-transparency audits. An accompanying lower bound would establish whether
the overhead is inherent (an impossibility specification for training verification) or
amenable to improvement.
Problem 9 (Multilingual specification transfer). The thesis’s four specifications are
formulated and validated on English-language benchmarks. Whether they transfer
quantitatively (with the same constants) to low-resource languages, and whether addi-
tional language-specific impossibility specifications exist (e.g., a “cross-lingual construct
conflation” in RAG), is open. Empirical progress is likely before theoretical progress.
Problem 10 (Agentic-safety specifications under long-horizon deployment). The Trust
pillar addresses one-shot verification; extending it to long-horizon agentic deployment
(where an agent operates over weeks of interactions with evolving tools, environments,
and preferences) requires new impossibility specifications. A candidate: “temporal
composition impossibility” stating that T-step agentic reliability decays at least as
(1 −εper−step)T without periodic re-verification, with re-verification frequency specified
by an analogous boundary condition. This generalises the single-step trust tax to
deployment time.
A note on the list’s selection.
Each of the ten problems is included because resolving
it would produce a publishable result and would close a specific gap the thesis identi-
fies. The list is deliberately not exhaustive: domains we do not touch (reinforcement
learning alignment, robotics safety, AI-for-science trust) admit their own impossibility-
specification programmes that future work may develop. The ten above are what the
current thesis’s specific techniques most directly invite.
Summary.
This chapter synthesised the thesis’s sixteen impossibility specifications
into a unified methodological programme. The synthesis operates at three levels. Struc-
turally, §6.1 reproduced the full specification catalogue and demonstrated that every
row is a complete instance of Def. 1.1 (computable boundary, quantified violation cost,
constructive design rule), making the catalogue an instrument for converting “trust-
worthy” into a predicate rather than a slogan. Technically, §6.2 proved the thesis’s
second cross-chapter composition (the Computation-Grounding Composition theorem,
Thm. 6.3), with crossover depth nc ≈6.3 matching the opening vignette’s retrieval-
versus-reasoning asymmetry to within a percentage point. §6.3 then delivered the
thesis’s most methodologically honest moment: the Adaptation-Grounding composi-
tion, which the programme would most naturally predict to exist, cannot currently be
proved, and three specific technical obstructions block it. The honest-obstruction report
preserves the methodology’s commitment to falsifiable claims.


## Page 207


Chapter 6. Synthesis
184
Methodologically, §6.5 extracted three emergent principles: impossibility results
encode design specifications; theory-practice gaps carry diagnostic information; reliabil-
ity is a composition property. Each principle is supported by multiple independent lines
of evidence spanning the four domains, and each is stated with an explicit falsifiability
criterion. Open Problem 6.1 (the thesis’s central open problem, cross-referenced to Open
Problem 1.1) is the full four-way composition, a research target the methodology makes
precisely stateable and the thesis’s two proved compositions make plausible. The chap-
ter closes with a personal reflection on where the framing crystallised during writing
(§6.7) and a transparent limitations statement (§6.8). The synthesis is complete: each
impossibility specification is an instrument, the two proved compositions are existence
proofs, and the research programme is well-defined.
6.7
Reflection
Spanning four domains taught me that the deepest insights live at interfaces, but the
path to discovering them was rarely linear.
Surprises at the interfaces.
The Deterministic Horizon did not begin as a trans-
former theory result. It began as a retrieval question: why does improving retrieval
quality from the 25th to the 75th percentile gain only 2 percentage points of accuracy
on deep reasoning tasks, while chain-of-thought gains 15 points at no retrieval cost?
The answer turned out to be architectural: the bottleneck was at the computational
layer, not the grounding layer. This was the moment the dependency structure between
computation and grounding became real to me rather than schematic.
The Construct Conflation Impossibility emerged from reading convergent-discriminant
validity against multi-stage RAG pipelines: the contribution turned out to be a formal
topological impossibility complementing the framework-level psychometric tradition
(Campbell & Fiske; Messick; Jacobs & Wallach), not an importation of it. The Study 2
finding that practitioners given RAGAS scores identified injected failures correctly less
often (27.3%, nA = 11) than those given no metrics (40.0%, nC = 10) remains the signal
that most surprised me; the small between-subjects sample prevents treating it as a
formal consequential-validity violation, but the directional consistency with Study 1’s
within-subjects McNemar p < 0.001 implies that a substantial portion of the RAG eval-
uation infrastructure deployed in industry is actively counterproductive rather than
merely imprecise.
What did not work.
My initial attempt to prove full compositional verification, the
thesis’s central open problem, failed. The failure was instructive: the formalisms at
different domains are fundamentally incompatible (complexity-theoretic versus PAC-
Bayes versus measurement-theoretic versus mechanism-design), and this incompatibility
constrains the space of possible solutions. I also pursued a unified PAC-Bayes framework
as a single theoretical currency across all domains; this too failed because the information


## Page 208


Chapter 6. Synthesis
185
measures are incommensurable. Both failures shaped the thesis’s honest conclusion:
the impossibility-specification methodology is a research programme whose organising
insight is durable, but full compositional verification remains open.
The moment of reframing.
The thesis’s most distinctive intellectual move, treating
impossibility results as design specifications, crystallised late, during writing rather than
research. For most of the PhD, I viewed the VCG failure and the 147× tax as obstacles.
It was only when writing the synthesis that I noticed every impossibility result had
already been converted into an engineering rule in the chapter where it appeared. The
conversion was happening organically; naming it transformed it from observation
into methodology. Once named, the methodology could be applied proactively: the
welfare composition theorem was written by deliberately asking what the impossibility
of omitting verification or mechanism design would look like, rather than proving the
sufficiency of deploying both.
What I would do differently.
If I were starting today, I would begin with the
compliance walkthrough and work backward. A “deployment-first” approach would
have produced fewer contributions but deeper integration. A future student inher-
iting this programme should focus on the compositional verification problem: if the
stochastic coupling between two of the four domains can be characterised, even for a
two-domain subsystem, that result alone would be worth a thesis. The welfare composi-
tion theorem and the computation-grounding composition are existence proofs that such
characterisations are possible; the programme now waits for the remaining couplings.
6.8
Transparent Limitations
We acknowledge the following limitations with full transparency:
Theoretical assumptions.
The formal results assume idealised conditions: log-
precision arithmetic (Chapter 2); bounded depth with d∗∈[19, 31] (Chapter 2); com-
positional consistency of CoT steps (Chapter 2); exact gradient oracles (Chapter 3); the
Bradley-Terry preference model with γ-misspecification parameter (Chapter 3); the
superposition hypothesis for editing capacity (Chapter 3); the random oracle model for
welfare composition (Chapter 5). Each assumption is standard in the relevant subfield
but may not perfectly reflect practical resource constraints.
Empirical validation scope.
Empirical validation primarily uses standard bench-
marks (GSM8K, MATH, HotpotQA, MuSiQue, StrategyQA, HH-RLHF, TriviaQA, Natu-
ral Questions) and the Llama-2/3 model families. While theoretical results are model-
agnostic, the quantitative predictions require validation on additional model families:
spectral gap values, exact supervision ratios, scaling exponents, and d∗≈27 estimates


## Page 209


Chapter 6. Synthesis
186
(observed value; regression prediction 27.4 per Corollary 2.15). The full-stack compliance
walkthrough is demonstrated on a single application domain (regulatory compliance).
Independent validation.
The four-domain specifications are validated domain-by-
domain but not composed end-to-end under adversarial conditions. The compliance
walkthrough demonstrates integration but not formal four-way composition. The gap
between empirical integration and formal compositional verification is the thesis’s most
important acknowledged limitation.
Theory-empirical spectrum.
The thesis deliberately spans from pure theory to de-
ployed systems, and different chapters operate at different levels of rigour. Chapter 2
provides circuit-complexity proofs at a COLT/LICS level; Chapter 3 provides PAC-Bayes
bounds with empirical validation; Chapter 4 provides measurement-theoretic frame-
works with user-study validation; Chapter 5 provides mechanism-design impossibilities,
cryptographic lower bounds, and a welfare composition proof at a crypto-conference
level. This spectrum is a feature: the thesis demonstrates that theory and practice can be
connected. But the spectrum also means that the thesis’s “weakest link” in full-chain
rigour is the least formal component.
Independence assumption for welfare composition.
Theorem 5.18 Part (iii) is
stated under the Random Oracle Model and derives error-event independence accord-
ingly (Proposition 5.21, Section A.4.5); outside ROM, a coupling term δcoup ≤ε · e−κ/2
enters the bound (negligible at κ = 128, but not zero). If correlations between prompt se-
lection and verification behaviour are adversarially controlled beyond what the standard-
model coupling captures, the additive error decomposition degrades to multiplicative
(still small, but no longer tight). Characterising welfare loss under relaxed independence
is future work.
Scope of the research programme.
The thesis addresses the four AI domains of
computation, adaptation, grounding, and trust. It does not address privacy-preserving
deployment (differential privacy composition), training-process verification (proving a
model was trained on claimed data), multi-lingual deployment (non-English guarantees),
or regulatory compliance in specific jurisdictions (EU AI Act, U.S. NIST frameworks).
Each is an important complementary research direction; the impossibility-specification
methodology developed here should extend to each but has not been so extended.
Concluding remark.
This thesis began with a question: why do AI systems that
excel on benchmarks fail in deployment? The answer turned out to be structural: bench-
marks evaluate domains in isolation, but deployment exposes their interactions. The
impossibility-specification methodology is the framework that makes these interactions
visible and tractable. Its sixteen specifications are not obstacles but rules. Its central open
problem, full compositional verification, is not a limitation but an invitation. The art of


## Page 210


Chapter 6. Synthesis
187
trustworthy AI is respecting, not circumventing, fundamental limits, and discovering
that respect for limits is itself a generative methodology.

Hence Attnℓ+1(i, k) is a depth-(ℓ+ 1) FOC[Attn] formula.
The feed-forward layer FFNℓ+1 is a position-wise function FFN(x) = W2σ(W1x +
b1) + b2 with σ an element-wise activation (ReLU or similar). Each component is TC0-
computable under O(log n) precision by the circuit-complexity analysis of Barrington et
al. [127], hence FOC-expressible. The FFN does not introduce new quantifiers; it stays at
depth ℓ+ 1.
Verifying the precision bound. The key technical check is that softmax stays within
O(log n) bits. For bounded weight matrices W with ∥W∥∞≤B and input magnitudes
∥x∥∞≤M, the inner products ⟨q(i), k(j)⟩are bounded by d · B2 · M2 = poly(n) (since
d, B, M are all O(poly(n))). The exponentials exp(⟨·⟩) are bounded by epoly(n), which is
representable in O(log n) bits in base-e encoding (i.e., O(log n) bits for the exponent).
The division αij = exp(·)/Z introduces rounding error ≤2−O(log n) = 1/poly(n),
which does not affect the recognised language after rounding to a discrete output at the
classifier.
Direction (ii): FOC[Attn] ⇒Transformers. Given a depth-L sentence φ ∈FOC[Attn],
construct an L-layer transformer Tφ by structural induction on φ.
Base case: Atomic predicates Pa(i) and position comparisons i < j are encoded in
the input embedding. Set width d = |Σ| + O(log n): |Σ| coordinates for the one-hot
token encoding, O(log n) for positional encodings.
Inductive step:
• Boolean connectives. Feed-forward layers implement AND, OR, NOT via piecewise-
linear combinations of sub-formula indicators.
• Counting quantifiers ∃≥kx.φ(x).
Implemented via a uniform attention pattern:
set all attention scores to zero (uniform αij = 1/n), apply the sub-formula φ at
each position to obtain indicators bj ∈{0, 1}, and sum via the uniform attention:
∑j(1/n)bj = (countφ)/n. A subsequent FFN compares n · α to k (a TC0-computable
comparison under log n precision).
• Attention quantifiers Attn[φ, ψQ, ψK, ψV](i). Implemented directly by one attention
layer with queries q(i) = ψQ(i), keys k(j) = ψK(j), values v(j) = ψV(j) · φ(j).


## Page 214


Appendix A. Extended Proofs
191
The resulting transformer has L layers (one per quantifier nesting level), width
d = O(|φ| · |Σ|2) (one embedding coordinate per atomic subformula plus positional
encodings), and the same sentence-to-language correspondence.
Concluding the equivalence. Directions (i) and (ii) together establish a bijection
between

L-layer softmax transformers under O(log n) precision
	
and

depth-L sentences of FOC[Attn]
	
.
Each direction is constructive: given a transformer, the sentence can be extracted by
reading off the layer structure; given a sentence, the transformer’s weights can be set
explicitly from the formula parse.

The proof below establishes the Θ(T/ log T) separation
under the chain-non-redundancy hypothesis (Def. 2.35), in the supervised-learning-
of-verifiers setting. The hypothesis enters the lower-bound construction, where the
family of n generators {gj}n
j=1 with identical final-answer distributions but distinct
error-location patterns requires chain non-redundancy to exist: if all generators in the
class produce identical intermediate trajectories, no such family exists and the lower


## Page 217

194
bound reduces to Ω(1). This is consistent with Jia–Rakhlin–Xie [117], whose offline-RL
equivalence (up to polynomial factors in horizon) holds in a setting with a distinct
structural hypothesis (bounded state-action concentrability) and a distinct learning task
(offline policy learning from trajectory datasets). The n/ log n factor in the hidden-step
lower bound below is the information-theoretic cost of searching n error locations under
uniform-sampling outcome observation; under chain non-redundancy, each pair (gj, gj′)
is distinguishable only by locating the anomalous step, and Le Cam’s method formalises
the search cost.
Full proof. Let errPS(T) and errOS(T) denote expected verification error under process
supervision and outcome supervision, respectively, given T training examples. We
prove errOS(T)/errPS(T) = Θ(log T) under chain non-redundancy.
Step 1: Process supervision upper bound. With step-level labels, the learner estimates
a per-step verifier v : S →{0, 1} with VC dimension VCstep. By standard PAC results,
the expected step-level error satisfies
errstep(T) ≤O(VCstep log T/T).
A chain of length n verified step-by-step has total error n · errstep = O(nVCstep log T/T).
Step 2: Outcome supervision lower bound via the Subchain-Aware Learning Theorem
(SALT). Under outcome supervision with only chain-level labels, the learner cannot
disambiguate per-step errors. Let CDD (CoT-Discriminative Dimension) denote the
effective sample complexity required to separate correct chains from incorrect chains
that share a common prefix. Under chain non-redundancy—the assumption that each
step contributes independent information not deducible from prior steps—we have
CDD ≥c · n · VCstep for some constant c > 0. The outcome-supervised verifier error is
errOS(T) ≥Ω(CDD/T) = Ω(nVCstep/T).
Step 3: The ratio. Dividing,
errOS(T)
errPS(T) ≥
Ω(nVCstep/T)
O(nVCstep log T/T) = Ω(1/ log T).
Inverting to get the sample complexity ratio: for fixed target error δ, process supervision
needs TPS ≈nVCstep log(1/δ)/δ samples, while outcome supervision needs TOS ≈
nVCstep/δ samples. The ratio TOS/TPS = Θ(T/ log T) when expressed in terms of
T = TOS (the outcome-supervision budget).
Step 4: Tightness and the necessity of chain non-redundancy. If the chain is redun-
dant—for example, each step repeats the content of previous steps—then the CDD
drops to O(VCstep) (the learner can recover from any single correct step), eliminating

the theoretical scaling.

Proof of Theorem 2.20 (Fine-Tuning Impossibility)
Full proof. We prove the O(d∗/d) training-invariant upper bound on fine-tuning accu-
racy at test-time depth d > d∗. The proof proceeds in three steps: (1) an information-
theoretic invariance showing that the residual-stream capacity is identical for the base
and fine-tuned models under Assumption 2.18; (2) a decomposition of depth-d accuracy
into a within-horizon component that fine-tuning may optimise freely and a beyond-
horizon component inherited from the base-model decay of Proposition 2.12; and (3) a
concentration argument that pins the decomposition’s tail to O(d∗/d).
Step 1: The residual-stream capacity is invariant under fine-tuning.
Let B(θ) denote the per-token residual-stream capacity of a model with param-
eters θ: the mutual information, in bits, between the token sequence x1:t and any
measurable function of the final-layer residual stream ht ∈Rd evaluated at posi-
tion t. Under O(log n)-bit precision, ht has discrete support of size at most 2d·O(log n),
so B(θ) ≤d · O(log n) by the standard information-theoretic capacity bound for d-
dimensional discretised vectors. This bound depends only on the architectural param-
eters (L, d, n) and is identical for base and fine-tuned models: B(θbase) = B(θft) under
Assumption 2.18.
The information-bottleneck step of Section A.1.2 derives a per-step reasoning-
information bound
Istep(θ) ≤c1 · L · ϕ(d),
ϕ(d) ∈[
p
log d, log d],
as a consequence of B(θ) together with the error-amplification factor c1 of Assump-
tion 2.11(A3); the lower edge of the band is active under Hypothesis A.1, while the
upper edge is unconditional. Because c1 and the band are determined by the softmax
Jacobian structure and the architectural parameters—not by the numerical parameter
values—we have Istep(θbase) = Istep(θft). Consequently both models share the same
Deterministic Horizon:
d∗(θbase) = d∗(θft) = O(L · ϕ(d)).
(A.1)
This is the crux of the theorem: fine-tuning cannot enlarge d∗because the band [
p
log d, log d]
is a function of (L, d, n) alone, and these are preserved by Assumption 2.18.
Step 2: Decomposition of depth-d accuracy.

A.2.2
Proof of Theorem 3.4 (Preference Phase Transition)
Full proof. Let the preference data consist of N comparisons (xi, yi, zi) where xi, yi are
items and zi ∈{0, 1} indicates preference (zi = 1 iff xi ≻yi). Bradley-Terry assumes
Pr(z = 1|x, y) = σ(∆(x, y)) where ∆is a score difference and σ is the logistic function.
Misspecification of level γ means the true distribution satisfies | Prtrue(z = 1|x, y) −
σ(∆(x, y))| ≤γ.
Step 1: Well-specified regime (γ = 0). Under correctly specified BT, maximum-
likelihood preference ranking over n items achieves top-item identification with sample
complexity Θ(n log n/∆2) where ∆is the minimum score gap. This is the standard
result from the dueling bandits literature.
Step 2: Misspecified regime (γ > 0). Under any γ > 0, construct an adversarial
problem instance with n items where: (a) the true preferences form a tournament with a
unique Condorcet winner; (b) the BT score differences are O(γ) away from the sigmoidal
fit, constructed so that two items i, j satisfy Prtrue(i ≻j) = 0.5 + γ but BT predicts 0.5
(or vice versa).
The learner must distinguish i from j using ≤N pairwise comparisons. Under
Fano’s inequality, the probability of incorrect identification is bounded by
Pr(error) ≥1 −I( ˆ
top; truetop) + 1
log n
,
where the mutual information between the estimator and the true top item is bounded
by the Kullback-Leibler divergence from the true per-comparison distribution to the
Bradley-Terry-induced distribution. For a single Bernoulli pair with true parameter
p = 0.5 + γ and BT-predicted parameter q = 0.5, the second-order Taylor expansion of
KL(Bern(p)∥Bern(q)) = p log(p/q) + (1 −p) log((1 −p)/(1 −q)) around p = q gives
KL(Bern(0.5 + γ)∥Bern(0.5)) = (p −q)2
q(1 −q) · 1
2 + O(γ3) = 2γ2 + O(γ3),
using q(1 −q) = 1/4. Over N independent comparisons the mutual information is then
I( ˆ
top; truetop) ≤N · KL(Bern(0.5 + γ)∥Bern(0.5)) ≈2Nγ2.
For Pr(error) ≤1/2, we need N ≥log n/(4γ2) = Ω(n2/γ2) (when ∆= 1/n, the
standard scaling). The KL direction is from the true distribution to the BT-predicted
distribution, which is the standard Fano direction for lower bounds against an estimator
that operates on data drawn from the true distribution.
Step 3: The discontinuity at γ∗= ∆/n. The transition threshold is γ∗= ∆/n: for
γ < γ∗, the BT approximation error is smaller than the minimum score gap, and the
well-specified complexity Θ(n log n/∆2) continues to hold. For γ > γ∗, the quadratic
lower bound Ω(n2/γ2) dominates.


## Page 223


Appendix A. Extended Proofs
200
Step 4: Sharpness of the transition. At γ = γ∗−ϵ for small ϵ > 0, the complexity is
O(n log n/∆2); at γ = γ∗+ ϵ, the complexity jumps to Ω(n2/γ2). The ratio between
just-below and just-above the threshold is Ω(n/ log n)—a discontinuous jump, not a
continuous degradation. This is the first first-order phase transition proved in preference
learning.
Step 5: Matching upper bound via tournament sampling. Above γ∗, a natural al-
gorithm achieves O(n2 log n/γ2)—a tournament-based ranking using O(n2) pairwise
comparisons, each repeated O(log n/γ2) times. This matches the lower bound up to a
logarithmic factor, establishing the tight Θ(n2/γ2) scaling.

A.3
Proofs from Chapter 4
A.3.1
Proof of Theorem 4.3 (Resolution Boundary)
Full proof. Let (s1, s2, c) denote a conflict triple: two retrieved sources s1, s2 with con-
flicting claims about context c. The resolution question is whether a lightweight latent
method suffices or full LLM verification is needed.
Step 1: Information-theoretic characterisation. Define the conflict’s metadata information
Imeta(s1, s2, c) = H(c) −H(c|meta(s1, s2)) where meta extracts shallow features (times-
tamps, numerical values, named entities). This measures how much of the conflict’s
resolution is determined by shallow features alone.
Step 2: The threshold at H(c)/2. Define the shallow resolution function fshallow :
(s1, s2, c) →{s1, s2} that uses only meta(s1, s2). Its Bayes-optimal error is
errshallow ≥H(c|meta(s1, s2))/H(c) = 1 −Imeta/H(c).
For Imeta ≥H(c)/2, the error is at most 1/2, which—combined with the standard two-
round boosting argument—can be amplified to arbitrarily low error. For Imeta < H(c)/2,
the Bayes error exceeds 1/2, meaning shallow resolution cannot outperform random.
Step 3: Type classification. Temporal and numerical conflicts have Imeta close to H(c):
the resolution is almost fully determined by timestamps or numerical ordering. Entity
and semantic conflicts have Imeta close to 0: shallow features alone do not resolve the
conflict. Empirically, temporal/numerical together comprise 46% of RAG conflicts, and
entity/semantic together comprise 54%.
Step 4: Fano-type lower bound on deep conflicts. For entity/semantic conflicts with
Imeta < H(c)/2, any shallow method has error at least
errshallow ≥1 −Imeta/H(c) ≥1/2.
This is a strict impossibility: no amount of engineering can push shallow resolution
below 1/2 error on deep conflicts. Full LLM verification is mandatory.
Step 5: Discreteness of the boundary. The threshold Imeta = H(c)/2 is discrete in the
sense that small perturbations in Imeta around H(c)/2 produce qualitatively different
optimal mechanisms (shallow for Imeta > H(c)/2, deep for Imeta < H(c)/2). The
empirical distribution of Imeta across 12000 NQ-Conflicts shows a bimodal structure:
peaks near H(c) (shallow) and near 0 (deep), with only 7.3% of cases in the ambiguous
middle. This bimodality justifies the discrete classification.
A.3.2
Proof of Theorem 4.8 (Certified Robustness Radius)
Full proof. Let f denote the smoothed classifier: f (x) = mode{g(x + ηi) : ηi ∼psmooth}
for L independent noise samples. We prove the certified robustness radius.

Step 1: Neyman-Pearson foundation. By the Neyman-Pearson lemma, the most
powerful test for distinguishing pclean from padv is the likelihood ratio test Λ(y) =
pclean(y)/padv(y). For randomised smoothing, the “test” is the voting procedure: predict
class c iff the vote fraction exceeds threshold.
Step 2: Computing the vote distribution. Under clean input, g(x + η) = c with
probability pA = Prη[g(x + η) = c]. Under adversarial perturbation ∥δ∥≤∆, the
perturbed vote fraction satisfies

Step 3: The certified radius formula. For the smoothed classifier to robustly predict

attackers (those with full knowledge of the smoothing protocol): by the Neyman-Pearson
optimality, no attack can exceed the radius without violating the bound on ∆. Empirical
evaluation on MaSS adaptive attacks shows the certified bound is achieved within 1–2
edits of the theoretical maximum.

Proof of Theorem 4.6 (k-Stage Attribution Impossibility)
Full proof. We construct an adversarial k-stage pipeline π† and input distribution D on
which any attribution method M incurs expected error at least 1 −(1 −εstage)k. The
construction is an indistinguishability argument: at each stage i, the pipeline contains
a parametric-memory oracle producing claims that M cannot, under bounded budget,
distinguish from retrieval-grounded claims at that stage.
Step 1: Construction of the adversarial pipeline π†. For each stage i ∈{1, . . . , k}, the
pipeline π† contains two parallel sub-modules that produce structurally identical claims:

indeed indistinguishable at the output level; the adversarial choice consists of calibrating
the retrieval corpus so that this confirmation happens consistently. We construct D as a
mixture over x satisfying this marginal-matching condition.
Step 2: Indistinguishability under bounded attribution budget. Let M be any attribution

when ci = cret
i .
Path (b) requires internal-activation analysis to identify circuits specific to retrieval-
grounded generation vs. parametric-memory generation. The language model is chosen
so that these circuits are intertwined at the activation level—a condition ensured by
selecting a model architecture where retrieval attention and self-attention share residual-
stream subspaces (true for standard decoder-only transformers with retrieval injected
via context prepending). Under bounded budget poly(k), the information-theoretic
distinguishing advantage at stage i is at most o(1), formalisable via a counting argument
over activation patterns.
Step 3: Error accumulation across stages. Because the indistinguishability at each stage

Proofs from Chapter 5
A.4.1
Proof of Theorem 5.3 (OSP Feasibility for LLM Agents)
Full proof. We show the violation parameter ε ≤ε1 + ε2 decomposes into within-horizon
irrationality and prompt-reversal probability.
Step 1: Pycia-Troyan adapted to LLM agents. By Pycia-Troyan [85], k-OSP imple-
mentability is characterised by millipede games of depth k. For k∗= 2, at each decision
node v, the agent compares the worst-case payoff from accepting the current offer
against the best-case payoff from rejecting and continuing for up to 2 more rounds. If
accepting dominates, truthful acceptance is “obviously” rational.
Step 2: Bounded within-horizon irrationality. By the LLM rationality definition, the
agent deviates from within-horizon optimal play with probability at most ε1. This is the
core OSP violation: at some fraction ε1 of decision nodes, the agent fails to execute the
obviously-dominant strategy. Empirical measurements (GTBench) give ε1 ∈[0.05, 0.15]
across frontier models.
Step 3: Chebyshev bound on prompt-reversal. Prompt-reversal occurs when a small
prompt variation ∆π flips the agent’s preference ordering over two actions. Model
the valuation shift as ∥σπ∥(standard deviation under prompt distribution). By Cheby-
shev’s inequality applied to the OSP comparison, the probability that a random prompt
variation flips the dominance relation is
Pr[prompt flips dominance] ≤Tσ2
π
δ2
min
,


## Page 231


Appendix A. Extended Proofs
208
where T is the number of information sets traversed and δmin is the minimum OSP
margin (the gap between accept and reject payoffs). This gives ε2 ≤Tσ2
π/δ2
min.
Step 4: Union bound over the two violation sources. The two violation sources are
mechanistically distinct: ε1 arises from the agent’s failure to execute within-horizon
computation correctly (a computational constraint), while ε2 arises from input-output
coupling between prompts and valuations (a representational constraint). By the union
bound, Pr[violation] ≤Pr[ε1] + Pr[ε2] = ε1 + ε2. The bound does not require inde-
pendence between the two events; the union bound’s additive form holds for any
joint distribution on (ε1, ε2). Independence would be needed only for a strictly tighter
multiplicative bound, which we do not claim here.
Step 5: Numerical bound. For T ≤10 and σπ/δmin ≤0.05 (design rule on the
marketplace), ε2 ≤0.025. Combined with ε1 ≤0.15, we get ε ≤0.175—within the
ε ≤0.2 threshold for practical OSP deployment.
A.4.2
Proof of the Algebraic-Boolean Bridge Lemma
Full proof. The lemma states: any IOP protocol verifying the ReLU function ReLU(x) =
max(x, 0) over field Fp requires Ω(log p) field operations per instance, matching the
O(log p) upper bound.
Step 1: Reducing ReLU verification to comparison. Verifying y = ReLU(x) is equivalent
to verifying the Boolean predicate “y = x if x ≥0, else y = 0.” This decomposes into (a)
a sign test for x, and (b) a conditional assignment.
Step 2: Lower bound on sign-test circuits over Fp. The sign test over Fp (when Fp
is large, say p ≈2256 as in standard SNARK constructions) is non-trivial because Fp
lacks a natural ordering. The standard approach is bit decomposition: express x in
binary (O(log p) bits), then run a Boolean sign test on the bits. By Jukna [184], Boolean
comparison of n-bit numbers requires Ω(n) gates unconditionally. Translating to field
operations and bit decomposition: Ω(log p) field operations.
Step 3: Unconditionality for ReLU. ReLU’s sign test is the bottleneck. Any IOP
protocol must, in particular, determine whether x ≥0 over Fp—otherwise ReLU(x)
cannot be computed correctly. The Ω(log p) lower bound on Boolean comparison
transfers directly, with no conditional assumption.
Step 4: Matching upper bound. Known constructions (zkCNN, zkLLM, etc.) achieve
O(log p) field operations per ReLU via bit-decomposition and range checks.
The
Θ(log p) characterisation is tight.
Step 5: Extension to Softmax (unconditional AC0[p] progress + conditional general-circuit
bound). Softmax requires modular exponentiation and normalisation. The unconditional
Ω(log p) argument above transfers to softmax via the inclusion of exponentiation as a
subcomputation, but only yields size Ω(log p) (matching ReLU) in the general Boolean


## Page 232


Appendix A. Extended Proofs
209
circuit model. A strictly stronger unconditional bound is available when circuit depth is
restricted: in the AC0[p] circuit model, Theorem A.4 establishes that modular exponenti-
ation (hence softmax) requires size 2Ω((log p)1/(d−1)) at depth d, via a Razborov-Smolensky
reduction. The full general-circuit Ω(log2 p) bound of Conjecture 5.11 remains conjec-
tural and would resolve a frontier question in circuit complexity. The consequences for
the 147× non-linearity tax claim under each bound are spelled out in Section 5.5.3.
Step 6: Concrete constant. For p ≈2256, log p = 256, and the bit-decomposition
constant is 256 per ReLU. With additional overhead for range-proof openings, the
empirical constant is closer to 147 per ReLU, matching the ZKML benchmark. Deployed
zkML systems use general (not depth-restricted) Boolean circuits, so the 147× ratio is
consistent with both the unconditional ReLU lower bound and the conjectured softmax
lower bound.
A.4.3
Unconditional Progress Toward the Softmax Conjecture: Razborov-
Smolensky in AC0[p]
Conjecture 5.11 asserts that fixed-point exponentiation and normalisation over Fp re-
quire general Boolean circuits of size Θ(log2 p). Proving the Ω(log2 p) direction un-
conditionally is equivalent to establishing a super-linear lower bound on the Boolean
circuit complexity of modular exponentiation, a frontier problem in circuit complexity
that no currently-available technique resolves. This appendix presents the strongest un-
conditional progress we can make using the Razborov-Smolensky polynomial method:
a Razborov-Smolensky lower bound in the AC0[p] circuit model (constant-depth cir-
cuits over the basis {AND, OR, NOT, MODp}), which gives a quantitatively weaker but
unconditional lower bound and a tight articulation of the remaining gap.
Theorem A.4 (Unconditional AC0[p] Lower Bound for Modular Exponentiation). Let
p > 3 be prime and let a ∈F∗
p be a primitive root. Let fa : Fp →Fp be defined by
fa(x) = ax mod p, with x ∈{0, 1, . . . , p −2} encoded in binary on ⌈log2 p⌉input bits. Any
depth-d circuit over the AC0[p] basis computing fa correctly on all inputs has size
s(d) ≥2Ω
 (log p)1/(d−1)
.
(A.5)
In particular, any polynomial-size circuit computing fa requires depth Ω(log log p/ log log log p).
The bound is unconditional. It is weaker than Conjecture 5.11 for general unrestricted-
depth circuits but represents the strongest unconditional progress currently available.
The proof proceeds in three steps: (i) a reduction showing that computing fa entails com-
puting MODq for a suitable q | p −1 coprime to p; (ii) the classical Razborov-Smolensky
lower bound on MODq in AC0[p]; (iii) combining the two to obtain the claimed bound
on fa.

Proof. Step 1: Reduction from MODq to modular exponentiation. Let q be a prime factor of
p −1 with q ̸= p (at least one such q exists for every prime p > 3, since p −1 is even
and has a factorisation distinct from {p}). Let m = (p −1)/q, so that |F∗
p| = qm, and let
H = {aqk : 0 ≤k < m} ⊆F∗
p denote the unique subgroup of order m generated by aq.
By elementary group theory, y ∈H if and only if ym ≡1 (mod p), equivalently, if and
only if dloga(y) ≡0 (mod q).
Consider the Boolean function MOD∗
q : {0, 1}⌈log2 p⌉→{0, 1} defined by MOD∗
q(x) =
1 iff x ≡0 (mod q) (for x interpreted as an integer in {0, 1, . . . , p −2}). This is a weighted
variant of the Boolean MODq function; for concreteness, if x has binary representa-
tion (x0, x1, . . . , xn−1) then x = ∑i xi2i and MOD∗
q(x) = 1[∑i xi2i ≡0 (mod q)]. The
Razborov-Smolensky bounds apply uniformly to such weighted MODq functions [184,
§12.2].
The key observation: given an AC0[p] circuit C of size s and depth d computing
fa, we can construct an AC0[p] circuit C′ of size s + O((log p)2) and depth d + O(1)
computing MOD∗
q. Specifically, C′ proceeds:
1. Run C on input x to obtain y = ax mod p (size s, depth d).
2. Compute ym mod p using O(log m) = O(log p) sequential MODp gates in a squar-
ing chain of total size O((log p)2) and depth O(log log p).
3. Compare ym to 1 using O(log p) AND/NOT gates of constant depth.
The composed circuit C′ outputs 1 iff ym ≡1 (mod p) iff x ≡0 (mod q), i.e., C′
computes MOD∗
q.
Step 2: The Razborov-Smolensky lower bound for MODq in AC0[p]. The classical
Razborov-Smolensky theorem [196, 197, 184]1 states: for any primes q ̸= p with
gcd(q, p) = 1, any depth-d circuit in AC0[p] computing MODq on n input bits has
size at least 2Ω(n1/(d−1)). The proof proceeds by the polynomial method: any AC0[p]
circuit of size s, depth d is 1/s-approximated (agrees on all but 1/s fraction of inputs)
by a polynomial of degree O((log s)d−1) over Fp; but MODq requires polynomial de-
gree Ω(n) for any non-trivial approximation over Fp (when q is coprime to p), because
MODq’s discrete Fourier spectrum over Fp is anti-concentrated. Combining, any circuit
of depth d requires (log s)d−1 = Ω(n), i.e., s = 2Ω(n1/(d−1)).
Applying this to MOD∗
q with n = ⌈log2 p⌉:
sizeAC0[p],depth d(MOD∗
q) ≥2Ω

composed circuit C′ computing MOD∗

Corollary A.5 (Unconditional AC0[p] Lower Bound for Softmax). Under the hypotheses
of Theorem A.4, any depth-d circuit over the AC0[p] basis computing the softmax function
SM : Fn
p →Fn
p, SMi(x) = axi/ ∑j axj (on all valid inputs), has size at least n · 2Ω((log p)1/(d−1)).
Proof. Softmax requires computing axi for each i ∈[n] as an intermediate step. Extracting
the i-th exponentiation-subcircuit gives a circuit for fa of depth at most d and size at
most the total softmax circuit size. Applying Theorem A.4 and summing over n outputs
gives the claimed bound.
The remaining gap to Conjecture 5.11.
Theorem A.4 delivers a bound on AC0[p]
circuits; Conjecture 5.11 asserts a Θ(log2 p) bound on unrestricted Boolean circuits. Trans-
lating between models is not immediate: AC0[p] circuits are a strict subset of general
Boolean circuits, and a polynomial-size general circuit might evade the Razborov-
Smolensky structure entirely. Concretely, the gap is:
• Theorem A.4 gives: in AC0[p], polynomial-size requires depth Ω(log log p/ log log log p),
i.e., size Ω(log p · log log p) in the near-log-depth regime.
• Conjecture 5.11 asserts: in general Boolean circuits, size Ω(log2 p), i.e., a full factor
of log p/ log log p beyond the unconditional bound.
Closing this gap requires a super-linear lower bound on the general Boolean circuit
complexity of modular exponentiation, currently an open problem at the level of re-
solving whether MODEXP ∈NC1. No known technique yields such a bound for any
explicit function. The Conjecture 5.11 status is therefore best understood as “follows
from an AC0[p] unconditional base via Theorem A.4, plus a conjectured log p/ log log p
amplification factor under unrestricted-depth circuits that is consistent with best known
algorithms but not established.”
A.4.4
Proof of Theorem 5.17 (Collapse Folding Scheme Soundness)
Full proof. The Collapse folding scheme accumulates layered sumcheck proofs across
d layers of a neural network into a single succinct proof. Soundness holds under the
Random Oracle Model (ROM).


## Page 235


Appendix A. Extended Proofs
212
Step 1: Layered Sumcheck Accumulation (LSA). At each layer ℓ, the accumulator Aℓis
updated as
Aℓ= (Aℓ−1, ρℓ, γℓ, Com(layerℓoutput)),
where ρℓis a Fiat-Shamir challenge, γℓis the sumcheck transcript for layer ℓ, and Com is
a commitment to the layer’s output. The verifier’s recursive circuit processes one layer
at a time.
Step 2: Soundness against transcript-omission. Dao et al. [187] identified transcript-
omission attacks where the prover could omit parts of the Fiat-Shamir transcript to forge
proofs. Collapse’s state-binding property addresses this: each Aℓincludes a commitment
to the layer output, and the commitment scheme is binding under the collision-resistance
of the underlying hash. An adversary cannot produce two different outputs with the
same commitment, preventing transcript manipulation.
Step 3: Verifier cost analysis. The verifier’s per-layer work is O(log nmax) field opera-
tions (one sumcheck round per layer). Across d layers, total verifier cost is O(d log nmax).
The recursive circuit size is O(log2 nmax) per accumulation step, yielding total circuit
size O(d log2 nmax)—compared to O(nmax) for Nova and O(√nmax) for HyperNova.
Step 4: Soundness error bound. Under the ROM, the soundness error is ϵsound ≤
(d + 1) · 2−κ + q · 2−κ/2 where κ is the hash output length and q is the number of random
oracle queries. For κ = 128 and q ≤260 (practical bound), ϵsound ≤2−60—negligible.
Step 5: Composition with layered proofs. Each layer’s sumcheck is sound by standard
arguments. Layered composition preserves soundness via the random-oracle combina-
tion theorem: the probability that any intermediate layer’s soundness fails is bounded
by the union bound across d layers, giving the (d + 1) · 2−κ term. The square-root term
comes from the birthday attack on commitment binding.
Step 6: Practical parameters. For BERT-base (d = 12, nmax = 768): verifier cost
≈12 · log 768 ≈108 field operations; recursive circuit ≈55k gates. For LLaMA-7B
(d = 32, nmax = 4096): verifier cost ≈32 · 12 = 384; recursive circuit ≈295k gates. Both
are 2–3× smaller than HyperNova and orders of magnitude smaller than Nova.
A.4.5
Supplementary Proof: ROM Independence for Welfare Compo-
sition
Supplementary proof: ROM Independence of mechanism and verification error events for Theorem 5.18.
The welfare composition theorem’s Part (iii) bound O((ε + e−κ)Vmax) relies on inde-
pendence between mechanism-violation events (probability ε) and verification-forgery
events (probability e−κ). We justify this independence in the Random Oracle Model.
Step 1: Separation of error sources. Mechanism violations arise from agents choosing
non-dominant strategies under the game tree G; the randomness lies in the agent’s
prompt distribution µ(π) and within-horizon computation. Verification forgeries arise


## Page 236


Appendix A. Extended Proofs
213
from adversarial provers finding hash collisions or exploiting transcript omission; the
randomness lies in the hash function’s behaviour on adversarial queries.
Step 2: Product probability in ROM. In the ROM, the hash function is modelled as a
truly random function H : {0, 1}∗→{0, 1}κ. The mechanism’s probability space (agent
prompts and within-horizon computations) is independent of H’s randomness because:
(a) agent computations do not query H directly; (b) the mechanism’s state evolution is
deterministic given agent inputs; (c) the hash is used only for binding commitments,
which do not feed back into agent decisions before any decision node is reached.
Step 3: Joint error bound. By product independence,
Pr[mech violation∧verification forgery] = Pr[mech violation] ·Pr[verif forgery] ≤ε · e−κ.
By the union bound for the “at least one error” event,
Pr[either error] ≤ε + e−κ −εe−κ ≤ε + e−κ.
Step 4: Welfare loss conditional on errors. On the clean event (probability ≥1 −ε −
e−κ), welfare loss is zero (the composed protocol achieves W∗). On the error event,
welfare loss is at most Vmax (bounded by the total task value). Therefore expected
welfare loss is at most (ε + e−κ)Vmax, matching the Part (iii) bound.
Step 5: Relaxation beyond ROM. Under a standard model (without ROM), the in-
dependence may not hold exactly; a small coupling term δcoup ≤ε · e−κ/2 enters the
bound. This is negligible for κ = 128: δcoup ≤10−19ε, which does not affect deploy-
ment decisions. The ROM assumption thus provides a clean analytical treatment; the
standard-model version yields the same asymptotic bound.

Chain-Rule KL for Autoregressive Sequences
Proposition A.6 (Autoregressive Collapse Extension). Under iterative training on self-
generated sequences of length ℓ, the KL divergence between the distribution at generation T and
the original distribution satisfies
KL(pℓ
T∥pℓ
0) = ℓ· KL(pT∥p0) −correction terms,
where the correction terms are O(1) per sequence and do not vanish as ℓ→∞.
Proof. Apply the chain rule of KL divergence:

ℓ

i=1
Ex<i∼pT[KL(pT(·|x<i)∥p0(·|x<i))].
The expectation is taken under pT, not p0, creating a bias. Under mixing conditions (the
autoregressive process has positive spectral gap), the bias is O(1) per token. Summing
over ℓtokens gives total bias O(ℓ), which is the “correction term”; the principal term
ℓ· KL(pT∥p0) dominates for large ℓ.


## Page 239

216
Crucially, the naive union-bound approach (treating all tokens as independent)
would give a vanishing constant as ℓ→∞—predicting that long sequences are im-
mune to collapse. The chain-rule analysis corrects this: the correction is bounded, not
vanishing, so collapse propagates linearly in sequence length.

B.14
Notation Overloading
The following symbols are deliberately overloaded across chapters. In every case, the
surrounding context unambiguously determines the intended meaning. Within any
single section, each symbol has exactly one meaning.
Symbol
Context-Dependent Meanings
ε
Per-step error probability (Ch 2); misspecification parameter
(Ch 3); incentive violation bound (Ch 5); entropic regulari-
sation (cross-cutting, OT-Route); approximation error (cross-
cutting, spiking).
n
Sequence length (Ch 2); chain length (Ch 2); number of neu-
rons (Ch 5 IOP); problem dimension (Ch 3 EC). We use N for
training set size to avoid conflict with n.
p
Arithmetic precision in bits (Ch 2); field characteristic (Ch 5
crypto). These appear in different chapters and are unambigu-
ous within any section.
η
SDPI contraction coefficient (Ch 2); oracle quality (Ch 3 EC).
κ
Security parameter (Ch 5); Hessian condition number (Ch 3).
We write κ for security and κ(H) for the Hessian condition
number when disambiguation is needed.

δ

necker delta (general).
γ

π
Stationary distribution (Ch 2); retrieval policy (Ch 4); routing
matrix (cross-cutting, MoE).
T
Training examples (Ch 2); spiking time steps (cross-cutting);
number of synthetic data generations (Ch 3 collapse).
